## XVI A Chance Meeting / 十六 不期而遇

===Original===
I roamed the place in search of the varlet for the space of half-an-hour, and, after having drawn all his familiar haunts, found him at length leaning over the seawall near the church, gazing thoughtfully into the waters below.

I confronted him.

“Well,” I said, “you’re a beauty, aren’t you?”

He eyed me owlishly. Even at this early hour, I was grieved to see, he showed signs of having looked on the bitter while it was brown. His eyes were filmy, and his manner aggressively solemn.

“Beauty?” he echoed.

“What have you got to say for yourself?”

===Chinese===
我在镇上转悠了半个钟头，四处搜寻那厮，把他惯常出没的巢穴逐一打捞了一遍，最后才在教堂附近的海堤边找到他——他正俯在堤上，若有所思地凝望下面的海水。

我迎面堵住了他。

“好啊，”我说，“你可真是个宝贝，不是吗？”

他猫头鹰似的打量我。我很沉痛地发现，才这么早的时辰，他已显出趁苦啤酒还棕黄的时候就对着它“看”过了的迹象。他两眼蒙着一层薄膜，神态庄严得咄咄逼人。

“宝贝？”他应声道。

“你还有什么好替自己辩解的？”

===Original===
“Say f’self.”

It was plain that he was engaged in pulling his faculties together by some laborious process known only to himself. At present my words conveyed no meaning to him. He was trying to identify me. He had seen me before somewhere, he was certain, but he could not say where, or who I was.

“I want to know,” I said, “what induced you to be such an abject idiot as to let our arrangement get known?”

I spoke quietly. I was not going to waste the choicer flowers of speech on a man who was incapable of understanding them. Later on, when he had awakened to a sense of his position, I would begin really to talk to him.

He continued to stare at me. Then a sudden flash of intelligence lit up his features.

“Mr. Garnick,” he said.

===Chinese===
“替俺…俺说说。”

很明显，他正用某种只有他自己才明白的费劲法子，把分散的心智一点点拢到一处。此刻，我的话对他不构成任何意义。他正在努力辨认我。他确信从前在哪儿见过我，但说不出是在哪儿，也想不起我是谁。

“我想知道，”我说，“是什么缘故，让你做了这么个不争气的傻瓜，竟把咱们的约定泄露出去了？”

我说得很平静。我可不想把语言中的精妙花朵，浪费在一个领会不了它们的人身上。等一会儿，等他意识到自己处境不妙，我再真正开始跟他算账。

他继续瞪着我。忽然，一道智力的闪光照亮了他的脸。

“加尼克先生。”他说。

===Original===
“You’ve got it at last.”

“From ch﻿—chicken farm,” he continued, with the triumphant air of a cross-examining King’s Counsel who has at last got on the track.

“Yes,” I said.

“Up top the hill,” he proceeded, clinchingly. He stretched out a huge hand.

“How you?” he inquired with a friendly grin.

“I want to know,” I said distinctly, “what you’ve got to say for yourself after letting our affair with the professor become public property?”

===Chinese===
“你总算想起来了。”

“从养…养鸡场来的。”他接着说，带着一位盘问证人的皇家大律师终于摸到线索时的得意神气。

“不错。”我说。

“在山…山顶上。”他再接再厉，一锤定音。他伸出一只蒲扇大手。

“你…你好？”他咧嘴一笑，友好地问道。

“我想知道，”我一字一顿地说，“咱们跟教授的那档子事如今闹得尽人皆知，对此你还有什么好替自己辩白的？”

===Original===
He paused awhile in thought.

“Dear sir,” he said at last, as if he were dictating a letter, “dear sir, I owe you﻿—ex﻿—exp﻿—”

He waved his hand, as who should say, “It’s a stiff job, but I’m going to do it.”

“Explashion,” he said.

“You do,” said I grimly. “I should like to hear it.”

“Dear sir, listen me.”

===Chinese===
他沉思了片刻。

“亲爱的先生，”他终于开口，仿佛在口授一封信，“亲爱的先生，俺欠您一个——解——解——”

他挥了挥手，那意思是说：“这活儿棘手，可我横竖要干完它。”

“——解…解释。”他说。

“你是欠我一个。”我冷冷地说。“我倒想听听。”

“亲爱的先生，您听俺说。”

===Original===
“Go on, then.”

“You came me. You said ‘Hawk, Hawk, ol’ fren’, listen me. You tip this ol’ bufflehead into watter,’ you said, ‘an’ gormed if I don’t give ’ee a poond note.’ That’s what you said me. Isn’t that what you said me?”

I did not deny it.

“ ‘Ve’ well,’ I said you. ‘Right,’ I said. I tipped the ol’ soul into watter, and I got the poond note.”

“Yes, you took care of that. All this is quite true, but it’s beside the point. We are not disputing about what happened. What I want to know﻿—for the third time﻿—is what made you let the cat out of the bag? Why couldn’t you keep quiet about it?”

He waved his hand.

===Chinese===
“那就说吧。”

“您来找俺。您说：‘霍克，霍克，老伙计，听俺说。你把这个老糊涂捅进水里去，’您说，‘俺就给你一张一镑的票子。’您就是这么跟俺说的。难道不是这么跟俺说的吗？”

我没有否认。

“‘好…好吧，’俺对您说。‘成，’俺说。俺把那位老先生捅进了水里，俺就拿到了那一镑票子。”

“是啊，这一桩你倒看得牢。这些全是实话，可尽是题外话。咱们争的不是发生过什么。我想知道——这已是第三回了——是什么让你走了嘴？你怎么就不能缄口不言？”

他挥了挥手。

===Original===
“Dear sir,” he replied, “this way. Listen me.”

It was a tragic story that he unfolded. My wrath ebbed as I listened. After all the fellow was not so greatly to blame. I felt that in his place I should have acted as he had done. It was Fate’s fault, and Fate’s alone.

It appeared that he had not come well out of the matter of the accident. I had not looked at it hitherto from his point of view. While the rescue had left me the popular hero, it had had quite the opposite result for him. He had upset his boat and would have drowned his passenger, said public opinion, if the young hero from London﻿—myself﻿—had not plunged in, and at the risk of his life brought the professor ashore. Consequently, he was despised by all as an inefficient boatman. He became a laughingstock. The local wags made laborious jests when he passed. They offered him fabulous sums to take their worst enemies out for a row with him. They wanted to know when he was going to school to learn his business. In fact, they behaved as wags do and always have done at all times all the world over.

Now, all this, it seemed, Mr. Hawk would have borne cheerfully and patiently for my sake, or, at any rate for the sake of the crisp pound note I had given him. But a fresh factor appeared in the problem, complicating it grievously. To wit, Miss Jane Muspratt.

“She said to me,” explained Mr. Hawk, with pathos, “ ‘Harry ’Awk,’ she said, ‘yeou’m a girt fule, an’ I don’t marry noone as is ain’t to be trusted in a boat by hisself, and what has jokes made about him by that Tom Leigh!’

“I punched Tom Leigh,” observed Mr. Hawk parenthetically. “ ‘So,’ she said me, ‘you can go away, an’ I don’t want to see yeou again!’ ”

===Chinese===
“亲爱的先生，”他答道，“是这么回事。您听俺说。”

他讲出的原是一出悲剧。我听着听着，怒气便消退了。说到底，这家伙也怪不得太深。我觉得，换了我处在他的境地，也会照他那么办。这是命运的错，且是命运一家的错。

看来，那场事故里他可没落着好。此前的我，还不曾从他的角度看过这件事。那场救援把我捧成了众口相传的英雄，落到他头上的效果却恰恰相反。照公众舆论的说法，他打翻了自己的船，险些淹死船上的乘客，若不是伦敦来的少年英雄——正是在下——纵身入水，冒着性命危险把教授救上岸去。于是，作为无能的船夫，他为众人所不齿。他成了笑柄。本地的促狭鬼们一见他走过，就费尽心机地编笑话。他们开出天价，雇他载他们最恨的仇人出海划船。他们还问他几时回炉再学学这门营生。总之，促狭鬼们怎么行事、历来怎么行事、普天之下怎么行事，他们便怎么行事。

话说回来，据我看，这些霍克先生本可以看在我的份上愉快而耐心地忍受下去——或者无论如何，看在我给他的那张脆生生一镑钞票的份上。可是一道新因素掺进了这道难题，把它搅得万分棘手。那就是简·马斯普拉特小姐。

“她跟俺说，”霍克先生凄然解释道，“‘哈利·霍克，’她说，‘你是个大笨蛋，俺才不嫁给自个儿一个人划船都叫人不放心的男人呢，何况还叫那个汤姆·利拿他编笑话！’”

“俺把汤姆·利揍了一顿。”霍克先生顺势插了一句。“‘所以，’她跟俺说，‘你可以走了，俺再也不想见你！’”

===Original===
This heartless conduct on the part of Miss Muspratt had had the natural result of making him confess in self-defence; and she had written to the professor the same night.

I forgave Mr. Hawk. I think he was hardly sober enough to understand, for he betrayed no emotion. “It is Fate, Hawk,” I said, “simply Fate. There is a Divinity that shapes our ends, rough-hew them how we will, and it’s no good grumbling.”

“Yiss,” said Mr. Hawk, after chewing this sentiment for a while in silence, “so she said me, ‘Hawk,’ she said﻿—like that﻿—‘you’re a girt fule﻿—’ ”

“That’s all right,” I replied. “I quite understand. As I say, it’s simply Fate. Goodbye.” And I left him.

As I was going back, I met the professor and Phyllis. They passed me without a look.

I wandered on in quite a fervour of self-pity. I was in one of those moods when life suddenly seems to become irksome, when the future stretches black and grey in front of one. I should have liked to have faded almost imperceptibly from the world, like Mr. Bardell, even if, as in his case, it had involved being knocked on the head with a pint pot in a public-house cellar.

===Chinese===
马斯普拉特小姐这片无情的行径，自然引得他为了自保而全盘招认；当天夜里，她就给教授写去了信。

我原谅了霍克先生。我想他还没清醒到能听懂的地步，因为他毫无情绪流露。“这是命运，霍克，”我说，“纯纯粹粹是命运。冥冥中自有神明塑造我们的结局，任凭我们怎样粗凿乱刻；抱怨也无济于事。”

“是…是啊，”霍克先生把这句格言默嚼了半晌后说，“她也这么跟俺说，‘霍克，’她说——就是那个调调——‘你是个大笨蛋——’”

“这就对了，”我答道，“我完全明白。正如我所说，纯粹是命运。再见。”说罢我便离开了他。

回去的路上，我遇见了教授和菲莉斯。他们打我身边走过，看都没看我一眼。

我继续踽踽独行，沉浸在自怜的热狂之中。人有时会陷入这样一种心绪：生活突然变得叫人厌倦，前途在眼前铺展开一片灰黑。我真想像巴德尔先生那样，几乎神不知鬼不觉地从世界上淡出——哪怕像他那样，得在酒馆地窖里挨一品脱酒罐敲一下脑袋也在所不惜。

===Original===
In such a mood it is imperative that one should seek distraction. The shining example of Mr. Harry Hawk did not lure me. Taking to drink would be a nuisance. Work was what I wanted. I would toil like a navvy all day among the fowls, separating them when they fought, gathering in the eggs when they laid, chasing them across country when they got away, and even, if necessity arose, painting their throats with turpentine when they were stricken with roop. Then, after dinner, when the lamps were lit, and Mrs. Ukridge nursed Edwin and sewed, and Ukridge smoked cigars and incited the gramophone to murder “Mumbling Mose,” I would steal away to my bedroom, and write﻿—and write﻿—and *write*. And go on writing till my fingers were numb and my eyes refused to do their duty. And, when time had passed, I might come to feel that it was all for the best. A man must go through the fire before he can write his masterpiece. We learn in suffering what we teach in song. What we lose on the swings we make up on the roundabouts. Jerry Garnet, the Man, might become a depressed, hopeless wreck, with the iron planted immovably in his soul; but Jeremy Garnet, the Author, should turn out such a novel of gloom, that strong critics would weep, and the public jostle for copies till Mudie’s doorway became a shambles.

Thus might I some day feel that all this anguish was really a blessing﻿—effectively disguised.

---

But I doubted it.

---

===Chinese===
人在这种心绪之下，务必得寻些排遣。哈里·霍克先生那光辉的榜样吸引不了我。纵酒又会惹麻烦。我要的是工作。我要像个挖土工一样，整日在鸡群里卖苦力：它们打架时去拉架，下蛋时去收蛋，跑掉了就满山遍野去追，情势所需时，还得趁它们染上鸡喉炎，用松节油涂抹它们的喉咙。然后，晚饭之后，灯点亮了，厄克里奇太太一边看顾埃德温一边做针线，厄克里奇抽着雪茄、怂恿留声机去谋杀《咕哝的莫斯》，我便悄悄溜回卧室，写啊——写啊——*写*啊。一直写到手指麻木、双眼罢工方休。待时日流转，我说不定会渐渐觉得，这一切原是好事。一个人总得先历过烈火，才写得出他的杰作。我们在苦难中学得的，在歌中传授。秋千上失掉的，旋转木马上补回来。作为“人”的杰里米·加尼特，也许会沦为一副消沉绝望的残躯，灵魂里楔进一块纹丝不动的铁；然而作为“作家”的杰里米·加尼特，却该写出一部如此阴郁的小说，叫铁打的评论家潸然泪下，叫大众挤破门槛抢购，直到马迪图书馆的门前变成一片屠场。

这样，有朝一日我便可能觉得，这满腹愁苦实在是一桩福分——只是伪装得颇为到家。

---

可我信不过这一套。

---

===Original===
We were none of us very cheerful now at the farm. Even Ukridge’s spirit was a little daunted by the bills which poured in by every post. It was as if the tradesmen of the neighbourhood had formed a league, and were working in concert. Or it may have been due to thought-waves. Little accounts came not in single spies but in battalions. The popular demand for the sight of the colour of his money grew daily. Every morning at breakfast he would give us fresh bulletins of the state of mind of each of our creditors, and thrill us with the announcement that Whiteley’s were getting cross, and Harrod’s jumpy, or that the bearings of Dawlish, the grocer, were becoming overheated. We lived in a continual atmosphere of worry. Chicken and nothing but chicken at meals, and chicken and nothing but chicken between meals had frayed our nerves. An air of defeat hung over the place. We were a beaten side, and we realised it. We had been playing an uphill game for nearly two months, and the strain was beginning to tell. Ukridge became uncannily silent. Mrs. Ukridge, though she did not understand, I fancy, the details of the matter, was worried because Ukridge was. Mrs. Beale had long since been turned into a soured cynic by the lack of chances vouchsafed her for the exercise of her art. And as for me, I have never since spent so profoundly miserable a week. I was not even permitted the anodyne of work. There seemed to be nothing to do on the farm. The chickens were quite happy, and only asked to be let alone and allowed to have their meals at regular intervals. And every day one or more of their number would vanish into the kitchen, Mrs. Beale would serve up the corpse in some cunning disguise, and we would try to delude ourselves into the idea that it was something altogether different.

There was one solitary gleam of variety in our menu. An editor sent me a cheque for a set of verses. We cashed that cheque and trooped round the town in a body, laying out the money. We bought a leg of mutton, and a tongue and sardines, and pineapple chunks, and potted meat, and many other noble things, and had a perfect banquet. Mrs. Beale, with the scenario of a smile on her face, the first that she had worn in these days of stress, brought in the joint, and uncovered it with an air.

“Thank God!” said Ukridge, as he began to carve.

It was the first time I had ever heard him say a grace, and if ever an occasion merited such a deviation from habit, this occasion did.

After that we relapsed into routine again.

Deprived of physical labour, with the exception of golf and bathing﻿—trivial sports compared with work in the fowl-run at its hardest﻿—I tried to make up for it by working at my novel.

===Chinese===
如今农场里，我们谁都不大快活。连厄克里奇的精神，也被每班邮差涌来的账单压得有点蔫了。那光景，就仿佛左近的商贩们结成了同盟，协同作战。也可能该归咎于思维波。小账单前来，从来不是单兵探子，而是整营整师地开到。想一睹他钞票颜色的群众呼声，一天高过一天。每天早晨吃早饭时，他都会向我们发布最新战报，通报各债主的心境，诸如怀特利开始动火了，哈罗德沉不住气了，或是杂货商道利什的轴承渐渐过热了，听得我们心惊肉跳。我们终日生活在愁云惨雾里。正餐吃鸡，除了鸡还是鸡；两餐之间垫肚子，也是鸡，除了鸡还是鸡——我们的神经都给磨毛了。一片败象笼罩着农场。我们是一支败军之师，自己也心里有数。近两个月来我们一直打的是逆风球，劳损终于开始显形。厄克里奇变得出奇地沉默。厄克里奇太太，我想，虽然不明就里，却也因为厄克里奇发愁而发愁。比尔太太呢，因为始终得不到施展手艺的机会，早就变成了一位怨气冲天的犬儒。至于我，后来再没度过像那样凄惶欲绝的一个星期。连工作这剂止痛药也不容我享用。农场上似乎无事可做。鸡群们快活得很，只求别去打扰，准它们按时开饭。可它们的队伍里，每天总有一只或几只消失在厨房里，比尔太太便把尸体乔装改扮端上桌来，我们还竭力哄骗自己，说那完完全全是另一码东西。

我们的菜单上，也曾有过一线孤零零的花色。有位编辑寄来一张支票，酬我一组诗。我们兑了那张支票，倾巢而出，成群结队地把钱花遍全城。我们买了一条羊腿、一根口条、沙丁鱼、菠萝块、罐头肉酱，还有许多别的高贵物事，美美地大宴了一场。比尔太太端着大盘进来，脸上挂着一抹初具轮廓的微笑——那是她在这段艰难时世里头一回展颜——又郑重其事地把盖子一揭。

“谢天谢地！”厄克里奇说着，动手切肉。

这是我头一回听他念谢饭祷告。倘若说有哪个场合当得起这等破例，那便是这一回了。

那以后，我们又跌回了老一套。

除了打高尔夫和洗海水澡——跟鸡场最吃重的活计一比，不过是无足轻重的小运动——我既与体力劳动绝缘，便想靠小说创作来弥补。

===Original===
It refused to materialise.

The only progress I achieved was with my villain.

I drew him from the professor, and made him a blackmailer. He had several other social defects, but that was his profession. That was the thing he did really well.

It was on one of the many occasions on which I had sat in my room, pen in hand, through the whole of a lovely afternoon, with no better result than a slight headache, that I bethought me of that little paradise on the Ware Cliff, hung over the sea and backed by green woods. I had not been there for some time, owing principally to an entirely erroneous idea that I could do more solid work sitting in a straight hard chair at a table than lying on soft turf with the sea wind in my eyes.

But now the desire to visit that little clearing again drove me from my room. In the drawing-room below the gramophone was dealing brassily with “Mister Blackman.” Outside the sun was just thinking of setting. The Ware Cliff was the best medicine for me. What does Kipling say?

===Chinese===
它拒绝成形。

我唯一的进展，是在反派身上。

我以教授为原型，把他写成了一个敲诈勒索的歹徒。他还有好几样别的社会性劣迹，但敲诈是他的职业。那才是他真正擅长的本事。

那样可爱的下午，我手握钢笔在房里枯坐整场的次数已然不少；便是在其中一回，我想起了韦尔崖上那座小天堂——它悬在大海之上，背倚苍翠林莽。我有阵子没去那儿了，主要缘于一个全然错误的观念：以为挺直腰板坐在硬椅子上伏案，能比躺在软草皮上、任海风吹进眼里干出更多扎实的活儿。

可这会儿，重访那片小林间空地的欲望，把我从房里撵了出来。楼下客厅里，留声机正铜管铿锵地折腾着《布莱克曼先生》。屋外，太阳正琢磨着下山。韦尔崖于我是最好的药。吉卜林怎么说来着？

===Original===
> “And soon you will find that the sun and the wind

 And the Djinn of the Garden, too,

 Have lightened the hump, Cameelious Hump,

 The Hump that is black and blue.”

His instructions include digging with a hoe and a shovel also, but I could omit that. The sun and the wind were what I needed.

===Chinese===
> “不久你就会发现，太阳和风

 还有园中的魔灵，

 都卸下那驼峰，骆驼似的驼峰，

 那乌青斑驳的驼峰。”

他的方子上还有拿锄头铁锹挖土这一条，不过我可以略去。我需要的，正是太阳和风。

===Original===
I took the upper road. In certain moods I preferred it to the path along the cliff. I walked fast. The exercise was soothing.

To reach my favourite clearing I had to take to the fields on the left, and strike down hill in the direction of the sea. I hurried down the narrow path.

I broke into the clearing at a jog trot, and stood panting. And at the same moment, looking cool and beautiful in her white dress, Phyllis entered in from the other side. Phyllis﻿—without the professor.

===Chinese===
我走了上面那条大路。在某些心绪之下，我宁走它，也不走沿崖的小径。我走得很快。这运动叫人心境平和。

要到我心仪的那片空地，得拐进左边的田野，朝着海的方向一路下坡。我沿着窄径匆匆而下。

我小跑着冲进空地，站定了直喘。也就在同一刻，一袭白裙、清丽照人的菲莉斯从另一头走了进来。菲莉斯——身边没有教授。

## XIV A Council of War / 十四 战时会议

===Original===
“The fact is,” said Ukridge, “if things go on as they are now, my lad, we shall be in the cart. This business wants bucking up. We don’t seem to be making headway. Why it is, I don’t know, but we are *not* making headway. Of course, what we want is time. If only these scoundrels of tradesmen would leave us alone for a spell, we could get things going properly. But we’re hampered and rattled and worried all the time. Aren’t we, Millie?”

“Yes, dear.”

“You don’t let me see the financial side of the thing enough,” I complained. “Why don’t you keep me thoroughly posted? I didn’t know we were in such a bad way. The fowls look fit enough, and Edwin hasn’t had one for a week.”

“Edwin knows as well as possible when he’s done wrong, Mr. Garnet,” said Mrs. Ukridge. “He was so sorry after he had killed those other two.”

“Yes,” said Ukridge, “I saw to that.”

===Chinese===
“实情是这么回事，”厄克里奇说，“要是事情照眼下这样下去，我的老弟，咱们就要砸锅了。这买卖得打打气。咱们似乎毫无进展。为什么这样，我不知道，可咱们就是*没有*进展。当然，咱们缺的是时间。只要这帮商贩恶棍肯放咱们一马，让咱们喘上一口气，咱们就能把局面好好转动起来。可咱们时时刻刻受人掣肘，叫人吵得六神无主。是不是，米莉？”

“是的，亲爱的。”

“你可没让我把事情的银钱这一面看真切，”我抱怨道，“你为什么不随时向我通报详情？我都不知道咱们已经糟到这步田地。鸡瞧着倒是满健壮，埃德温也整整一个礼拜没叼走一只了。”

“埃德温对自己闯了祸，心里再明白不过，加尼特先生，”厄克里奇太太说，“上回他弄死了那两只以后，可难过哩。”

“可不，”厄克里奇说，“那还是我亲自过问的。”

===Original===
“As far as I can see,” I continued, “we’re going strong. Chicken for breakfast, lunch, and dinner is a shade monotonous, perhaps, but look at the business we’re doing. We sold a whole heap of eggs last week.”

“But not enough, Garny old man. We aren’t making our presence felt. England isn’t ringing with our name. We sell a dozen eggs where we ought to be selling them by the hundred, carting them off in trucks for the London market and congesting the traffic. Harrod’s and Whiteley’s and the rest of them are beginning to get on their hind legs and talk. That’s what they’re doing. Devilish unpleasant they’re making themselves. You see, laddie, there’s no denying it﻿—we *did* touch them for the deuce of a lot of things on account, and they agreed to take it out in eggs. All they’ve done so far is to take it out in apologetic letters from Millie. Now, I don’t suppose there’s a woman alive who can write a better apologetic letter than her nibs, but, if you’re broad-minded and can face facts, you can’t help seeing that the juiciest apologetic letter is not an egg. I mean to say, look at it from their point of view. Harrod﻿—or Whiteley﻿—comes into his store in the morning, rubbing his hands expectantly. ‘Well,’ he says, ‘how many eggs from Combe Regis today?’ And instead of leading him off to a corner piled up with bursting crates, they show him a four-page letter telling him it’ll all come right in the future. I’ve never run a store myself, but I should think that would jar a chap. Anyhow, the blighters seem to be getting tired of waiting.”

“The last letter from Harrod’s was quite pathetic,” said Mrs. Ukridge sadly.

I had a vision of an eggless London. I seemed to see homes rendered desolate and lives embittered by the slump, and millionaires bidding against one another for the few rare specimens which Ukridge had actually managed to despatch to Brompton and Bayswater.

Ukridge, having induced himself to be broad-minded for five minutes, now began to slip back to his own personal point of view and became once more the man with a grievance. His fleeting sympathy with the wrongs of Mr. Harrod and Mr. Whiteley disappeared.

===Chinese===
“依我看，”我接着说，“咱们势头正猛呢。早中晚三餐全是鸡，或许单调了那么一点，可你瞧瞧咱们的营业额。上礼拜我们卖掉了好大一堆鸡蛋。”

“可是不够多，加尼老兄。咱们还没打出威风。英格兰还没有到处回响着咱们的大名。咱们本该论百地卖蛋，一卡车一卡车运去伦敦市场，把交通都堵它一堵，如今却是论打地卖。哈罗德、怀特利，还有他们那一伙，已经开始后腿着地、人立而起，开口发难了。他们正在干的就是这个。那副嘴脸刁钻得叫人不痛快。你瞧，小老弟，这可赖不掉——咱们确实*曾*跟他们赊了一大票东西，他们也答应了拿鸡蛋抵账。可他们到手的，至今只有米莉一封又一封的道歉信。这嘛，我估摸着天底下再没有哪个女人，写道歉信能赛过咱们这位好夫人；可你要是心胸开阔、敢于直面事实，就不能不看到：再肥美的道歉信，也终究不是一枚鸡蛋。我是说，你从他们的角度想想。哈罗德——或者怀特利——清晨走进自己的铺子，搓着双手，满心期盼。‘怎么样，’他说，‘今天库姆里吉斯到了多少蛋？’人家没把他领到堆满胀裂货箱的角落去，反倒递给他一封四页的信，告诉他将来一切都会好起来。我自己没开过铺子，可我想这一下任谁都得心头冒火。反正，这帮混账看来是等得不耐烦了。”

“哈罗德上一封来信，写得怪可怜见的。”厄克里奇太太黯然道。

我眼前浮现出一座无蛋可食的伦敦。我仿佛看见家家户户因这场蛋荒而愁云惨淡、生计凋敝，百万富翁们为厄克里奇好不容易发到布朗普顿和贝斯沃特的那寥寥几枚珍品争相竞价。

厄克里奇强逼着自己心胸开阔了五分钟，此刻便开始滑回他个人的立场，重新变回那个满腹委屈的人。他对哈罗德先生与怀特利先生所受委屈的那点转瞬即逝的同情，随之烟消云散。

===Original===
“What it all amounts to,” he said complainingly, “is that they’re infernally unreasonable. I’ve done everything possible to meet them. Nothing could have been more manly and straightforward than my attitude. I told them in my last letter but three that I proposed to let them have the eggs on the *Times* instalment system, and they said I was frivolous. They said that to send thirteen eggs as payment for goods supplied to the value of £25 1s. 8½d. was mere trifling. Trifling, I’ll trouble you! That’s the spirit in which they meet my suggestions. It was Harrod who did that. I’ve never met Harrod personally, but I’d like to, just to ask him if that’s his idea of cementing amiable business relations. He knows just as well as anyone else that without credit commerce has no elasticity. It’s an elementary rule. I’ll bet he’d have been sick if chappies had refused to let him have tick when he was starting his store. Do you suppose Harrod, when he started in business, paid cash down on the nail for everything? Not a bit of it. He went about taking people by the coat-button and asking them to be good chaps and wait till Wednesday week. Trifling! Why, those thirteen eggs were absolutely all we had over after Mrs. Beale had taken what she wanted for the kitchen. As a matter of fact, if it’s anybody’s fault, it’s Mrs. Beale’s. That woman literally eats eggs.”

“The habit is not confined to her,” I said.

“Well, what I mean to say is, she seems to bathe in them.”

“She says she needs so many for puddings, dear,” said Mrs. Ukridge. “I spoke to her about it yesterday. And of course, we often have omelettes.”

“She can’t make omelettes without breaking eggs,” I urged.

===Chinese===
“归根结底，”他怨气冲天地说，“就是这帮人蛮不讲理到了极点。凡能做到的，我全都迁就了。我的态度再汉子、再爽直也没有了。我在倒数第四封信里通知他们，我打算按《泰晤士报》分期付款制的办法把鸡蛋拨付给他们，他们反倒说我轻浮儿戏。他们说，送十三枚鸡蛋来抵 25 英镑 1 先令 8 便士半的货款，纯属敷衍搪塞。敷衍搪塞，我请你评评理！他们便是用这种精神来对待我的提议的。干这事的正是哈罗德。我个人从没见过哈罗德，可我倒真想见见，就为问问他，这算不算他巩固友好商务往来的高见。他跟谁都一样明白：没有信用，商业便没有伸缩的余地。这是入门的规矩。我敢打赌，当年他创业时，要是哪位好汉不肯赊他账，他准得气个半死。你以为哈罗德当初下海时，样样东西都现钱现付、分文不欠？才不呢。他满街拽住人家的衣扣，求人家做做好人，宽限到下下个星期三再说。敷衍搪塞！嗐，那十三枚鸡蛋，是比尔太太把厨房要用的拿走之后，咱们压箱底的全部家当了。说实在的，真要怪谁，那就怪比尔太太。那女人简直是拿鸡蛋当饭吃。”

“有这习惯的可不止她一个。”我说。

“呃，我的意思是说，她简直像是在蛋里洗澡。”

“她说做布丁要用这么多呢，亲爱的，”厄克里奇太太说，“昨天我还跟她提过这事。当然啦，咱们也常吃煎蛋卷。”

“她总不能不打破鸡蛋就摊出煎蛋卷吧。”我帮腔道。

===Original===
“She can’t make them without breaking us, dammit,” said Ukridge. “One or two more omelettes, and we’re done for. No fortune on earth could stand it. We mustn’t have any more omelettes, Millie. We must economise. Millions of people get on all right without omelettes. I suppose there are families where, if you suddenly produced an omelette, the whole strength of the company would get up and cheer, led by father. Cancel the omelettes, old girl, from now onward.”

“Yes, dear. But﻿—”

“Well?”

“I don’t *think* Mrs. Beale would like that very much, dear. She has been complaining a good deal about chicken at every meal. She says that the omelettes are the only things that give her a chance. She says there are always possibilities in an omelette.”

“In short,” I said, “what you propose to do is deliberately to remove from this excellent lady’s life the one remaining element of poetry. You mustn’t do it. Give Mrs. Beale her omelettes, and let’s hope for a larger supply of eggs.”

===Chinese===
“她也不能不打破咱们就摊出煎蛋卷哪，见鬼。”厄克里奇说，“再这么一卷两卷地煎下去，咱们就全完了。天大的家私也架不住这么造。往后不许再煎蛋卷了，米莉。咱们得节俭。千千万万的人没有煎蛋卷，不也过得好好的。我料想有些人家里，你要是冷不丁端出一盘煎蛋卷，满座老小全得起立欢呼，由父亲领头。从今往后，把煎蛋卷取消了吧，老姑娘。”

“好的，亲爱的。可是——”

“可是什么？”

“我*想*比尔太太不会太乐意的，亲爱的。她一直满腹牢骚，嫌顿顿都是鸡。她说，煎蛋卷是她唯一能施展身手的东西。她说，一盘煎蛋卷里永远有大有可为的天地。”

“总而言之，”我说，“你打算做的，就是存心从这位好太太的生活里夺走仅存的最后一点诗意。这可使不得。把煎蛋卷还给比尔太太吧，咱们还是盼着鸡蛋多下些。”

===Original===
“Another thing,” said Ukridge. “It isn’t only that there’s a shortage of eggs. That wouldn’t matter so much if only we kept hatching out fresh squads of chickens. I’m not saying the hens aren’t doing their best. I take off my hat to the hens. As nice a hardworking lot as I ever want to meet, full of vigour and earnestness. It’s that damned incubator that’s letting us down all the time. The rotten thing won’t work. *I* don’t know what’s the matter with it. The long and the short of it is that it simply declines to incubate.”

“Perhaps it’s your dodge of letting down the temperature. You remember, you were telling me? I forget the details.”

“My dear old boy,” he said earnestly, “there’s nothing wrong with my figures. It’s a mathematical certainty. What’s the good of mathematics if not to help you work out that sort of thing? No, there’s something deuced wrong with the machine itself, and I shall probably make a complaint to the people I got it from. Where did we get the incubator, old girl?”

“Harrod’s, I think, dear﻿—yes, it was Harrod’s. It came down with the first lot of things.”

“Then,” said Ukridge, banging the table with his fist, while his glasses flashed triumph, “we’ve got ’em. The Lord has delivered Harrod’s into our hand. Write and answer that letter of theirs tonight, Millie. Sit on them.”

===Chinese===
“还有一桩，”厄克里奇说，“也不单是鸡蛋短缺。只要咱们能源源孵出一批批新鸡，缺蛋倒还罢了。我不是说母鸡们没尽心。我对母鸡们脱帽致敬。多好的一群勤勤恳恳的劳动者，我乐意结识得很，个个劲头十足、认真肯干。坏就坏在那台天杀的孵蛋器，它误了咱们一整路。那破玩意儿就是不肯干活。*我*可不知道它出了什么毛病。长话短说，它干脆拒绝孵蛋。”

“兴许坏在你调低温度的那个诀窍上。你可记得，你跟我讲过的？细节我忘了。”

“我亲爱的老弟，”他一脸恳切地说，“我的数字一丝毛病也没有。那是数学上的铁定结论。数学要不是帮你推算这类事情，还有什么用处？不，是机器本身坏得邪门，我多半得向卖它给咱们的那些人投诉。咱们的孵蛋器是在哪儿买的来着，老姑娘？”

“哈罗德，我想是，亲爱的——不错，正是哈罗德。它是跟头一批货色一起运来的。”

“这就对了，”厄克里奇一拳捶在桌上，眼镜片上闪过胜利的光辉，“咱们把他们拿住了。上帝把哈罗德交到了咱们手里。今晚就写回信答复他们，米莉。劈头盖脸训他们一顿。”

===Original===
“Yes, dear.”

“Tell ’em that we’d have sent them their confounded eggs long ago, if only their rotten, twopenny-ha’penny incubator had worked with any approach to decency.” He paused. “Or would you be sarcastic, Garny, old horse? No, better put it so that they’ll understand. Say that I consider that the manufacturer of the thing ought to be in Colney Hatch﻿—if he isn’t there already﻿—and that they are scoundrels for palming off a groggy machine of that sort on me.”

“The ceremony of opening the morning’s letters at Harrod’s ought to be full of interest and excitement tomorrow,” I said.

This dashing counter-stroke seemed to relieve Ukridge. His pessimism vanished. He seldom looked on the dark side of things for long at a time. He began now to speak hopefully of the future. He planned out ingenious improvements. Our fowls were to multiply so rapidly and consistently that within a short space of time Dorsetshire would be paved with them. Our eggs were to increase in size till they broke records and got three-line notices in the “Items of Interest” column in the *Daily Mail*. Briefly, each hen was to become a happy combination of rabbit and ostrich.

“There is certainly a good time coming,” I said. “May it be soon. Meanwhile, what of the local tradesmen?”

===Chinese===
“好的，亲爱的。”

“告诉他们，要不是他们那台烂得掉渣、不值两文半便士的孵蛋器稍稍像样地运转过，他们那批混账鸡蛋早就给送去多时了。”他顿了顿。“还是你来点刻薄的好，加尼老马？不，还是写得让他们看得懂为妙。就说我认定，那东西的制造商理应送进科尔尼哈奇——要是他还没进那儿的话——还说他们是一帮恶棍，竟把那样一台病歪歪的机器糊弄给我。”

“明天早晨哈罗德那边拆阅晨信的仪式，想必一定妙趣横生、激动人心。”我说。

这一记漂亮的反击似乎让厄克里奇浑身通泰。他的悲观一扫而空。他看事情的阴暗面，向来看不了多久。此刻他已经开始兴致勃勃地谈起未来。他规划起种种别出心裁的改良。咱们的鸡将要繁衍得又快又稳，不出多久，多塞特郡就要拿它们铺路。咱们的鸡蛋将越长越大，直到打破纪录，在《每日邮报》的“趣闻撷英”栏里赢得三行小讯。简而言之，每只母鸡都将成为兔子与鸵鸟的完美合体。

“好日子肯定要来了，”我说，“但愿来得快。眼下呢，本地的商贩们怎么办？”

===Original===
Ukridge relapsed once more into gloom.

“They are the worst of the lot. I don’t mind the London people so much. They only write, and a letter or two hurts nobody. But when it comes to butchers and bakers and grocers and fishmongers and fruiterers and whatnot coming up to one’s house and dunning one in one’s own garden﻿—well it’s a little hard, what?”

“Oh, then those fellows I found you talking to yesterday were duns? I thought they were farmers, come to hear your views on the rearing of poultry.”

“Which were they? Little chap with black whiskers and long, thin man with beard? That was Dawlish, the grocer, and Curtis, the fishmonger. The others had gone before you came.”

It may be wondered why, before things came to such a crisis, I had not placed my balance at the bank at the disposal of the senior partner for use on behalf of the farm. The fact was that my balance was at the moment small. I have not yet in the course of this narrative gone into my pecuniary position, but I may state here that it was an inconvenient one. It was big with possibilities, but of ready cash there was but a meagre supply. My parents had been poor. But I had a wealthy uncle. Uncles are notoriously careless of the comfort of their nephews. Mine was no exception. He had views. He was a great believer in matrimony, as, having married three wives﻿—not simultaneously﻿—he had every right to be. He was also of opinion that the less money the young bachelor possessed, the better. The consequence was that he announced his intention of giving me a handsome allowance from the day that I married, but not an instant before. Till that glad day I would have to shift for myself. And I am bound to admit that﻿—for an uncle﻿—it was a remarkably sensible idea. I am also of opinion that it is greatly to my credit, and a proof of my pure and unmercenary nature, that I did not instantly put myself up to be raffled for, or rush out into the streets and propose marriage to the first lady I met. But I was making quite enough with my pen to support myself, and, be it never so humble, there is something pleasant in a bachelor existence, or so I had thought until very recently.

===Chinese===
厄克里奇又跌回了阴郁里。

“他们是最难缠的一伙。伦敦那边的我倒不怎么在乎。他们只管写信，一两封信又伤不了人。可要是屠夫、面包师、杂货商、鱼贩子、水果贩，还有诸如此类的人物，一路找上门来，堵在自家花园里跟人讨债——这可就有点吃不消了，你说是不是？”

“哦，那么昨天我撞见跟你说话的那几位，都是讨债的？我还当是来听取您家禽饲养高见的庄稼汉呢。”

“是哪几位？黑连鬓胡的小个子，还有留胡子的高瘦个儿？那是杂货商道利什和鱼贩柯蒂斯。其余几位在你来之前就走了。”

人们也许要奇怪，事情还没闹到这般绝境之前，我怎么不把银行里的存款交由这位大股东支配，充作农场经费。实情是，我那时的存款寥寥。在本篇记述里，我还从未交代过自己的财务状况，不妨在此说明：那状况很叫人尴尬。它远景宏大、机遇无穷，可论现钱，却只有薄薄一份。我父母生前清寒。但我有位阔叔叔。叔叔们向来以漠视侄儿的安乐著称，我这位也未见例外。他自有主张。他是婚姻的大力信徒——连娶过三位太太——并非同时——也确有信的资格。他还认定，年轻的单身汉钱越少越好。于是他宣布：从我成婚那天起，他愿给我一笔可观的津贴，但一刻也不提前。在那大喜的日子到来之前，我只得自谋生计。而且我不得不承认——以一位叔叔而论——这主意出奇地明智。我还认为，我没有立刻把自己摆出去供人抽奖，也没有冲上街头向遇见的头一位女士求婚，足见我品性纯洁、不贪钱财，颇堪自许。不过我靠笔杆子挣的钱，养活自己原本绰绰有余，而且不论多么清苦，单身汉的日子总有它的妙处——至少直到最近，我一直这么以为。

===Original===
I had thus no great stake in Ukridge’s chicken farm. I had contributed a modest five pounds to the preliminary expenses, and another five after the roop incident. But further I could not go with safety. When his income is dependent on the whims of editors and publishers, the prudent man keeps something up his sleeve against a sudden slump in his particular wares. I did not wish to have to make a hurried choice between matrimony and the workhouse.

Having exhausted the subject of finance﻿—or, rather, when I began to feel that it was exhausting me﻿—I took my clubs, and strolled up the hill to the links to play off a match with a sportsman from the village. I had entered some days previously for a competition for a trophy (I quote the printed notice) presented by a local supporter of the game, in which up to the present I was getting on nicely. I had survived two rounds, and expected to beat my present opponent, which would bring me into the semifinal. Unless I had bad luck, I felt that I ought to get into the final, and win it. As far as I could gather from watching the play of my rivals, the professor was the best of them, and I was convinced that I should have no difficulty with him. But he had the most extraordinary luck at golf, though he never admitted it. He also exercised quite an uncanny influence on his opponent. I have seen men put completely off their stroke by his good fortune.

I disposed of my man without difficulty. We parted a little coldly. He had decapitated his brassy on the occasion of his striking Dorsetshire instead of his ball, and he was slow in recovering from the complex emotions which such an episode induces.

In the clubhouse I met the professor, whose demeanour was a welcome contrast to that of my late antagonist. The professor had just routed his opponent, and so won through to the semifinal. He was warm, but jubilant.

I congratulated him, and left the place.

===Chinese===
如此说来，我在厄克里奇的养鸡场里本没押上多少本钱。开办费用我薄薄地认捐过五镑，鸡喉炎风波之后又添了五镑。再往后，为稳妥计，我就不敢奉陪了。一个人的进项既系于编辑与出版商的一念之间，精明人总得在袖子里留一手，防着自己那路货色行情骤跌。我可不想在结婚和济贫院之间被迫仓促二选一。

银钱这个题目既已谈透——更确切地说，等我开始觉得被它榨得油尽灯枯——我便扛起球杆，踱上山去高尔夫球场，同村里一位运动健将赛一场球。几天前我报名参加了一场奖杯赛（此处照抄印好的启事），奖品由本地一位高尔夫爱好者捐赠；到眼下为止，我在其中一路顺风。我已熬过了两轮，预计能拿下眼前这位对手，从而挺进半决赛。只要不倒大霉，我自觉理应打入决赛，并捧杯而归。就我观察各路对手的球路所知，教授是其中最强的一位；而我也确信，对付他我不会有困难。可他打高尔夫的运气好得出奇，虽然他自己从不认账。他对对手还有一股说不出的慑人影响。我亲眼见过有人被他的好运搅得完全乱了挥杆。

我不费吹灰之力料理了我的对手。分手时彼此有点冷淡。他有一杆没打着球，打进了多塞特郡的地皮，顺势把三号木杆的杆头都磕飞了；这般事故激发的百般滋味，他一时半会儿缓不过来。

在俱乐部会所里，我遇见了教授。他的做派与我方才那位对手恰成可喜的对照。教授刚把他的对手打得落花流水，从而杀进半决赛。他浑身发热，却喜气洋洋。

我道了贺，便离开了会所。

===Original===
Phyllis was waiting outside. She often went round the course with him.

“Good afternoon,” I said. “Have you been round with the professor?”

“Yes. We must have been in front of you. Father won his match.”

“So he was telling me. I was very glad to hear it.”

“Did you win, Mr. Garnet?”

===Chinese===
菲莉斯在外面等着。她常陪他走完整场球。

“午安，”我说，“您陪教授走完一圈了？”

“是啊。我们想必一直在你们前头。父亲赢了他的比赛。”

“他刚跟我提了。听了很替他高兴。”

“您赢了吗，加尼特先生？”

===Original===
“Yes. Pretty easily. My opponent had bad luck all through. Bunkers seemed to have a magnetic attraction for him.”

“So you and father are both in the semifinal? I hope you will play very badly.”

“Thank you,” I said.

“Yes, it does sound rude, doesn’t it? But father has set his heart on winning this year. Do you know that he has played in the final round two years running now?”

“Really?”

===Chinese===
“赢了。赢得很轻松。我那位对手从头倒霉到尾。沙坑对他仿佛有磁石般的引力。”

“这么说，您和父亲都进了半决赛？我盼着您打得一塌糊涂。”

“多谢。”我说。

“是啊，听上去是够无礼的，对不对？可父亲今年一心要夺魁。您可知道，他已经连着两年打进决赛轮了？”

“当真？”

===Original===
“Both times he was beaten by the same man.”

“Who was that? Mr. Derrick plays a much better game than anybody I have seen on these links.”

“It was nobody who is here now. It was a Colonel Jervis. He has not come to Combe Regis this year. That’s why father is hopeful.”

“Logically,” I said, “he ought to be certain to win.”

“Yes; but, you see, you were not playing last year, Mr. Garnet.”

===Chinese===
“两回都输给了同一个人。”

“是谁？德里克先生的球，比我在这些球场上见过的谁都高明得多。”

“不是如今在场的哪一位。是位杰维斯上校。他今年没来库姆里吉斯。所以父亲才抱有指望。”

“照逻辑讲，”我说，“他理应稳操胜券。”

“是啊；可是您瞧，去年您还没来打球呢，加尼特先生。”

===Original===
“Oh, the professor can make rings round me,” I said.

“What did you go round in today?”

“We were playing match-play, and only did the first dozen holes; but my average round is somewhere in the late eighties.”

“The best father has ever done is ninety, and that was only once. So you see, Mr. Garnet, there’s going to be another tragedy this year.”

“You make me feel a perfect brute. But it’s more than likely, you must remember, that I shall fail miserably if I ever do play your father in the final. There are days when I play golf as badly as I play tennis. You’ll hardly believe me.”

===Chinese===
“哦，教授能把我绕得团团转。”我说。

“您今天一轮打了几杆？”

“我们打的是比洞赛，只打了头十二个洞；不过我平均一轮下来，总在八十八九杆上下。”

“父亲打出的最好成绩是九十杆，还只有那么一回。所以您瞧，加尼特先生，今年又要有一场悲剧了。”

“您让我觉得自己活像个恶棍。可您得记住，倘若我真跟令尊在决赛里碰上，我极可能败得一塌糊涂。我有些日子打高尔夫，糟得跟我打网球一个水平。您几乎不会相信。”

===Original===
She smiled reminiscently.

“Tom is much too good at tennis. His service is perfectly dreadful.”

“It’s a little terrifying on first acquaintance.”

“But you’re better at golf than at tennis, Mr. Garnet. I wish you were not.”

“This is special pleading,” I said. “It isn’t fair to appeal to my better feelings, Miss Derrick.”

===Chinese===
她回忆起什么似的，笑了。

“汤姆的网球可太厉害了。他的发球实在要命。”

“初次领教，确实叫人胆战心惊。”

“不过您打高尔夫比打网球在行，加尼特先生。我倒希望不是这样。”

“这叫歪理偏袒，”我说，“诉诸我的善心，可不公平，德里克小姐。”

===Original===
“I didn’t know golfers had any where golf was concerned. Do you really have your off-days?”

“Nearly always. There are days when I slice with my driver as if it were a bread-knife.”

“Really?”

“And when I couldn’t putt to hit a haystack.”

“Then I hope it will be on one of those days that you play father.”

===Chinese===
“我原不知道打高尔夫的人在高尔夫上还有善心这一说。您真有状态糟透的日子？”

“几乎回回如此。有些日子，我一开球就打出右曲球，那支木杆在我手里跟面包刀一个用法。”

“真的？”

“还有些日子，我推杆推出去，连干草堆都打不中。”

“那我就盼着，您跟父亲碰上的，恰是那样一个日子。”

===Original===
“I hope so, too,” I said.

“You hope so?”

“Yes.”

“But don’t you want to win?”

“I should prefer to please you.”

===Chinese===
“我也盼着呢。”我说。

“您也盼着？”

“是啊。”

“可您不想赢吗？”

“我宁愿讨您的欢心。”

===Original===
“Really, how very unselfish of you, Mr. Garnet,” she replied, with a laugh. “I had no idea that such chivalry existed. I thought a golfer would sacrifice anything to win a game.”

“Most things.”

“And trample on the feelings of anybody.”

“Not everybody,” I said.

At this point the professor joined us.

===Chinese===
“哎呀，您可真是无私得很，加尼特先生，”她笑着答道，“我全然想不到世间还有这般骑士精神。我原以为打高尔夫的人为了赢一场球，什么都肯牺牲。”

“多半东西都肯。”

“还不惜把谁的感情都踩在脚下。”

“倒也不是随便谁。”我说。

正说着，教授走了过来。

## I A Letter with a Postscript / 一 附有附言的信

===Original===
“A gentleman called to see you when you were out last night, sir,” said Mrs. Medley, my landlady, removing the last of the breakfast things.

“Yes?” I said, in my affable way.

“A gentleman,” said Mrs. Medley meditatively, “with a very powerful voice.”

“Caruso?”

“Sir?”

“I said, did he leave a name?”

===Chinese===
“您昨晚出门的时候，有位先生来拜访过您，先生。”我的房东梅德利太太一边说，一边撤走最后几样早餐杯盘。

“是吗？”我和颜悦色地说。

“一位先生，”梅德利太太若有所思地说，“嗓门特别洪亮。”

“卡鲁索？”

“您说什么，先生？”

“我是说，他留名字了吗？”

===Original===
“Yes, sir. Mr. Ukridge.”

“Oh, my sainted aunt!”

“Sir?”

“Nothing, nothing.”

“Thank you, sir,” said Mrs. Medley, withdrawing from the presence.

Ukridge! Oh, hang it! I had not met him for years, and, glad as I am, as a general thing, to see the friends of my youth when they drop in for a chat, I doubted whether I was quite equal to Ukridge at the moment. A stout fellow in both the physical and moral sense of the words, he was a trifle too jumpy for a man of my cloistered and intellectual life, especially as just now I was trying to plan out a new novel, a tricky job demanding complete quiet and seclusion. It had always been my experience that, when Ukridge was around, things began to happen swiftly and violently, rendering meditation impossible. Ukridge was the sort of man who asks you out to dinner, borrows the money from you to pay the bill, and winds up the evening by embroiling you in a fight with a cabman. I have gone to Covent Garden balls with Ukridge, and found myself legging it down Henrietta Street in the grey dawn, pursued by infuriated costermongers.

===Chinese===
“留了，先生。厄克里奇先生。”

“哦，我的老天爷！”

“您说什么，先生？”

“没什么，没什么。”

“谢谢您，先生。”梅德利太太说着，从御前退了下去。

厄克里奇！哦，要命！我已多年没见过他。虽说一般而言，少年时代的朋友登门闲谈，我总是欢迎的，可眼下我怀疑自己消受不起厄克里奇。此君无论按肉身还是按道义的讲法，都是一条壮汉；对于过我这种隐修式文人生活的人，他未免太蹦跶了些——何况我正构思一部新小说，那是个精细活儿，要求绝对的清静与遁世。我一向的经验是：厄克里奇一在场，事态便开始急转直下、风雷并作，叫人无从静思。厄克里奇是那样一种人：请你出去吃饭，跟你借钱付账单，最后再让你跟马车夫卷进一场恶斗，为这一晚收尾。我同厄克里奇去过考文特花园的舞会，结果在灰白的黎明，发现自己正撒腿狂奔在海丽埃塔街上，身后是怒火中烧的水果贩子们。

===Original===
I wondered how he had got my address, and on that problem light was immediately cast by Mrs. Medley, who returned, bearing an envelope.

“It came by the morning post, sir, but it was left at Number Twenty by mistake.”

“Oh, thank you.”

“Thank *you*, sir,” said Mrs. Medley.

I recognised the handwriting. The letter, which bore a Devonshire postmark, was from an artist friend of mine, one Lickford, who was at present on a sketching tour in the west. I had seen him off at Waterloo a week before, and I remember that I had walked away from the station wishing that I could summon up the energy to pack and get off to the country somewhere. I hate London in July.

The letter was a long one, but it was the postscript which interested me most.

===Chinese===
我正纳闷他怎么弄到我的地址，梅德利太太便立刻为这谜题投下一线光明——她回来了，手里拿着一只信封。

“是早晨邮班送来的，先生，只是错投到了二十号。”

“哦，谢谢你。”

“该谢*您*才对，先生。”梅德利太太说。

我认得这笔迹。信上盖着德文郡的邮戳，寄信人是我的一位画家朋友，姓利克福德，眼下正在西部写生旅行。一星期前我到滑铁卢车站为他送行，我记得自己走出车站时一路盼着，盼自己能鼓起劲头，收拾行装，也去乡下什么地方躲一躲。我讨厌七月的伦敦。

信写得很长，可最让我感兴趣的是那条附言。

===Original===
> “… By the way, at Yeovil I ran into an old friend of ours, Stanley Featherstonehaugh Ukridge, of all people. As large as life﻿—quite six foot two, and tremendously filled out. I thought he was abroad. The last I heard of him was that he had started for Buenos Aires in a cattle ship, with a borrowed pipe by way of luggage. It seems he has been in England for some time. I met him in the refreshment-room at Yeovil Station. I was waiting for a down train; he had changed on his way to town. As I opened the door, I heard a huge voice entreating the lady behind the bar to ‘put it in a pewter’; and there was S. F. U. in a villainous old suit of grey flannels (I’ll swear it was the one he had on last time I saw him), with pince-nez tacked on to his ears with ginger-beer wire as usual, and a couple of inches of bare neck showing between the bottom of his collar and the top of his coat﻿—you remember how he could never get a stud to do its work. He also wore a mackintosh, though it was a blazing day.

> “He greeted me with effusive shouts. Wouldn’t hear of my standing the racket. Insisted on being host. When we had finished, he fumbled in his pockets, looked pained and surprised, and drew me aside. ‘Look here, Licky, old horse,’ he said, ‘you know I never borrow money. It’s against my principles. But I *must* have a couple of bob. Can you, my dear good fellow, oblige me with a couple of bob till next Tuesday? I’ll tell you what I’ll do. (In a voice full of emotion). I’ll let you have this (producing a beastly little threepenny bit with a hole in it which he had probably picked up in the street) until I can pay you back. This is of more value to me than I can well express, Licky my boy. A very, very dear friend gave it to me when we parted, years ago﻿ ﻿… It’s a wrench﻿ ﻿… Still﻿—no, no﻿ ﻿… You must take it, you must take it. Licky, old man, shake hands, old horse. Shake hands, my boy.’ He then tottered to the bar, deeply moved, and paid up out of the five shillings which he had made as an afterthought. He asked after you, and said you were one of the noblest men on earth. I gave him your address, not being able to get out of it, but if I were you I should fly while there is yet time.”

It seemed to me that the advice was good and should be followed. I needed a change of air. London may have suited Doctor Johnson, but in the summer time it is not for the ordinary man. What I wanted, to enable me to give the public of my best (as the reviewer of a weekly paper, dealing with my last work, had expressed a polite hope that I would continue to do) was a little haven in the country somewhere.

I rang the bell.

“Sir?” said Mrs. Medley.

===Chinese===
> “……顺带一提，在约维尔，我竟撞见了咱们的一位老熟人——偏偏是斯坦利·费瑟斯通豪·厄克里奇。还是那副活生生的原样——足足六英尺二，还发福得可观。我以为他还在国外。我最后听到的消息，是他搭一艘运牛船动身去了布宜诺斯艾利斯，全部行李是一支借来的烟斗。看样子他在英国已待了不少日子。我是在约维尔车站的茶点室遇见他的。我在等下行车，他是进城途中换车。一推开门，就听见一条洪亮的大嗓门正恳求柜台后的女士‘把它倒进锡杯里’；S. F. U. 果然就在那儿，穿一身邪门歪道的灰色法兰绒旧衣（我敢起誓，就是我上回见他穿的那身），夹鼻眼镜照例用姜汁汽水的铁丝箍在耳朵上，领子下沿和上衣上沿之间露出两英寸光脖子——你总记得，他从没能让一颗领扣恪尽职守。那天烈日当空，他还罩着一件雨衣。

> “他大呼小叫地招呼我，热情四溢。我说什么也不肯让我破费，非要尽地主之谊。吃喝完毕，他在口袋里摸索一阵，面露痛楚与讶异，把我拉到一边。‘听我说，利基老马，’他说，‘你知道我从不借钱。这有违我的原则。可我*非*得要两枚先令不可。亲爱的好伙计，能否行个好，借我两先令，撑到下星期二？我告诉你我要怎么办。（声音满怀深情）我把我这个押给你（掏出一枚恶劣的小小三便士，中间还带着窟窿，八成是街上捡的），等我回头还你钱。这东西于我的价值，实非言语所能表达，利基我的孩子。多年前分别时，一位顶顶亲爱的朋友把它送给了我……我真舍不得……不过——不，不成……你必须收下，你必须收下。利基老兄，握个手，老马。握个手，我的孩子。’随后他百感交集，踉跄着走向柜台，用他临时又讨来的五先令付了账。他问起你的近况，还说你是世上最高贵的男子汉之一。我把你的地址给了他——实在推脱不掉——可我要是你，就趁还来得及，赶紧远走高飞。”

我看这忠告很有道理，应当遵行。我正需要换换空气。伦敦或许合约翰逊博士的意，可到了夏天，它不属于寻常人。我需要的——好让我把最好的自己献给大众（某家周刊的评论家评到我上一部作品时，曾客客气气地表示希望我继续如此）——是乡间某处的一个小小避风港。

我按了铃。

“您叫我，先生？”梅德利太太应道。

===Original===
“I’m going away for a bit,” I said.

“Yes, sir.”

“I don’t know where. I’ll send you the address, so that you can forward letters.”

“Yes, sir.”

“And, if Mr. Ukridge calls again﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===
“我要出门一阵子。”我说。

“好的，先生。”

“去哪儿还没定。我会把地址寄来，信件你好转给我。”

“好的，先生。”

“还有，要是厄克里奇先生再来……”

===Original===
At this point a thunderous knocking on the front door interrupted me. Something seemed to tell me who was at the end of that knocker. I heard Mrs. Medley’s footsteps pass along the hall. There was the click of the latch. A volume of sound rushed up the stairs.

“Is Mr. Garnet in? Where is he? Show me the old horse. Where is the man of wrath? Exhibit the son of Belial.”

There followed a violent crashing on the stairs, shaking the house.

“Garnet! Where are you, laddie? Garnet!! **Garnet!!!!!**”

Stanley Featherstonehaugh Ukridge was in my midst.

===Chinese===
话说到这儿，前门响起的一阵雷鸣般的敲门声把我打断了。冥冥中似有什么告诉我，门环那头是谁。我听见梅德利太太的脚步声穿过门厅。门闩咔哒一响。一团声浪冲上楼来。

“加尼特先生在家吗？人在哪儿？带我去见那匹老马。震怒之人在哪里？把彼列之子展示出来。”

接着是楼梯上一阵天崩地裂的巨响，震得整栋房子直晃。

“加尼特！你在哪儿，小老弟？加尼特！！**加尼特！！！！！**”

斯坦利·费瑟斯通豪·厄克里奇，已然置身我等中间。

## VIII A Little Dinner at Ukridge’s / 八 厄克里奇家的小宴

===Original===
“Edwin comes today,” said Mrs. Ukridge.

“And the Derricks,” said Ukridge, sawing at the bread in his energetic way. “Don’t forget the Derricks, Millie.”

“No, dear. Mrs. Beale is going to give us a very nice dinner. We talked it over yesterday.”

“Who is Edwin?” I asked.

We were finishing breakfast on the second morning after my visit to the Derricks. I had related my adventures to the staff of the farm on my return, laying stress on the merits of our neighbours and their interest in our doings, and the Hired Retainer had been sent off next morning with a note from Mrs. Ukridge inviting them to look over the farm and stay to dinner.

===Chinese===
“埃德温今天就到。”厄克里奇太太说。

“还有德里克一家。”厄克里奇说着，以他那精力充沛的架势锯着面包。“别忘了德里克一家，米莉。”

“忘不了，亲爱的。比尔太太要给我们做一顿非常丰盛的晚餐。我们昨天商量过了。”

“埃德温是谁？”我问。

这是我拜访德里克一家之后的第二个早晨，我们快吃完早饭了。回来时我已把自己那番奇遇向农场全体员工汇报过，着重强调了咱们邻居的人品，以及他们对咱们事业的热心；第二天一早，雇来的老家人便揣着厄克里奇太太的便函被打发出门，邀请他们来参观农场并留下用晚餐。

===Original===
“Edwin?” said Ukridge. “Oh, beast of a cat.”

“Oh, Stanley!” said Mrs. Ukridge plaintively. “He’s not. He’s such a dear, Mr. Garnet. A beautiful, purebred Persian. He has taken prizes.”

“He’s always taking something. That’s why he didn’t come down with us.”

“A great, horrid, *beast* of a dog bit him, Mr. Garnet.” Mrs. Ukridge’s eyes became round and shone. “And poor Edwin had to go to a cats’ hospital.”

“And I hope,” said Ukridge, “the experience will do him good. Sneaked a dog’s dinner, Garnet, under his very nose, if you please. Naturally the dog lodged a protest.”

===Chinese===
“埃德温？”厄克里奇说。“哦，是那只畜生猫。”

“哎呀，斯坦利！”厄克里奇太太委屈地说。“他才不是呢。他可乖啦，加尼特先生。一只漂亮的纯种波斯猫，还得过奖呢。”

“他老在拿这拿那。所以他才没跟咱们一道下来。”

“一条凶神恶煞的*畜生*狗咬了他，加尼特先生。”厄克里奇太太的眼睛瞪圆了，闪着光。“可怜的埃德温只好进了猫医院。”

“我还盼着，”厄克里奇说，“这场教训能让他学个乖。你瞧瞧，他竟当着狗的面，把人家的饭偷吃了。狗当然要提出抗议。”

===Original===
“I’m so afraid that he will be frightened of Bob. He will be very timid, and Bob’s so boisterous. Isn’t he, Mr. Garnet?”

“That’s all right,” said Ukridge. “Bob won’t hurt him, unless he tries to steal his dinner. In that case we will have Edwin made into a rug.”

“Stanley doesn’t like Edwin,” said Mrs. Ukridge, sadly.

Edwin arrived early in the afternoon, and was shut into the kitchen. He struck me as a handsome cat, but nervous.

The Derricks followed two hours later. Mr. Chase was not of the party.

===Chinese===
“我真担心他会害怕博布。他胆子会很小，博布又这么闹腾。是不是，加尼特先生？”

“那不碍事，”厄克里奇说，“博布不会伤他——除非他打算偷博布的饭。真到那步田地，咱们就把埃德温做成一张地毯。”

“斯坦利不喜欢埃德温。”厄克里奇太太悲哀地说。

埃德温下午早早就到了，被关进了厨房。在我印象里，他是只漂亮的猫，就是神经紧张。

两个钟头后，德里克一家接踵而至。蔡斯先生没有同来。

===Original===
“Tom had to go to London,” explained the professor, “or he would have been delighted to come. It was a disappointment to the boy, for he wanted to see the farm.”

“He must come some other time,” said Ukridge. “We invite inspection. Look here,” he broke off suddenly﻿—we were nearing the fowl-run now, Mrs. Ukridge walking in front with Phyllis Derrick﻿—“were you ever at Bristol?”

“Never, sir,” said the professor.

“Because I knew just such another fat little buffer there a few years ago. Gay old bird, he was. He﻿—”

“This is the fowl-run, professor,” I broke in, with a moist, tingling feeling across my forehead and up my spine. I saw the professor stiffen as he walked, while his face deepened in colour. Ukridge’s breezy way of expressing himself is apt to electrify the stranger.

===Chinese===
“汤姆不得不去伦敦，”教授解释道，“不然他准会高高兴兴地来。这孩子失望得很，因为他想看看农场。”

“他改日一定要来，”厄克里奇说，“我们欢迎参观。哎对了，”他突然岔开话头——这会儿我们已经走近鸡场，厄克里奇太太同菲莉斯·德里克走在前头——“您从前到过布里斯托尔吗？”

“从未去过，先生。”教授说。

“因为几年前，我在那儿认识过一位跟您一模一样的矮胖小老儿。快活的老家伙，他——”

“这就是鸡场了，教授。”我赶紧插话，只觉额头湿漉漉的，一阵麻痒顺着脊梁骨往上爬。我看见教授边走边僵直起来，脸色也沉了下去。厄克里奇那种随口乱飘的说话方式，最容易叫生人触电。

===Original===
“You will notice the able way﻿—ha! ha!﻿—in which the wire-netting is arranged,” I continued feverishly. “Took some doing, that. By Jove, yes. It was hot work. Nice lot of fowls, aren’t they? Rather a mixed lot of course. Ha! ha! That’s the dealer’s fault though. We are getting quite a number of eggs now. Hens wouldn’t lay at first. Couldn’t make them.”

I babbled on, till from the corner of my eye I saw the flush fade from the professor’s face and his back gradually relax its poker-like attitude. The situation was saved for the moment, but there was no knowing what further excesses Ukridge might indulge in. I managed to draw him aside as we went through the fowl-run, and expostulated.

“For goodness sake be careful,” I whispered. “You’ve no notion how touchy he is.”

“But *I* said nothing,” he replied, amazed.

“Hang it, you know, nobody likes to be called a fat little buffer to his face.”

===Chinese===
“您注意看这铁丝网——哈！哈！——布置得多么能干，”我接着话茬，语速飞快。“那可费了些功夫。老天作证，可不是嘛。干得热火朝天。这批鸡不赖，对不对？当然，品种是杂了点。哈！哈！不过那是贩子的错。如今我们的鸡蛋相当可观了。母鸡起初不肯下蛋。怎么哄也不行。”

我就这么滔滔不绝，直到眼角瞥见教授脸上的红晕褪了下去，背脊也渐渐从那副烧火棍式的姿势里松开来。局面暂时保住了，可厄克里奇还会干出什么过火的事，谁也说不准。穿过鸡场时，我设法把他拉到一边，好言相劝。

“千万千万嘴下留德，”我低声说，“你不知道他多忌讳。”

“可我什么也没*说*啊。”他答道，一脸惊愕。

“见鬼，你知道，没人乐意被人当面叫矮胖小老儿。”

===Original===
“What! My dear old man, nobody minds a little thing like that. We can’t be stilted and formal. It’s ever so much more friendly to relax and be chummy.”

Here we rejoined the others, and I was left with a leaden foreboding of gruesome things in store. I knew what manner of man Ukridge was when he relaxed and became chummy. Friendships of years’ standing had failed to survive the test.

For the time being, however, all went well. In his role of lecturer he offended no one, and Phyllis and her father behaved admirably. They received his strangest theories without a twitch of the mouth.

“Ah,” the professor would say, “now is that really so? Very interesting indeed.”

Only once, when Ukridge was describing some more than usually original device for the furthering of the interests of his fowls, did a slight spasm disturb Phyllis’s look of attentive reverence.

===Chinese===
“什么！我亲爱的老伙计，这点小事谁在乎。咱们总不能拿腔作调、客客气气吧。放松一点、亲热一点，才够朋友得多呢。”

这时我们回到了众人身边，我心里只余下一块铅似的预感，晓得惨事在后头。厄克里奇放松下来、亲热起来是什么做派，我太清楚了。多年的交情，都没能经得起这个考验。

不过，眼下倒还一切太平。他以讲师的身份上场，倒没开罪任何人；菲莉斯和她父亲的仪态也可圈可点。听到他最离奇的高论，两人嘴角都不曾抽动一下。

“哦，”教授总是说，“当真如此吗？实在有趣得很。”

只有一回——厄克里奇正描述某个为增进鸡群福利而设计的、比寻常别致得多的装置——菲莉斯那副专注崇敬的神情，才被一阵轻微的痉挛搅乱了一下。

===Original===
“And you have really had no previous experience in chicken-farming?” she said.

“None,” said Ukridge, beaming through his glasses. “Not an atom. But I can turn my hand to anything, you know. Things seem to come naturally to me somehow.”

“I see,” said Phyllis.

It was while matters were progressing with this beautiful smoothness that I observed the square form of the Hired Retainer approaching us. Somehow﻿—I cannot say why﻿—I had a feeling that he came with bad news. Perhaps it was his air of quiet satisfaction which struck me as ominous.

“Beg pardon, Mr. Ukridge, sir.”

===Chinese===
“这么说，您从前真没碰过养鸡这一行？”她说。

“一点没有，”厄克里奇隔着眼镜容光焕发地说，“一丝一毫都没有。不过你知道，我干什么都是一把好手。事情到了我手里，不知怎么的就顺顺当当。”

“原来如此。”菲莉斯说。

正当局面以这般美妙的顺滑推进之际，我望见雇来的老家人那方方正正的身形朝我们走来。不知怎的——我说不出为什么——我预感他带来的是坏消息。也许是他那副安安静静、心满意足的神气，在我看来越发像凶兆。

“对不住，打搅了，厄克里奇先生。”

===Original===
Ukridge was in the middle of a very eloquent excursus on the feeding of fowls, a subject on which he held views of his own as ingenious as they were novel. The interruption annoyed him.

“Well, Beale,” he said, “what is it?”

“That there cat, sir, what came today.”

“Oh, Beale,” cried Mrs. Ukridge in agitation, “*what* has happened?”

“Having something to say to the missis﻿—”

===Chinese===
厄克里奇正讲到一半——一篇关于喂鸡的雄辩长论；在这题目上，他自有一套见解，既新颖又精巧。这一打搅让他大为光火。

“什么事，比尔？”他说，“说吧。”

“是今天来的那只猫，先生。”

“哎呀，比尔，”厄克里奇太太激动地叫起来，“到底*出*了什么事？”

“俺有句话要跟太太说——”

===Original===
“What has happened? Oh, Beale, don’t say that Edwin has been hurt? Where is he? Oh, *poor* Edwin!”

“Having something to say to the missis﻿—”

“If Bob has bitten him I hope he had his nose *well* scratched,” said Mrs. Ukridge vindictively.

“Having something to say to the missis,” resumed the Hired Retainer tranquilly, “I went into the kitchen ten minutes back. The cat was sitting on the mat.”

Beale’s narrative style closely resembled that of a certain book I had read in my infancy. I wish I could remember its title. It was a well-written book.

===Chinese===
“出什么事了？哎呀比尔，可别说埃德温伤着了？他在哪儿？哦，*可怜的*埃德温！”

“俺有句话要跟太太说——”

“要是博布咬了他，我盼着他把博布的鼻子*好好*抓了一通。”厄克里奇太太狠狠地说。

“俺有句话要跟太太说，”雇来的老家人不动声色地重新开口，“十分钟前，俺就进了厨房。那猫正蹲在垫子上。”

比尔的叙事文风，同我幼年读过的一本书极为相似。可惜我想不起书名了。那是本写得很好的书。

===Original===
“Yes, Beale, yes?” said Mrs. Ukridge. “Oh, do go on.”

“ ‘Hullo, puss,’ I says to him, ‘and ’ow are *you*, sir?’ ‘Be careful,’ says the missis. ‘ ’E’s that timid,’ she says, ‘you wouldn’t believe,’ she says. ‘ ’E’s only just settled down, as you may say,’ she says. ‘Ho, don’t you fret,’ I says to her, ‘ ’im and me understands each other. ’Im and me,’ I says, ‘is old friends. ’E’s my dear old pal, Corporal Banks.’ She grinned at that, ma’am, Corporal Banks being a man we’d ’ad many a ’earty laugh at in the old days. ’E was, in a manner of speaking, a joke between us.”

“Oh, do﻿—go﻿—on, Beale. What has happened to Edwin?”

The Hired Retainer proceeded in calm, even tones.

“We was talking there, ma’am, when Bob, what had followed me unknown, trotted in. When the cat ketched sight of ’im sniffing about, there was such a spitting and swearing as you never ’eard; and blowed,” said Mr. Beale amusedly, “blowed if the old cat didn’t give one jump, and move in quick time up the chimley, where ’e now remains, paying no ’eed to the missis’ attempts to get him down again.”

===Chinese===
“是啊，比尔，然后呢？”厄克里奇太太说。“哦，快往下讲。”

“‘你好啊，猫咪，’俺对他说，‘你*老人家*可好？’‘当心点，’太太说。‘他胆小得很，’她说，‘你不会信的，’她说。‘他这会儿才刚安顿下来，可以说，’她说。‘嗬，你甭操心，’俺对她说，‘俺跟他彼此心照。俺跟他，’俺说，‘是老朋友了。他就是俺亲爱的老伙计，班克斯下士。’太太听了直咧嘴，太太——那位班克斯下士，从前那些日子，俺们没少拿他开怀大笑过。可以说，他是俺俩之间的一个笑话。”

“哦，往——下——讲啊，比尔。埃德温到底怎么样了？”

雇来的老家人以平静匀称的语调继续下去。

“俺们正说着话呢，太太，博布——他悄悄跟着俺，俺不知道——小跑着进来了。那猫一眼瞧见他在四下里嗅，立马炸了毛，又啐又骂，那动静您这辈子没听过；嘿，”比尔先生饶有兴致地说，“嘿，那老猫一纵身，齐步开拔，噌噌就上了烟囱，这会儿还蹲在上头呢，太太怎么哄也不理，就是不肯下来。”

===Original===
Sensation, as they say in the reports.

“But he’ll be cooked,” cried Phyllis, open-eyed.

“No, he won’t. Nor will our dinner. Mrs. Beale always lets the kitchen fire out during the afternoon. And how she’s going to light it with that﻿—”

There was a pause while one might count three. It was plain that the speaker was struggling with himself.

“﻿—that cat,” he concluded safely, “up the chimney? It’s a cold dinner we’ll get tonight, if that cat doesn’t come down.”

===Chinese===
用法庭报道里的话说：全场哗然。

“可他会被烤熟的。”菲莉斯睁大了眼睛叫道。

“不会的。咱们的晚饭也不会。每到下午，比尔太太总让厨房的火熄掉。可她要怎么生火，那只——”

他停顿了一下，够人数到三的工夫。显然，说话的人正在跟自己搏斗。

“——那只猫，”他总算安全收了尾，“蹲在烟囱上头？今儿晚上咱们就只好吃冷餐了，要是那猫再不下来的话。”

===Original===
The professor’s face fell. I had remarked on the occasion when I had lunched with him his evident fondness for the pleasures of the table. Cold impromptu dinners were plainly not to his taste.

We went to the kitchen in a body. Mrs. Beale was standing in front of the empty grate, making seductive cat-noises up the chimney.

“What’s all this, Mrs. Beale?” said Ukridge.

“He won’t come down, sir, not while he thinks Bob’s about. And how I’m to cook dinner for five with him up the chimney I don’t see, sir.”

“Prod at him with a broom handle, Mrs. Beale,” said Ukridge.

===Chinese===
教授的脸垮了下来。先前在他家午宴时我就注意到，他对口腹之乐显然情有独钟。临时凑合的冷餐，明摆着不合他的口味。

我们一行人浩浩荡荡开进厨房。比尔太太正站在空空的炉膛前，朝着烟囱发出诱哄猫儿的叫声。

“这是怎么回事，比尔太太？”厄克里奇说。

“他不肯下来，先生，只要他还当博布在跟前，就不下来。他蹲在烟囱上，俺可怎么做五个人的晚饭，俺想不出来，先生。”

“拿扫帚柄捅捅他，比尔太太。”厄克里奇说。

===Original===
“Oh, don’t hurt poor Edwin,” said Mrs. Ukridge.

“I ’ave tried that, sir, but I can’t reach him, and I’m only bin and drove ’im further up. What must be,” added Mrs. Beale philosophically, “must be. He may come down of his own accord in the night. Bein’ ’ungry.”

“Then what we must do,” said Ukridge in a jovial manner, which to me at least seemed out of place, “is to have a regular, jolly picnic-dinner, what? Whack up whatever we have in the larder, and eat that.”

“A regular, jolly picnic-dinner,” repeated the professor gloomily. I could read what was passing in his mind﻿—remorse for having come at all, and a faint hope that it might not be too late to back out of it.

“That will be splendid,” said Phyllis.

===Chinese===
“哦，别伤着可怜的埃德温。”厄克里奇太太说。

“俺试过咧，先生，可俺够不着他，反倒把他捅得更上去了。该咋的，”比尔太太豁达地补了一句，“就咋的。夜里没准儿他自己就下来了。饿的。”

“那咱们就该这么办，”厄克里奇喜气洋洋地说——至少在我看来未免不合时宜——“正儿八经来一顿痛快的野餐晚饭，怎么样？把食橱里有的全摆出来，吃了它。”

“正儿八经、痛快的野餐晚饭。”教授阴沉沉地重复道。他心里转的念头我读得出来——悔不该来，还有一丝渺茫的希望：如今打道回府，兴许还不算太晚。

“那太妙了。”菲莉斯说。

===Original===
“Er, I think, my dear sir,” said her father, “it would be hardly fair of us to give any further trouble to Mrs. Ukridge and yourself. If you will allow me, therefore, I will﻿—”

Ukridge became gushingly hospitable. He refused to think of allowing his guests to go empty away. He would be able to whack up something, he said. There was quite a good deal of the ham left. He was sure. He appealed to me to endorse his view that there was a tin of sardines and part of a cold fowl and plenty of bread and cheese.

“And after all,” he said, speaking for the whole company in the generous, comprehensive way enthusiasts have, “what more do we want in weather like this? A nice, light, cold, dinner is ever so much better for us than a lot of hot things.”

We strolled out again into the garden, but somehow things seemed to drag. Conversation was fitful, except on the part of Ukridge, who continued to talk easily on all subjects, unconscious of the fact that the party was depressed and at least one of his guests rapidly becoming irritable. I watched the professor furtively as Ukridge talked on, and that ominous phrase of Mr. Chase’s concerning four-point-seven guns kept coming into my mind. If Ukridge were to tread on any of his pet corns, as he might at any minute, there would be an explosion. The snatching of the dinner from his very mouth, as it were, and the substitution of a bread-and-cheese and sardines menu had brought him to the frame of mind when men turn and rend their nearest and dearest.

The sight of the table, when at length we filed into the dining room, sent a chill through me. It was a meal for the very young or the very hungry. The uncompromising coldness and solidity of the viands was enough to appal a man conscious that his digestion needed humouring. A huge cheese faced us in almost a swashbuckling way. I do not know how else to describe it. It wore a blatant, rakish, *nemo-me-impune-lacessit* air, and I noticed that the professor shivered slightly as he saw it. Sardines, looking more oily and uninviting than anything I had ever seen, appeared in their native tin beyond the loaf of bread. There was a ham, in its third quarter, and a chicken which had suffered heavily during a previous visit to the table. Finally, a black bottle of whisky stood grimly beside Ukridge’s plate. The professor looked the sort of man who drank claret of a special year, or nothing.

===Chinese===
“呃，我想，亲爱的先生，”她父亲说，“再给您和厄克里奇太太添麻烦，就太说不过去了。所以，要是您允许的话，我就——”

厄克里奇登时热情好客得如决堤洪水。让客人们饿着肚子离开？他想都不肯想。他说，他准能张罗出些东西来。火腿还剩下不少呢。他敢肯定。他还引我作证，担保还有一罐沙丁鱼、半只冷鸡，外加面包和奶酪管够。

“再说，”他说道，以热心人那种慷慨周全的口气，替全体宾客发了言，“这样的天气，咱们还想怎么样呢？一顿可口、清淡的冷餐，比一堆滚烫的菜对身体好多啦。”

我们又踱回花园里去，可不知怎么，气氛就是活泛不起来。谈话有一搭没一搭——只有厄克里奇例外，他继续就一切题目侃侃而谈，浑然不觉满座意兴阑珊，至少有一位客人正迅速地火冒三丈。厄克里奇絮絮讲着，我偷偷端详教授，蔡斯先生那句关于四点七英寸大炮的不祥话，一再浮上我的心头。厄克里奇只要踩着他哪颗鸡眼——而这一刻随时可能到来——就要爆炸了。眼看着到嘴的晚饭被夺走，换成面包奶酪加沙丁鱼的菜单，已把他逼到那种心绪：人到了这步田地，是会反转身来撕咬自己至亲至近之人的。

等我们终于鱼贯走进饭厅，桌上的景象叫我不寒而栗。那是给年纪极小或饿极之人吃的一餐。那一片毫不通融的冷与硬，足以吓坏任何自知肠胃需要将就着哄的人。一大块奶酪以近乎剑客寻衅的架势与我们对面而立——除了这么说，我不知道还能怎么形容。它摆出一副嚣张的、放浪的、*nemo-me-impune-lacessit*（谁惹我谁遭殃）的神气；我注意到，教授一眼望见它，微微打了个寒噤。沙丁鱼在面包那头以其原装铁罐登场，油光锃亮，比我有生以来见过的任何东西都更倒人胃口。一条火腿已去了四分之三，一只鸡则在上一轮上桌时创巨痛深。最后，一瓶黑黢黢的威士忌冷冰冰立在厄克里奇的餐盘旁。教授看那样子，是只喝特选年份红葡萄酒、否则滴水不沾的那种人。

===Original===
We got through the meal somehow, and did our best to delude ourselves into the idea that it was all great fun; but it was a shallow pretence. The professor was very silent by the time we had finished. Ukridge had been terrible. The professor had forced himself to be genial. He had tried to talk. He had told stories. And when he began one﻿—his stories would have been the better for a little more briskness and condensation﻿—Ukridge almost invariably interrupted him, before he had got halfway through, without a word of apology, and started on some anecdote of his own. He furthermore disagreed with nearly every opinion the professor expressed. It is true that he did it all in such a perfectly friendly way, and was obviously so innocent of any intention of giving offence, that another man﻿—or the same man at a better meal﻿—might have overlooked the matter. But the professor, robbed of his good dinner, was at the stage when he had to attack somebody. Every moment I had been expecting the storm to burst.

It burst after dinner.

We were strolling in the garden, when some demon urged Ukridge, apropos of the professor’s mention of Dublin, to start upon the Irish question. I had been expecting it momentarily, but my heart seemed to stand still when it actually arrived.

Ukridge probably knew less about the Irish question than any male adult in the kingdom, but he had boomed forth some very positive opinions of his own on the subject before I could get near enough to him to whisper a warning. When I did, I suppose I must have whispered louder than I had intended, for the professor heard me, and my words acted as the match to the powder.

“He’s touchy about Ireland, is he?” he thundered. “Drop it, is it? And why? Why, sir? I’m one of the best tempered men that ever came from Dublin, let me tell you, and I will not stay here to be insulted by the insinuation that I cannot discuss Ireland as calmly as anyone in this company or out of it. Touchy about Ireland, is it? Touchy﻿—?”

===Chinese===
这顿饭我们总算对付过去了，还竭力哄自己说这一切其乐无穷；可那是层一戳就破的伪装。到吃完时，教授已沉默寡言。厄克里奇的表现可怕至极。教授强打精神撑着和气。他试着找话讲。他讲了些故事。而他每开一个头——他的故事若能再爽利些、再精炼些，本可以更拿得出手——厄克里奇几乎回回不等他讲到一半，就毫无歉意地打断，自顾自说起另一段掌故。教授发表什么见解，他几乎都要唱反调。不假，他做这一切时态度友善至极，显然全无冒犯之意，换一个人——或者同一个人换一顿好饭——兴许就不了了之。可教授被夺去了他的好晚饭，正到了非找个人开刀不可的阶段。风暴欲来，我时时刻刻都在等着它炸响。

饭后，它炸响了。

我们正在花园里散步，教授提到了都柏林，这时不知哪个恶魔撺掇厄克里奇，就爱尔兰问题开了腔。我无时无刻不在提防这一着，可它当真来临时，我的心还是仿佛停跳了一拍。

关于爱尔兰问题，厄克里奇的知识多半比王国境内任何成年男子都少；可不等我凑近他耳边递一句警告，他已轰隆隆抛出了好几条斩钉截铁的个人见解。等我终于凑近时，我那耳语想必是超出了预想的音量——教授听见了，我的话成了点着火药的那根火柴。

“他忌讳爱尔兰，是吗？”教授雷霆大怒。“闭嘴不谈，是吗？凭什么？凭什么，先生？告诉您，我是都柏林出过的脾气最好的人之一，我绝不留在原地，受人含沙射影的侮辱，影射我不能像场内场外任何人一样心平气和地谈爱尔兰。忌讳爱尔兰，是吗？忌讳——？”

===Original===
“But, professor﻿—”

“Take your hand off my arm, Mr. Garnet. I will not be treated like a child. I am as competent to discuss the affairs of Ireland without heat as any man, let me tell you.”

“Father﻿—”

“And let me tell you, Mr. Ukridge, that I consider your opinions poisonous. Poisonous, sir. And you know nothing whatever about the subject, sir. Every word you say betrays your profound ignorance. I don’t wish to see you or to speak to you again. Understand that, sir. Our acquaintance began today, and it will cease today. Good night to you, sir. Come, Phyllis, me dear. Mrs. Ukridge, good night.”

===Chinese===
“可是，教授——”

“把您的手从我胳膊上拿开，加尼特先生。我不容许被人当小孩子哄。告诉您，我同任何人一样，有本事不带火气地讨论爱尔兰事务。”

“爸爸——”

“还要告诉您，厄克里奇先生，我认为您的见解是毒药。毒药，先生。而且您对这个题目一无所知，先生。您吐出的每个字，都暴露着您深不见底的无知。我不想再见到您，也不想再同您说话。把这话记住，先生。咱们的交情始于今日，也将终于今日。晚安，先生。走吧，菲莉斯，乖囡。厄克里奇太太，晚安。”

## XXIII After the Storm / 二十三 风暴之后

===Original===
A yell of welcome drowned the tumult of the looters.

“Is that you, Garny, old horse? What’s up? What’s the matter? Has everyone gone mad? Who are those infernal scoundrels in the fowl-run? What are they doing? What’s been happening?”

“I have been entertaining a little meeting of your creditors,” I said. “And now they are entertaining themselves.”

“But what did you let them do it for?”

“What is one amongst so many?”

===Chinese===
一声欢呼的呐喊，压过了劫掠者们的喧嚣。

“是你吗，加尼老马？出什么事了？怎么回事？大家都疯了不成？鸡场里那群天杀的恶棍是谁？他们在干什么？出了什么事？”

“我刚才正招待着你的债主们开一个小会，”我说，“现在轮到他们自娱自乐了。”

“可你干吗由着他们胡来？”

“寡不敌众，为之奈何？”

===Original===
“Well, ’pon my Sam,” moaned Ukridge, as, her sardonic calm laid aside, that sinister hen which we called Aunt Elizabeth flashed past us pursued by the whiskered criminal, “it’s a little hard! I can’t go away for a day﻿—”

“You certainly can’t! You’re right there. You can’t go away without a word﻿—”

“Without a word? What do you mean? Garny, old boy, pull yourself together. You’re overexcited. Do you mean to tell me you didn’t get my note?”

“What note?”

“The one I left on the dining-room table.”

===Chinese===
“唉，我向上天起誓，”厄克里奇哀叹道。恰在此时，那只被我们唤作伊丽莎白姨妈的阴险母鸡，撇开它一惯的讥诮安详，被那个蓄络腮胡的罪犯追着，从我们面前一闪而过。“这可有点儿难受！我才离开一天——”

“你当然是不能！这话不假。你不能一声不吭就一走了之——”

“一声不吭？你这是什么话？加尼老兄，定定神。你太激动了。你莫不是要告诉我，你没见到我的便条？”

“什么便条？”

“我留在饭厅桌上的那张。”

===Original===
“There was no note there.”

“What!”

I was reminded of the scene that had taken place on the first day of our visit.

“Feel in your pockets,” I said.

“Why, damme, here it is!” he said in amazement.

===Chinese===
“桌上可没有便条。”

“什么！”

我想起我们到访头一天发生过的一幕。

“摸摸你的口袋。”我说。

“咦，怪了，它在这儿呢！”他惊讶地说。

===Original===
“Of course. Where did you expect it would be? Was it important?”

“Why, it explained the whole thing.”

“Then,” I said, “I wish you would let me read it. A note like that ought to be worth reading.”

“It was telling you to sit tight and not worry about us going away﻿—”

“That’s good about worrying. You’re a thoughtful chap, Ukridge.”

===Chinese===
“可不。你还指望它在哪儿？要紧吗？”

“怎么不要紧，它把一切都说明白了。”

“那么，”我说，“劳驾让我拜读一下吧。那样的便条，想必值得一读。”

“它让你稳坐钓鱼台，别为我们出门操心——”

“这声操心说得好。你真是个体贴人的人，厄克里奇。”

===Original===
“﻿—because we should be back immediately.”

“And what sent you up to town?”

“Why, we went to touch Millie’s Aunt Elizabeth.”

“Oh!” I said, a light shining on the darkness of my understanding.

“You remember Aunt Elizabeth? The old girl who wrote that letter.”

===Chinese===
“——因为我们立刻就回来。”

“那是什么风把你们吹进城去了？”

“嗨，我们去找米莉的伊丽莎白姨妈敲竹杠去了。”

“哦！”我说，一道光照亮了我懵懂的黑暗。

“你还记得伊丽莎白姨妈吧？写那封信的老太太。”

===Original===
“I know. She called you a gaby.”

“And a guffin.”

“Yes. I remember thinking her a shrewd and discriminating old lady, with a great gift for character delineation. So you went to touch her?”

“That’s it. We had to have more money. So I naturally thought of her. Aunt Elizabeth isn’t what you might call an admirer of mine﻿—”

“Bless her for that!”

===Chinese===
“记得。她管你叫傻小子。”

“还叫你老糊涂。”

“不错。我记得当时就想，这位老太太精明识人，勾勒人物性格的天才着实了得。这么说，你们找她敲竹杠去了？”

“正是。我们非得再弄点钱。于是我自然想到了她。伊丽莎白姨妈算不上我的崇拜者——”

“这一点要为她祝福！”

===Original===
“﻿—but she’s very fond of Millie, and would do anything if she’s allowed to chuck about a few home-truths before doing it. So we went off together, looked her up at her house, stated our case, and collected the stuff. Millie and I shared the work. She did the asking, while I inquired after the rheumatism. She mentioned the figure that would clear us; I patted the dog. Little beast! Got after me when I wasn’t looking and chewed my ankle!”

“Thank Heaven!”

“In the end Millie got the money, and I got the home-truths.”

“Did she call you a gaby?”

“Twice. And a guffin three times.”

===Chinese===
“——可她疼米莉疼得紧，只要允许她先把几句逆耳忠言劈头盖脸扔出来，让她干什么都行。于是我们双双出动，到她府上拜望，陈明来意，把货款收齐了。米莉跟我分工合作。她管开口要钱，我管问候风湿。她报出能解咱们之困的数目，我拍拍那条狗。小畜生！趁我不注意扑上来，啃了我的脚脖子！”

“谢天谢地！”

“最后，米莉拿到了钱，我领到了逆耳忠言。”

“她叫你傻小子了吗？”

“叫了两回。老糊涂叫了三回。”

===Original===
“Your Aunt Elizabeth is beginning to fascinate me. She seems just the sort of woman I would like. Well, you got the money?”

“Rather! And I’ll tell you another thing, old horse. I scored heavily at the end of the visit. She’d got to the quoting-proverbs stage by that time. ‘Ah, my dear,’ she said to Millie. ‘Marry in haste, repent at leisure.’ Millie stood up to her like a little brick. ‘I’m afraid that proverb doesn’t apply to me, Aunt Elizabeth,’ she said, ‘because I haven’t repented!’ What do you think of that, laddie?”

“Of course, she *hasn’t* had much leisure lately,” I agreed.

Ukridge’s jaw dropped slightly. But he rallied swiftly.

“Idiot! That wasn’t what she meant. Millie’s an angel!”

===Chinese===
“你这位伊丽莎白姨妈开始叫我着迷了。她似乎正是我中意的那类女人。那么，钱到手了？”

“到手了！再告诉你一件事，老马。临告辞我还大获全胜呢。她那时已进行到引格言的阶段了。‘唉，亲爱的，’她对米莉说，‘匆匆结婚，悠悠后悔。’米莉像条小好汉似的顶了回去。‘恐怕这句格言用不到我头上，伊丽莎白姨妈，’她说，‘因为我压根儿没后悔过！’这个你怎么看，小老弟？”

“可不，近来她确实*没有*多少闲暇。”我附和道。

厄克里奇的下巴微微垂了下来。但他随即恢复了元气。

“蠢材！她不是那个意思。米莉是位天使！”

===Original===
“Of course she is,” I said cordially. “She’s a precious sight too good for you, you old rotter. You bear that fact steadily in mind, and we’ll make something of you yet.”

At this point Mrs. Ukridge joined us. She had been exploring the house, and noting the damage done. Her eyes were open to their fullest extent.

“Oh, Mr. Garnet, *couldn’t* you have stopped them?”

I felt a worm. Had I done as much as I might have done to stem the tide?

“I’m awfully sorry, Mrs. Ukridge,” I said humbly. “I really don’t think I could have done much more. We tried every method. Beale had seven fights, and I made a speech on the lawn, but it was all no good. Directly they had finished the whisky﻿—”

===Chinese===
“她当然是，”我由衷地说，“配你呀，简直是天大的委屈，你这老无赖。你把这一条牢牢记在心里，咱们迟早还能把你造就成个人样。”

这时厄克里奇太太来到我们中间。她刚把宅子巡了一遍，清点损失。她那双眼睛瞪到了极限。

“哎呀，加尼特先生，您就*不能*拦住他们吗？”

我自觉形同虫豸。力挽狂澜这件事，我是否已尽了全力？

“实在对不住，厄克里奇太太，”我谦卑地说，“我真觉得已无计可施了。什么法子都试过。比尔打了七架，我还在草坪上发表了一篇演说，可全不顶事。他们一灌完威士忌——”

===Original===
Ukridge’s cry was like that of a lost spirit.

“They didn’t get hold of the whisky!”

“They did! It seemed to me that it would smooth things down a little if I served it out. The mob had begun to get a trifle out of hand.”

“I thought those horrid men were making a lot of noise,” said Mrs. Ukridge.

Ukridge preserved a gloomy silence. Of all the disasters of that stricken field, I think the one that came home most poignantly to him was the loss of the whisky. It seemed to strike him like a blow.

===Chinese===
厄克里奇的呼号，像出自一个沉沦的亡魂。

“他们没碰威士忌吧！”

“碰了！依我看，把酒分发下去，兴许能让局面平顺些。那群人已开始有点收不住缰了。”

“我还纳闷那些可怕的男人怎么那么吵。”厄克里奇太太说。

厄克里奇阴郁地缄默着。那片劫后战场上所有的灾殃里，我想最扎心刺骨击中他的，还数威士忌的覆灭。那打击对他不啻当头一棒。

===Original===
“Isn’t it about time to collect these men and explain things?” I suggested. “I don’t believe any of them know you’ve come back.”

“They will!” said Ukridge grimly, coming out of his trance. “They soon will! Where’s Beale! Beale!”

The Hired Retainer came running out at the sound of the well-remembered voice.

“Lumme, Mr. Ukridge, sir!” he gasped.

It was the first time Beale had ever betrayed any real emotion in my presence. To him, I suppose, the return of Ukridge was as sensational and astonishing an event as a reappearance from the tomb. He was not accustomed to find those who had shot the moon revisiting their ancient haunts.

===Chinese===
“是不是该把这些人集合起来，把事情讲讲清楚了？”我提议道，“我看他们谁都不知道你回来了。”

“会知道的！”厄克里奇从出神中醒转，狠狠地说，“马上就会知道！比尔呢！比尔！”

听见这条人人心有余悸的嗓音，雇来的老家人飞跑出来。

“我的老天，厄克里奇先生！”他倒抽着气说。

这是比尔头一回当着我的面流露真情。我猜想，在他看来，厄克里奇的归来，简直同死人还阳一样耸人听闻。夜里卷铺盖逃了债的人，居然还回到老巢来，这种事他可不习惯遇见。

===Original===
“Beale, go round the place and tell those scoundrels that I’ve come back, and would like a word with them on the lawn. And, if you find any of them stealing the fowls, knock them down!”

“I ’ave knocked down one or two,” said Beale, with approval. “That Charlie﻿—”

“Beale,” said Ukridge, much moved, “you’re an excellent fellow! One of the very best. I will pay you your back wages before I go to bed.”

“Those fellars, sir,” said Beale, having expressed his gratification, “they’re bin and scattered most of them birds already, sir. They’ve bin chasin’ of them this half-hour back.”

Ukridge groaned.

===Chinese===
“比尔，去各处转转，告诉那群恶棍，我回来了，请在草坪上对他们讲几句。还有，碰见偷鸡的，一律撂倒！”

“俺已经撂倒一两个咧，”比尔赞许地说，“那个查理——”

“比尔，”厄克里奇大为感动，“你是条好汉子！顶顶好的那种。睡觉之前，我一定把欠你的工钱结清。”

“那帮家伙，先生，”比尔表达过感激之后说，“他们已经把那些鸡追散了大半咧，先生。这半个钟头一直在撵它们。”

厄克里奇哀叹一声。

===Original===
“Scoundrels! Demons!”

Beale went off.

“Millie, old girl,” said Ukridge, adjusting the ginger-beer wire behind his ears and hoisting up his grey flannel trousers, which showed an inclination to sag, “you’d better go indoors. I propose to speak pretty chattily to these blighters, and in the heat of the moment one or two expressions might occur to me which you would not like. It would hamper me, your being here.”

Mrs. Ukridge went into the house, and the vanguard of the audience began to come on to the lawn. Several of them looked flushed and dishevelled. I have a suspicion that Beale had shaken sobriety into them. Charlie, I noticed, had a black eye.

They assembled on the lawn in the moonlight, and Ukridge, with his cap well over his eyes and his mackintosh hanging round him like a Roman toga, surveyed them sternly, and began his speech.

===Chinese===
“恶棍！恶魔！”

比尔去了。

“米莉，老姑娘，”厄克里奇说着，扶正耳后箍夹鼻眼镜的姜汁汽水铁丝，又提了提总想往下出溜的灰法兰绒裤子，“你最好先进屋去。我打算同这帮混账痛快聊聊；聊到兴头上，没准会蹦出一两个你不爱听的词儿。你在这儿，会缚住我的手脚。”

厄克里奇太太进了屋，听众的先头部队开始走上草坪。有几个满面红光、衣衫不整。我疑心比尔已把清醒摇晃进了他们体内。我还注意到，查理乌青了一只眼。

他们在月光下的草坪上集合完毕。厄克里奇帽檐压得低低的，雨衣披挂在身上，宛如罗马人的托加长袍；他严厉地扫视众人，开始了他的演说。

===Original===
“You﻿—you﻿—you﻿—you scoundrels! You blighters! You worms! You weeds!”

I always like to think of Stanley Featherstonehaugh Ukridge as I saw him at that moment. There have been times during a friendship of many years when his conduct did not recommend itself to me. It has sometimes happened that I have seen flaws in him. But on this occasion he was at his best. He was eloquent. He dominated his audience. Long before he had finished I was feeling relieved that he had thought of sending Mrs. Ukridge indoors when he did, and Beale was hanging on his words with a look in his eyes which I had never seen there before﻿—a look of reverence, almost of awe, the look of a disciple who listens to a master.

He poured scorn upon his hearers, and they quailed. He flung invective at them, and they wilted. Strange oaths, learned among strange men on cattle-ships or gleaned on the waterfronts of Buenos Aires and San Francisco, slid into the stream of his speech. It was hard, he said in part, it was, upon his Sam, a little hard that a gentleman﻿—a gentleman, moreover, who had done so much to stimulate local trade with large orders and whatnot﻿—could not run up to London for five minutes on business without having his private grounds turned upside down by a gang of cattle-ship adjectived San Francisco substantives who behaved as if the whole of the Buenos Aires phrased place belonged to them. He had intended to do well by them. He had meant to continue putting business in their way, expanding their trade. But would he after what had occurred? Not by a jugful! As soon as ever the sun had risen and another day begun, their miserable accounts should be paid in full, and their connection with him cut off. Afterwards it was probable that he would institute legal proceedings against them in the matter of trespass and wholesale damage to property, and if they didn’t all end their infernal days in some dashed prison they might consider themselves uncommonly lucky, and if they didn’t make themselves scarce in considerably under two ticks, he proposed to see what could be done with Beale’s shotgun. (Beale here withdrew with a pleased expression to fetch the weapon.) He was sick of them. They were blighters. Creatures that it would be fulsome flattery to describe as human beings. He would call them skunks, only he did not see what the skunks had done to be compared with them. And now they might go﻿—*quick*!

Beale at this point came out with the shotgun, and the meeting dispersed without the usual vote of thanks.

---

===Chinese===
“你们——你们——你们——你们这群恶棍！混账！蛆虫！莠草！”

我总喜欢照那一刻的模样，去忆想斯坦利·费瑟斯通豪·厄克里奇。多年的交情里，也有几个时候，他的行径叫我不敢恭维。我也不时在他身上看出些瑕疵。可这一回，他发挥得淋漓尽致。他雄辩滔滔。他慑服了全场。离他讲完还早得很，我便庆幸他当机立断把厄克里奇太太打发进了屋；而比尔正一字不漏地听着，眼里有一种我从未见过的神采——崇敬，近乎敬畏，门徒聆听宗师时的神采。

他把讥诮泼向听众，众人缩成一团。他把痛骂掷向听众，众人蔫了下去。种种古怪的咒骂——在运牛船上的古怪人堆里学来的，或从布宜诺斯艾利斯和旧金山的码头上拾掇的——汩汩流进他演讲的洪流。他说，这事够狠的，他对天起誓，一位绅士——一位向来用大笔订单等等刺激本地贸易、功勋卓著的绅士——竟不能上伦敦去办事五分钟，自家的地盘就叫一伙家伙掀了个底朝天——那伙家伙，形容词出自运牛船，名词出自旧金山——个个行事，仿佛这整个地名都得用布宜诺斯艾利斯话来讲的地方，通通归了他们似的。他本打算厚待他们。他本有意继续给他们送生意、扩张他们的买卖。可经过这一场之后他还肯吗？做梦！等太阳一升起新的一天开始，他们那点寒碜账目分文不少一律付清，同他的往来一刀两断。往后他多半还要就擅入私地、大规模毁坏财物两项，对他们提起诉讼；倘若他们不至于统统把那作孽的日子消磨在某个混账牢房里，就该算自家祖上积德；倘若他们不在两眨眼功夫之内滚得没影，他倒要看看比尔的猎枪能派上什么用场。（比尔此时面露得色，退下去取那件家伙。）他腻透了他们。他们是混账。管他们叫人类，都算谄媚得肉麻。他本想骂他们臭鼬，又觉得不知臭鼬造了什么孽，要蒙受这种比附。现在他们可以滚了——*快滚*！

比尔这时提着猎枪出来，大会就此散场，也没照例来上一段感谢辞。

---

===Original===
We were quiet at the farm that night. Ukridge sat like Marius among the ruins of Carthage, and refused to speak. Eventually he took Bob with him and went for a walk.

Half an hour later I, too, wearied of the scene of desolation. My errant steps took me in the direction of the sea. As I approached, I was aware of a figure standing in the moonlight, gazing silently out over the waters. Beside the figure was a dog.

The dark moments of optimistic minds are sacred, and I would no more have ventured to break in on Ukridge’s thoughts at that moment than, if I had been a general in the Grand Army, I would have opened conversation with Napoleon during the retreat from Moscow. I was withdrawing as softly as I could, when my foot grated on the shingle. Ukridge turned.

“Hullo, Garny.”

“Hullo, old man.” I murmured in a death-bedside voice.

===Chinese===
那天夜里，农场一片沉寂。厄克里奇坐在那里，活像面对迦太基废墟的马略，一言不发。末了，他带上博布，散步去了。

半个钟头后，我也看腻了这片凄凉。信步所之，我朝大海的方向走去。走近时，我瞥见月光下立着一条人影，正默默眺望海面。人影旁边是一条狗。

乐观心灵的阴郁时刻是神圣的。此时贸然搅扰厄克里奇的思绪，就好比假如我是大军团里的一名将军，绝不会在撤离莫斯科途中上前同拿破仑搭话。我正蹑手蹑脚地退走，脚下却在砾石上一硌。厄克里奇回过头来。

“喂，加尼。”

“喂，老兄。”我用临终探视般的嗓音嗫嚅道。

===Original===
He came towards me, Bob trotting at his heels: and, as he came, I saw with astonishment that his mien was calm, even cheerful. I should have known my Ukridge better than to be astonished. You cannot keep a good man down, and already Stanley Featherstonehaugh Ukridge was himself again. His eyes sparkled buoyantly behind their pince-nez.

“Garny, old horse, I’ve been thinking, laddie! I’ve got an idea! The idea of a lifetime. The best ever, ’pon my Sam! I’m going to start a duck farm!”

“A duck farm!”

“A duck farm, laddie! And run it without water. My theory is, you see, that ducks get thin by taking exercise and swimming about all over the place, so that, if you kept them always on land, they’d get jolly fat in about half the time﻿—and no trouble and expense. See? What? Not a flaw in it, old horse! I’ve thought the whole thing out.” He took my arm affectionately. “Now, listen. We’ll say that the profits of the first year at a conservative estimate﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===
他朝我走来，博布小跑着跟在脚跟。他一路走近，我惊讶地发现他神色平静，甚至称得上欢快。我本该更了解我的厄克里奇，不至于大惊小怪。好汉子是压不垮的，斯坦利·费瑟斯通豪·厄克里奇已经又是他自己了。夹鼻眼镜后面，他的双眼轻盈地闪着光。

“加尼老马，我一直在琢磨呢，小老弟！我想到一个主意！一辈子最好的主意。空前绝后，我对天起誓！我要办一个鸭子农场！”

“鸭子农场！”

“鸭子农场，小老弟！而且不用水。你听我讲，我的理论是：鸭子之所以瘦，就瘦在锻炼和满世界凫水上。所以，把它们一直圈在旱地上，膘就肥得快一倍——还省心省钱。懂了吗？怎么样？一个破绽也没有，老马！整个事儿我全盘想通了。”他亲热地挽住我的胳膊。“来，听着。咱们就算第一年的利润，按保守估计……”

## XIX Asking Papa / 十九 征询岳父

===Original===
Reviewing the matter later, I could see that I made one or two blunders in my conduct of the campaign to win over Professor Derrick. In the first place, I made a bad choice of time and place. At the moment this did not strike me. It is a simple matter, I reflected, for a man to pass another by haughtily and without recognition, when they meet on dry land; but, when the said man, being it should be remembered, an indifferent swimmer, is accosted in the water and out of his depth, the feat becomes a hard one. It seemed to me that I should have a better chance with the professor in the water than out of it.

My second mistake﻿—and this was brought home to me almost immediately﻿—was in bringing Ukridge along. Not that I really brought him along; it was rather a case of being unable to shake him off. When he met me on the gravel outside the house at a quarter to eight on the following morning, clad in a dingy mackintosh which, swinging open, revealed a purple bathing-suit, I confess that my heart sank. Unfortunately, all my efforts to dissuade him from accompanying me were attributed by him to a pardonable nervousness﻿—or, as he put it, to the needle.

“Buck up, laddie!” he roared encouragingly. “I had anticipated this. Something seemed to tell me that your nerve would go when it came to the point. You’re deuced lucky, old horse, to have a man like me at your side. Why, if you were alone, you wouldn’t have a word to say for yourself. You’d just gape at the man and yammer. But I’m with you, laddie, I’m with you. If your flow of conversation dries up, count on me to keep the thing going.”

And so it came about that, having reached the Cob and spying in the distance the grey head of the professor bobbing about on the face of the waters, we dived in and swam rapidly towards him.
