Strange how one reaches an epoch-making moment in one’s life without recognising it. If I had refused that invitation, I would not have﻿—at any rate, I would have missed a remarkable experience. It is not given to everyone to see Stanley Featherstonehaugh Ukridge manage a chicken farm.

“I was thinking of going somewhere where I could get some golf,” I said undecidedly.

“Combe Regis is just the place for you, then. Perfect hotbed of golf. Full of the finest players. Can’t throw a brick without hitting an amateur champion. Grand links at the top of the hill not half a mile from the farm. Bring your clubs. You’ll be able to play in the afternoons. Get through serious work by lunch time.”

===Chinese===
「听见没有？十封。十封讨鸡的信。这才是成功之道。闯劲加进取心。」

「有六封没回音，亲爱的斯坦利，其余的都回绝了。」

「无关紧要，」厄克里奇大手一挥。「那不要紧。要点在于信写了。这说明咱们扎实、实干。那么，明天你能把东西备齐吗，加尼老伙计？」

奇也哉，人生的划时代时刻临头，人竟浑然不觉。我当日若回绝了那分邀请，我就不会——至少，我会错过一段非凡的经历。眼见斯坦利·费瑟斯通豪·厄克里奇经营一座养鸡场，这可不是人人有份的眼福。

「我本想去个能打打高尔夫的地方。」我游移不定地说。

「那库姆里吉斯正对你胃口。高尔夫的温床。遍地一流高手。随手扔块砖，砸不着一个业余冠军才怪。山顶上有好球场，离农场不到半英里。带上你的球杆。下午尽可打球。午饭前把正经事干完。」

===Original===
“You know,” I said, “I am absolutely inexperienced as regards fowls. I just know enough to help myself to bread sauce when I see one, but no more.”

“Excellent! You’re just the man. You will bring to the work a mind unclouded by theories. You will act solely by the light of your intelligence. And you’ve got lots of that. That novel of yours showed the most extraordinary intelligence﻿—at least as far as that blighter at the bookstall would let me read. I wouldn’t have a professional chicken farmer about the place if he paid to come. If he applied to me, I should simply send him away. Natural intelligence is what we want. Then we can rely on you?”

“Very well,” I said slowly. “It’s very kind of you to ask me.”

“Business, laddie, pure business. Very well, then. We shall catch the eleven-twenty at Waterloo. Don’t miss it. Look out for me on the platform. If I see you first, I’ll shout.”

===Chinese===
「要知道，」我说，「论养鸡我是十足的外行。我懂的那一点儿，只够在餐桌上见到鸡时给自己舀一勺面包酱，再多就没有了。」

「妙极！你正是那个人。你来做这份工作，带来的是一副不被理论遮蔽的头脑。你将只凭你智力的光辉行事。而你的智力绰绰有余。你那部小说显出了非凡的智力——至少，是书摊上那个家伙容我读到的部分。职业养鸡人若肯倒贴钱来我这儿，我也不要。他若来求职，我干脆打发他走。咱们要的是天生的智力。那么，能指望你了？」

「好吧，」我缓缓地说。「承蒙相邀，实在太客气了。」

「生意，小老弟，纯粹的生意。那么说定了。咱们赶滑铁卢的十一点二十分那班。别误了。到月台上找我。我要是先瞧见你，就喊你。」

## VI Mr. Garnet’s Narrative﻿—Has to Do with a Reunion / 六 加尼特的自述——记一场重逢

===Original===
The day was Thursday, the date July the twenty-second. We had been chicken-farmers for a whole week, and things were beginning to settle down to a certain extent. The coops were finished. They were not masterpieces, and I have seen chickens pause before them in deep thought, as who should say, “Now what?” but they were coops within the meaning of the Act, and we induced hens to become tenants.

The hardest work had been the fixing of the wire-netting. This was the department of the Hired Man and myself, Ukridge holding himself proudly aloof. While Beale and I worked ourselves to a fever in the sun, the senior partner of the firm sat on a deck-chair in the shade, offering not unkindly criticism and advice and from time to time abusing his creditors, who were numerous. For we had hardly been in residence a day before he began to order in a vast supply of necessary and unnecessary things, all on credit. Some he got from the village, others from neighbouring towns. Axminster he laid heavily under contribution. He even went as far afield as Dorchester. He had a persuasive way with him, and the tradesmen seemed to treat him like a favourite son. The things began to pour in from all sides﻿—groceries, whisky, a piano, a gramophone, pictures. Also cigars in great profusion. He was not one of those men who want but little here below.

As regards the financial side of these transactions, his method was simple and masterly. If a tradesman suggested that a small cheque on account would not be taken amiss, as one or two sordid fellows did, he became pathetic.

“Confound it, sir,” he would say with tears in his voice, laying a hand on the man’s shoulder in a wounded way, “it’s a trifle hard, when a gentleman comes to settle in your neighbourhood, that you should dun him for money before he has got the preliminary expenses about the house off his back.” This sounded well, and suggested the disbursement of huge sums for rent. The fact that the house had been lent him rent free was kept with some care in the background. Having weakened the man with pathos, he would strike a sterner note. “A little more of this,” he would go on, “and I’ll close my account. Why, damme, in all my experience I’ve never heard anything like it!” Upon which the man would apologise, and go away, forgiven, with a large order for more goods.

By these statesmanlike methods he had certainly made the place very comfortable. I suppose we all realised that the things would have to be paid for some day, but the thought did not worry us.

===Chinese===
这天是星期四，日期是七月二十二日。我们当养鸡场主已满一个星期，诸事开始在一定程度上安定下来。鸡笼完工了。它们算不得杰作，我也见过鸡在笼前驻足深思，仿佛在问「这算怎么回事？」但它们毕竟是法条意义上的鸡笼，我们到底也劝得母鸡们做了租客。

最苦的活是固定铁丝网。这是那位雇仆和我的分内事，厄克里奇骄傲地置身事外。比尔和我在太阳底下干得发起烧来，商号的大老板却坐在荫凉里的帆布折椅上，不无情意地指点评议，间或骂一骂他的债主——债主为数众多。因为我们入住不到一天，他就开始大批订购必需与非必需的物资，一概赊账。有的从村里买，有的从邻镇买。他把阿克斯明斯特（Axminster）狠狠搜刮了一番，甚至远征到多尔切斯特（Dorchester）。他自有一套动人的本领，商人们待他竟像宠儿。物资开始从四面八方滚滚而来——杂货、威士忌、一架钢琴、一架留声机、图画，还有大量的雪茄。他可不是那种「但求于此、别无所欲」的人。

至于这些交易的银钱一面，他的办法简单而高明。倘有商人暗示，先开一张小额支票付部分账款不会见怪——有一两个庸俗家伙就这么干过——他便作出可怜之态。

「岂有此理，先生，」他会含着泪音说，一只手受伤似的搭在人家肩上，「一位绅士到贵地定居，安家的头一笔开销还没打发利索，您就上门催起账来，这未免叫人寒心。」这话听着体面，暗示已为房租付出巨款。而房子本是人家白借给他、分文租金不取的——这一节他谨慎地按在幕后不提。把对方用悲情削弱之后，他便转出严厉的调门。「再这般，」他接下去说，「我就销户。嚯，活了这半世，还没见过这样的！」于是对方赔不是，走的时候得到了原谅，还带走一大笔新订单。

靠这些政治家风的手腕，他确实把这份人家置办得舒舒服服。我想我们全都明白，这些东西早晚要付钱，但这念头并不搅扰我们。

===Original===
“Pay?” bellowed Ukridge on the only occasion when I ventured to bring up the unpleasant topic, “of course we shall pay. Why not? I don’t like to see this fainthearted spirit in you, old horse. The money isn’t coming in yet, I admit, but we must give it time. Soon we shall be turning over hundreds a week, hundreds! I’m in touch with all the big places﻿—Whiteley’s, Harrod’s, all the nibs. Here I am, I said to them, with a large chicken farm with all the modern improvements. You want eggs, old horses, I said: I supply them. I will let you have so many hundred eggs a week, I said; what will you give for them? Well, I’ll admit their terms did not come up to my expectations altogether, but we must not sneer at small prices at first.

“When we get a connection, we shall be able to name our terms. It stands to reason, laddie. Have you ever seen a man, woman, or child who wasn’t eating an egg or just going to eat an egg or just coming away from eating an egg? I tell you, the good old egg is the foundation of daily life. Stop the first man you meet in the street and ask him which he’d sooner lose, his egg or his wife, and see what he says! We’re on to a good thing Garny, my boy. Pass the whisky!”

The upshot of it was that the firms mentioned supplied us with a quantity of goods, agreeing to receive phantom eggs in exchange. This satisfied Ukridge. He had a faith in the laying power of his hens which would have flattered them if they could have known it. It might also have stimulated their efforts in that direction, which up to date were feeble.

It was now, as I have said, Thursday, the twenty-second of July﻿—a glorious, sunny morning, of the kind which Providence sends occasionally, simply in order to allow the honest smoker to take his after-breakfast pipe under ideal conditions. These are the pipes to which a man looks back in after years with a feeling of wistful reverence, pipes smoked in perfect tranquility, mind and body alike at rest. It is over pipes like these that we dream our dreams, and fashion our masterpieces.

My pipe was behaving like the ideal pipe; and, as I strolled spaciously about the lawn, my novel was growing nobly. I had neglected my literary work for the past week, owing to the insistent claims of the fowls. I am not one of those men whose minds work in placid independence of the conditions of life. But I was making up for lost time now. With each blue cloud that left my lips and hung in the still air above me, striking scenes and freshets of sparkling dialogue rushed through my brain. Another uninterrupted half hour, and I have no doubt that I should have completed the framework of a novel which would have placed me in that select band of authors who have no Christian names. Another half hour, and posterity would have known me as “Garnet.”

===Chinese===
「付钱？」在我仅有一次斗胆提起这桩不愉快话题时，厄克里奇吼道，「我们当然要付。为什么不？我不爱看你这份畏怯气，老伙计。钱眼下还没进来，我承认，可咱们得给它时间。不久咱们就要每星期周转几百镑，几百！我同各家大号都有联络——怀特利、哈罗德，各位巨头。我在这儿呢，我跟他们说，我有一座带全套现代改良设备的养鸡大场。你们要蛋，老伙计们，我说：我供应。我每礼拜让你们几百几百地拿蛋，我说；你们出什么价？好吧，我承认他们的条件没完全达到我的期望，可起初咱们不能小看小价钱。」

「等有了主顾，咱们就能自己开价了。天经地义，小老弟。你可曾见过哪个男人、女人或孩子，不是正在吃蛋、刚要吃蛋、就是刚吃完蛋？我告诉你，好老蛋是日常生活的基石。上街拦住头一个遇见的人，问问他宁可丢哪一样，他的蛋还是他的老婆，看他怎么说！咱们摸着好买卖了，加尼我的小子。把威士忌递过来！」

结果是，上述各家商号供给了我们一大批货，并同意收幻影蛋作抵。这叫厄克里奇满意了。他对他的母鸡们的产蛋能力怀着一种信心，母鸡们若有知，必要受宠若惊。这信心兴许还能激励她们朝那方面加把劲——迄今为止，她们的努力可怜得很。

这时，如我所说，是星期四，七月二十二日——一个晴朗明媚的早晨。上苍偶尔降下这种日子，专为让老实的吸烟人在理想条件下抽他早饭后的那斗烟。人到了晚年，正是带着怅惘的敬意回望这些烟斗的：在完美的宁静中抽过的烟，身心俱憩。正是在这样的烟斗上，我们做我们的梦，造我们的杰作。

我的烟斗表现得像理想中的烟斗；我在草地上大踱方步，我的小说也在茁壮生长。过去一星期我荒废了文事，因为鸡们催逼不休。我不是那种心智能安详独立于生活条件之外的人。但此刻我正在追回失去的时间。随着每一朵离开我唇边、悬在我头顶静止空气里的蓝烟，鲜明的场面与妙语连珠的对话激流从我脑中奔涌而过。再不受打扰地过半个钟头，我毫无疑问就能搭好一部小说的框架，让我跻身于那一小批不用教名的作家之列。再半个钟头，后世就将只知我叫「加尼特」。

===Original===
But it was not to be.

“Stop her! Catch her, Garny, old horse!”

I had wandered into the paddock at the moment. I looked up. Coming towards me at her best pace was a small hen. I recognised her immediately. It was the disagreeable, sardonic-looking bird which Ukridge, on the strength of an alleged similarity of profile to his wife’s nearest relative, had christened Aunt Elizabeth. A Bolshevist hen, always at the bottom of any disturbance in the fowl-run, a bird which ate its head off daily at our expense and bit the hands which fed it by resolutely declining to lay a single egg. Behind this fowl ran Bob, doing, as usual, the thing that he ought not to have done. Bob’s wrong-headedness in the matter of our hens was a constant source of inconvenience. From the first, he had seemed to regard the laying-in of our stock purely in the nature of a tribute to his sporting tastes. He had a fixed idea that he was a hunting dog and that, recognising this, we had very decently provided him with the material for the chase.

Behind Bob came Ukridge. But a glance was enough to tell me that he was a negligible factor in the pursuit. He was not built for speed. Already the pace had proved too much for him, and he had appointed me his deputy, with full powers to act.

“After her, Garny, old horse! Valuable bird! Mustn’t be lost!”

===Chinese===
但事与愿违。

「拦住她！抓住她，加尼老伙计！」

我当时正踱进了围场。我抬头一看。一只小母鸡正以她的极速朝我而来。我立刻认出了她。正是那只面相刻薄、带着讥诮神气的鸟——厄克里奇凭她侧影据称与他太太最近的一位亲戚相似，给她命名伊丽莎白姨妈。一只布尔什维克母鸡，鸡场里任何骚乱背后必有她；一只天天吃我们的饭把膘吃肥、还反咬喂养她的手——坚决拒绝下一只蛋——的鸟。博布在这只鸡后面追，照例干着他不该干的事。博布对我们母鸡的错误认识，是个经久不衰的麻烦源。打一开始，他就似乎把我们进这批货纯粹看作对他运动趣味的一种敬意。他有个定见：自己是条猎犬，而我们既然识得这一点，便十分体面地为他备下了狩猎的素材。

博布后面是厄克里奇。但一眼便知，在这场追逐里他是个可忽略的因素。他那身架不是为速度造的。眼下的配速早已叫他吃不消，他已委任我为他全权代表。

「追她，加尼老伙计！贵重的鸟！不能丢！」

===Original===
When not in a catalepsy of literary composition, I am essentially the man of action. I laid aside my novel for future reference, and we passed out of the paddock in the following order. First, Aunt Elizabeth, as fresh as paint, going well. Next, Bob, panting and obviously doubtful of his powers of staying the distance. Lastly, myself, determined, but wishing I were five years younger.

After the first field Bob, like the dilettante and unstable dog he was, gave it up, and sauntered off to scratch at a rabbit-hole with an insufferable air of suggesting that that was what he had come out for all the time. I continued to pound along doggedly. I was grimly resolute. I had caught Aunt Elizabeth’s eye as she passed me, and the contempt in it had cut me to the quick. This bird despised me. I am not a violent or a quick-tempered man, but I have my self-respect. I will not be sneered at by hens. All the abstract desire for Fame which had filled my mind five minutes before was concentrated now on the task of capturing this supercilious bird.

We had been travelling down hill all this time, but at this point we crossed a road and the ground began to rise. I was in that painful condition which occurs when one has lost one’s first wind and has not yet got one’s second. I was hotter than I had ever been in my life.

Whether Aunt Elizabeth, too, was beginning to feel the effects of her run, or whether she did it out of the pure effrontery of her warped and unpleasant nature, I do not know; but she now slowed down to walk, and even began to peck in a tentative manner at the grass. Her behaviour infuriated me. I felt that I was being treated as a cipher. I vowed that this bird should realise yet, even if, as seemed probable, I burst in the process, that it was no light matter to be pursued by J. Garnet, author of *The Manœuvres of Arthur*, etc., a man of whose work so capable a judge as the Peebles *Advertiser* had said “Shows promise.”

A judicious increase of pace brought me within a yard or two of my quarry. But Aunt Elizabeth, apparently distrait, had the situation well in hand. She darted from me with an amused chuckle, and moved off rapidly again up the hill.

===Chinese===
一旦不处于文学创作的僵直状态，我骨子里就是个行动家。我把小说搁在一旁以备将来参考，我们便以下列次序出了围场。头名，伊丽莎白姨妈，鲜艳如画，跑得正顺。次名，博布，气喘吁吁，显然对续航力没了把握。末名，在下，意志坚定，但恨不得自己年轻五岁。

跑过头一块地，博布便显出他那条半吊子狗没长性的本相，就此罢手，踱到一边去扒一个兔子洞，带着一股叫人无法容忍的神气，仿佛暗示他出来原本就是为的这个。我继续闷头猛追。我铁了心。她擦过我身边时，我捉住了伊丽莎白姨妈的眼风，那眼中的轻蔑直扎我的心窝。这只鸟瞧不起我。我不是个暴烈或急性的人，但我有我的自尊。我不能受母鸡的讥笑。五分钟前满脑子那套对声名的抽象渴望，此刻全数集中到擒获这只傲慢的鸟这一桩事上。

一路都是下坡，到这会儿我们过了条大路，地势开始抬升。我正处于那种痛苦的境地：第一口气已竭，第二口气未至。我这辈子从没这么热过。

不知是伊丽莎白姨妈也开始觉出奔跑的效应，还是她纯粹出于那副扭曲讨嫌天性的厚颜无耻，我不得而知；总之她这时放缓成走，甚至试着往草里啄了几口。她的行止激怒了我。我感到自己被当作一个零蛋对待。我发誓，这只鸟将来必有明白的一日——哪怕过程如所预示的那样，我会半路爆血管——被 J. 加尼特追捕不是小事，此人乃《阿瑟的谋略》等的作者，其作品曾蒙皮布尔斯《广告人报》（the Peebles *Advertiser*）这样一位有分量的评判者评曰「大有可为」。

一番审慎的加速，把我带到离猎物一两码之内。但伊丽莎白姨妈貌似心不在焉，实则大局在握。她一声得意的咯笑，从我身边窜出，重新快速向坡上移动。

===Original===
I followed, but there was that within me that told me I had shot my bolt. The sun blazed down, concentrating its rays on my back to the exclusion of the surrounding scenery. It seemed to follow me about like a limelight.

We had reached level ground. Aunt Elizabeth had again slowed to a walk, and I was capable of no better pace. Very gradually I closed in. There was a high boxwood hedge in front of us; and, just as I came close enough once more to stake my all on a single grab, Aunt Elizabeth, with another of her sardonic chuckles, dived in head-foremost and struggled through in the mysterious way in which birds do get through hedges. The sound of her faint spinster-like snigger came to me as I stood panting, and roused me like a bugle. The next moment I too had plunged into the hedge.

I was in the middle of it, very hot, tired, and dirty, when from the other side I heard a sudden shout of “Mark over! Bird to the right!” and the next moment I found myself emerging with a black face and tottering knees on the gravel path of a private garden. Beyond the path was a croquet lawn, and on this lawn I perceived, as through a glass darkly, three figures. The mist cleared from my eyes, and I recognised two of them.

One was the middle-aged Irishman who had travelled down with us in the train. The other was his blue-eyed daughter.

The third member of the party was a man, a stranger to me. By some miracle of adroitness he had captured Aunt Elizabeth, and was holding her in spite of her protests in a workmanlike manner behind the wings.

===Chinese===
我跟了上去，但心里有个声音告诉我：我已经竭尽全力了。骄阳当头直射，光线全集中在我背上，无暇顾及四周风景。它像一盏聚光灯，我走到哪儿跟到哪儿。

我们到了平地。伊丽莎白姨妈又放缓成走，我也拿不出更快的步子了。我一点一点地收拢包围。前面是一道高高的黄杨树篱；正当我又一次逼近得足以孤注一掷扑一把时，伊丽莎白姨妈又一声讥诮的咯笑，头朝前扎进树篱，以鸟类穿越树篱的那种神秘方式挣扎而过。我站在那儿喘气，听见她那声老处女式的窃笑，如军号般把我唤醒。下一刻我也一头扎进了树篱。

我正在树篱当中，浑身滚烫、疲惫、肮脏，忽听篱那边一声大喊：「看球！鸟朝右边去了！」随即我发现自己黑着脸、膝盖打颤，冒出到一座私家花园的砾石小径上。小径那边是一片槌球草坪，草坪上我如雾里看花般辨出三个人影。雾从我眼前散去，我认出了其中两位。

一位是火车上与我们同行的那个爱尔兰中年人。另一位是他的蓝眼睛女儿。

这伙人的第三位是个男人，于我全然陌生。他凭着某种奇迹般的敏捷，已经擒获了伊丽莎白姨妈，正不顾她的抗议，以熟练的手法扣住她的双翼。

## XVII Of a Sentimental Nature / 十七 一段柔情

===Original===
She was wearing a Panama, and she carried a sketching-block and campstool.

“Good evening,” I said.

“Good evening,” said she.

It is curious how different the same words can sound, when spoken by different people. My “good evening” might have been that of a man with a particularly guilty conscience caught in the act of doing something more than usually ignoble. She spoke like a rather offended angel.

“It’s a lovely evening,” I went on pluckily.

“Very.”

===Chinese===
她戴着一顶巴拿马草帽，携着写生簿和折叠小凳。

「晚上好。」我说。

「晚上好。」她说。

同样的话出自不同人之口，听来竟会如此不同，真是奇事。我那句「晚上好」，说得像个良心大坏的家伙，正干着一桩格外下流的勾当时被当场拿住。她那一句，却像一位微嗔的天使。

「今儿晚上真美。」我抖擞精神接着说。

「很美。」

===Original===
“The sunset!”

“Yes.”

“Er﻿—”

She raised a pair of blue eyes, devoid of all expression save a faint suggestion of surprise, and gazed through me for a moment at some object a couple of thousand miles away, and lowered them again, leaving me with a vague feeling that there was something wrong with my personal appearance.

Very calmly she moved to the edge of the cliff, arranged her campstool, and sat down. Neither of us spoke a word. I watched her while she filled a little mug with water from a little bottle, opened her paintbox, selected a brush, and placed her sketching-block in position.

She began to paint.

===Chinese===
「这晚霞！」

「是啊。」

「呃——」

她抬起一双蓝眼睛，除了淡淡一点惊讶之外毫无表情，隔着我在两千里外的什么物件上注视了片刻，又垂了下去，留给我一种隐隐的感觉：我的仪表哪里出了毛病。

她十分镇定地走到崖边，安好折叠凳，坐下。我们谁也没说话。我看着她用小瓶里的水注满小杯，打开颜料盒，挑出一支笔，把写生簿摆正位置。

她开始作画。

===Original===
Now, by all the laws of good taste, I should before this have made a dignified exit. It was plain that I was not regarded as an essential ornament of this portion of the Ware Cliff. By now, if I had been the Perfect Gentleman, I ought to have been a quarter of a mile away.

But there is a definite limit to what a man can do. I remained.

The sinking sun flung a carpet of gold across the sea. Phyllis’ hair was tinged with it. Little waves tumbled lazily on the beach below. Except for the song of a distant blackbird, running through its repertoire before retiring for the night, everything was silent.

She sat there, dipping and painting and dipping again, with never a word for me﻿—standing patiently and humbly behind her.

“Miss Derrick,” I said.

She half turned her head.

===Chinese===
话说回来，按一切雅趣的法则，我早该在此时体面地撤退了。显然，我不被看作这段韦尔崖（Ware Cliff）风景上必不可少的点缀。倘若我是位完美绅士，此刻早该退到四分之一英里外去了。

但人的能耐总有个限度。我留了下来。

沉落的太阳往海上铺了一幅金毯。菲莉斯的头发染上了那金色。小浪懒懒地翻滚在下面的海滩上。除了一只远处的乌鸫，在就寝前把自己的曲目预演一遍，万籁俱寂。

她坐在那里，蘸色，涂抹，再蘸色，没有一句话赏给我——我谦卑而耐心地立在她身后。

「德里克小姐。」我说。

她侧了侧头。

===Original===
“Yes.”

“Why won’t you speak to me?” I said.

“I don’t understand you.”

“Why won’t you speak to me?”

“I think you know, Mr. Garnet.”

“It is because of that boat accident?”

===Chinese===
「嗯。」

「您为什么不肯同我说话？」我说。

「我不明白您的意思。」

「您为什么不肯同我说话？」

「我想您是知道的，加尼特先生。」

「是因为那回船上的事故吗？」

===Original===
“Accident!”

“Episode,” I amended.

She went on painting in silence. From where I stood I could see her profile. Her chin was tilted. Her expression was determined.

“Is it?” I said.

“Need we discuss it?”

“Not if you do not wish it.”

===Chinese===
「事故！」

「『事件』，」我修正道。

她继续默默作画。从我站的地方望得见她的侧影。她的下巴微微扬着。神情坚决。

「是吗？」我说。

「咱们非谈它不可吗？」

「您若不愿，就不谈。」

===Original===
I paused.

“But,” I added, “I should have liked a chance to defend myself.﻿ ﻿… What glorious sunsets there have been these last few days. I believe we shall have this sort of weather for another month.”

“I should not have thought that possible.”

“The glass is going up,” I said.

“I was not talking about the weather.”

“It was dull of me to introduce such a worn-out topic.”

===Chinese===
我顿了顿。

「可是，」我补充道，「我本想有个为自己申辩的机会。……这几天的晚霞多壮丽啊。我看这样的天气还能再持续一个月。」

「我可没想到有这个可能。」

「气压计在往上走。」我说。

「我说的不是天气。」

「我提起这么个用滥了的话题，真是迟钝。」

===Original===
“You said you could defend yourself.”

“I said I should like the chance to do so.”

“You have it.”

“That’s very kind of you. Thank you.”

“Is there any reason for gratitude?”

“Every reason.”

===Chinese===
「您方才说，您能为自己申辩。」

「我说的是，我希望能有机会这样做。」

「现在您有了。」

「您太好了。谢谢。」

「有什么可谢的吗？」

「样样可谢。」

===Original===
“Go on, Mr. Garnet. I can listen while I paint. But please sit down. I don’t like being talked to from a height.”

I sat down on the grass in front of her, feeling as I did so that the change of position in a manner clipped my wings. It is difficult to speak movingly while sitting on the ground. Instinctively I avoided eloquence. Standing up, I might have been pathetic and pleading. Sitting down, I was compelled to be matter-of-fact.

“You remember, of course, the night you and Professor Derrick dined with us? When I say dined, I use the word in a loose sense.”

For a moment I thought she was going to smile. We were both thinking of Edwin. But it was only for a moment, and then her face grew cold once more, and the chin resumed its angle of determination.

“Yes,” she said.

“You remember the unfortunate ending of the festivities?”

===Chinese===
「说吧，加尼特先生。我可以边画边听。不过请坐下。我不喜欢有人居高临下地跟我说话。」

我在她面前的草地上坐下，一边觉得这一换姿势多少剪了我的翅膀。坐在地上，很难说得动人。我本能地回避了雄辩。站着，我本可以哀婉恳求；坐着，我只得就事论事。

「您当然记得，您和德里克教授来我们家吃晚饭的那晚？我说吃晚饭，这词用得宽泛。」

有一瞬间我以为她要笑了。我们都想起了埃德温。但只有一瞬间，随即她的脸又冷下来，下巴恢复了坚决的角度。

「记得。」她说。

「您记得那场欢宴的不幸收场吧？」

===Original===
“Well?”

“If you recall that at all clearly, you will also remember that the fault was not mine, but Ukridge’s.”

“Well?”

“It was his behaviour that annoyed Professor Derrick. The position, then, was this, that I was to be cut off from the pleasantest friendship I had ever formed﻿—”

I stopped for a moment. She bent a little lower over her easel, but remained silent.

“﻿—Simply through the tactlessness of a prize idiot.”

===Chinese===
「怎么样？」

「您若还清楚记得，也该记得错不在我，而在厄克里奇。」

「怎么样？」

「惹恼德里克教授的，是他的行止。于是局面就成了这样：我平生结下的最愉快的友谊，就要被斩断——」

我停了停。她朝画架俯得更低了些，但没有作声。

「——而这一刀，纯粹因为一个头号白痴的不识进退。」

===Original===
“I like Mr. Ukridge.”

“I like him, too. But I can’t pretend that he is anything but an idiot at times.”

“Well?”

“I naturally wished to mend matters. It occurred to me that an excellent way would be by doing your father a service. It was seeing him fishing that put the idea of a boat-accident into my head. I hoped for a genuine boat-accident. But those things only happen when one does not want them. So I determined to engineer one.”

“You didn’t think of the shock to my father.”

“I did. It worried me very much.”

===Chinese===
「我喜欢厄克里奇先生。」

「我也喜欢他。可我没法装作他有时不是个白痴。」

「怎么样？」

「我自然想把事情挽回。我想到一个极好的办法，就是替令尊效一次劳。正是看见他钓鱼，让我起了船祸的念头。我指望来一场货真价实的船祸。可那种事，偏偏只在人不想要它的时候发生。于是我决定亲手导演一场。」

「您没想到我父亲会受惊。」

「想到了。这叫我非常不安。」

===Original===
“But you upset him all the same.”

“Reluctantly.”

She looked up, and our eyes met. I could detect no trace of forgiveness in hers.

“You behaved abominably,” she said.

“I played a risky game, and I lost. And I shall now take the consequences. With luck I should have won. I did not have luck, and I am not going to grumble about it. But I am grateful to you for letting me explain. I should not have liked you to have gone on thinking that I played practical jokes on my friends. That is all I have to say, I think. It was kind of you to listen. Goodbye, Miss Derrick.”

I got up.

===Chinese===
「可您还是把他掀下了水。」

「很不愿意地。」

她抬起头，我们四目相对。她眼里觉察不出一丝宽恕。

「您的行为恶劣透了。」她说。

「我玩了一场冒险的赌局，输了。现在我认罚。运气好我本该赢的。我没交上好运，我也不抱怨。但我感激您容我解释。我不愿意您继续以为我拿朋友开实际的玩笑。我要说的就是这些了。承蒙垂听。再见，德里克小姐。」

我站起来。

===Original===
“Are you going?”

“Why not?”

“Please sit down again.”

“But you wish to be alone﻿—”

“Please sit down!”

There was a flush on the cheek turned towards me, and the chin was tilted higher.

===Chinese===
「您要走？」

「为什么不走？」

「请再坐下。」

「可您不是想一个人待着——」

「请坐下！」

她朝我这边的那半边脸颊上有一抹红晕，下巴扬得更高了。

===Original===
I sat down.

To westward the sky had changed to the hue of a bruised cherry. The sun had sunk below the horizon, and the sea looked cold and leaden. The blackbird had long since flown.

“I am glad you told me, Mr. Garnet.”

She dipped her brush in the water.

“Because I don’t like to think badly of﻿—people.”

She bent her head over her painting.

===Chinese===
我坐下了。

西边的天空已变成樱桃淤伤的颜色。太阳沉到地平线下，海面看上去又冷又灰。乌鸫早已飞走了。

「您把这话告诉了我，我很高兴，加尼特先生。」

她把画笔在水里蘸了蘸。

「因为我不愿意把人——往坏里想。」

她低头朝着她的画。

===Original===
“Though I still think you behaved very wrongly. And I am afraid my father will never forgive you for what you did.”

Her father! As if he counted.

“But you do?” I said eagerly.

“I think you are less to blame than I thought you were at first.”

“No more than that?”

“You can’t expect to escape all consequences. You did a very stupid thing.”

===Chinese===
「不过我仍旧认为您做得大错特错。而且我怕我父亲永远不会原谅您的所作所为。」

她父亲！倒像他算数似的。

「可您会原谅？」我急切地说。

「我认为您不像起初想的那么罪不可赦。」

「就到此为止？」

「您总不能指望逃脱一切后果吧。您干了一件非常愚蠢的事。」

===Original===
“I was tempted.”

The sky was a dull grey now. It was growing dusk. The grass on which I sat was wet with dew.

I stood up.

“Isn’t it getting a little dark for painting?” I said. “Are you sure you won’t catch cold? It’s very damp.”

“Perhaps it is. And it is late, too.”

She shut her paintbox, and emptied the little mug on to the grass.

===Chinese===
「我是受了诱惑。」

天空此刻是暗灰色了。暮色渐浓。我坐着的草已被露水打湿。

我站起身。

「这光线画画是不是太暗了点儿？」我说。「您真不会着凉吗？潮气很重。」

「也许是的。也不早了。」

她合上颜料盒，把小杯里的水泼在草上。

===Original===
“May I carry your things?” I said.

I think she hesitated, but only for a moment.

I possessed myself of the campstool, and we started on our homeward journey.

We were both silent. The spell of the quiet summer evening was on us.

===Chinese===
「让我替您拿东西吧？」我说。

我想她踌躇了一下，但只有一下。

我取过折叠凳，我们便踏上归程。

我们都没说话。夏日黄昏的静谧笼罩着我们。

===Original===
“ ‘And all the air a solemn stillness holds,’ ” she said softly. “I love this cliff, Mr. Garnet. It’s the most soothing place in the world.”

“I found it so this evening.”

She glanced at me quickly.

“You’re not looking well,” she said. “Are you sure you are not overworking yourself?”

===Chinese===
「『四野悄然，一片肃穆的沉静，』」她轻声说。「我爱这悬崖，加尼特先生。这是世界上最叫人心神安宁的地方。」

「今晚我是领教了。」

她飞快地瞥了我一眼。

「您气色不好，」她说。「您敢肯定不是劳累过度吗？」

===Original===
“No, it’s not that.”

Somehow we had stopped, as if by agreement, and were facing each other. There was a look in her eyes I had never seen there before. The twilight hung like a curtain between us and the world. We were alone together in a world of our own.

“It is because I had offended you,” I said.

She laughed a high, unnatural laugh.

“I have loved you ever since I first saw you,” I said doggedly.

===Chinese===
「不，不是那个。」

不知怎么，我们停住了，像商量好了似的，面对面站着。她眼中有一种我从未见过的神色。暮色像一道帷幕，垂在我们与世界之间。我们在一个自己的世界里，单独在一起。

「是因为我冒犯了您。」我说。

她高声笑了，笑得不自然。

「自从第一次见到您，我就一直爱着您。」我执拗地说。

## XX Scientific Golf / 二十 科学高尔夫

===Original===
People are continually writing to the papers﻿—or it may be one solitary enthusiast who writes under a number of pseudonyms﻿—on the subject of sport, and the overdoing of the same by the modern young man. I recall one letter in which “Efficiency” gave it as his opinion that if the Young Man played less golf and did more drill, he would be all the better for it. I propose to report my doings with the professor on the links at some length, in order to refute this absurd view. Everybody ought to play golf, and nobody can begin it too soon. There ought not to be a single able-bodied infant in the British Isles who has not foozled a drive. To take my case. Suppose I had employed in drilling the hours I had spent in learning to handle my clubs. I might have drilled before the professor by the week without softening his heart. I might have ported arms and grounded arms and presented arms, and generally behaved in the manner advocated by “Efficiency,” and what would have been the result? Indifference on his part, or﻿—and if I overdid the thing﻿—irritation. Whereas, by devoting a reasonable portion of my youth to learning the intricacies of golf I was enabled﻿ ﻿…

It happened in this way.

To me, as I stood with Ukridge in the fowl-run in the morning following my maritime conversation with the professor, regarding a hen that had posed before us, obviously with a view to inspection, there appeared a man carrying an envelope. Ukridge, who by this time saw, as Calverley almost said, “under every hat a dun,” and imagined that no envelope could contain anything but a small account, softly and silently vanished away, leaving me to interview the enemy.

“Mr. Garnet, sir?” said the foe.

I recognised him. He was Professor Derrick’s gardener.

I opened the envelope. No. Father’s blessings were absent. The letter was in the third person. Professor Derrick begged to inform Mr. Garnet that, by defeating Mr. Saul Potter, he had qualified for the final round of the Combe Regis Golf Tournament, in which, he understood, Mr. Garnet was to be his opponent. If it would be convenient for Mr. Garnet to play off the match on the present afternoon, Professor Derrick would be obliged if he would be at the Club House at half-past two. If this hour and day were unsuitable, would he kindly arrange others. The bearer would wait.

===Chinese===
人们不断写信给报纸——也可能只是一位孤身的热心人，用一堆笔名写——谈体育问题，谈现代青年从事体育之过度。我记得有一封信，「效率」（Efficiency）署名者发表意见说，青年男子若少打高尔夫、多出操，必将大有裨益。我打算相当详尽地报道我与教授在球场上的作为，以驳斥这荒谬的见解。人人都该打高尔夫，而且越早开始越好。英伦三岛上不该有一个肢体健全的婴儿不曾打出过一记臭球。以我为例。设想我把学握球杆的光阴都拿去出操，我也许能在教授面前操练上一星期也软不化他的心。我也许能持枪、放枪、举枪敬礼，全面按「效率」先生的主张行事——结果如何？换来他的漠然，或者——倘若我操练过了头——恼怒。而我呢，把青春里合理的一部分献给高尔夫的种种精微之后，我便得以……

事情是这样发生的。

我正同厄克里奇站在鸡场里——那是我与教授海上对话的次日早晨——端详一只在我们面前摆好姿势的母鸡，显然是等着验看，这时来了个捎信的，手里拿着一只信封。厄克里奇此时已是——如卡尔弗利（Calverley）几乎说过的——「每顶帽子底下都看见一个讨债的」，认定凡是信封装的只可能是一张小小的账单，便轻轻地、悄悄地消失不见，留我去会见敌人。

「加尼特先生？」来敌说。

我认出了他。是德里克教授的园丁。

我拆开信封。不。没有父亲的祝福。信是第三人称写的。德里克教授谨通知加尼特先生：由于战胜了索尔·波特先生（Mr. Saul Potter），他已取得库姆里吉斯高尔夫锦标赛决赛资格，而据他了解，加尼特先生将是他决赛的对手。加尼特先生若便于今日下午举行这场对决，敬请在两点半莅临会所。此时此日若有不便，烦请另择。来人恭候回音。

===Original===
The bearer did wait. He waited for half-an-hour, as I found it impossible to shift him, not caring to use violence on a man well stricken in years, without first plying him with drink. He absorbed more of our diminishing cask of beer than we could conveniently spare, and then trudged off with a note, beautifully written in the third person, in which Mr. Garnet, after numerous compliments and thanks, begged to inform Professor Derrick that he would be at the Club House at the hour mentioned.

“And,” I added﻿—to myself, not in the note﻿—“I will give him such a licking that he’ll brain himself with a cleek.”

For I was not pleased with the professor. I was conscious of a malicious joy at the prospect of snatching the prize from him. I knew he had set his heart on winning the tournament this year. To be runner-up two years in succession stimulates the desire for first place. It would be doubly bitter to him to be beaten by a newcomer, after the absence of his rival, the colonel, had awakened hope in him. And I knew I could do it. Even allowing for bad luck﻿—and I am never a very unlucky golfer﻿—I could rely almost with certainty on crushing the man.

“And I’ll do it,” I said to Bob, who had trotted up. I often make Bob the recipient of my confidences. He listens appreciatively, and never interrupts. And he never has grievances of his own. If there is one person I dislike, it is the man who tries to air his grievances when I wish to air mine.

“Bob,” I said, running his tail through my fingers, “listen to me, my old University chum, for I have matured a dark scheme. Don’t run away. You know you don’t really want to go and look at that chicken. Listen to me. If I am in form this afternoon, and I feel in my bones that I shall be, I shall nurse the professor. I shall play with him. Do you understand the principles of match play at golf, Robert? You score by holes, not strokes. There are eighteen holes. All right, how was *I* to know that you knew that without my telling you? Well, if you understand so much about the game, you will appreciate my dark scheme. I shall toy with the professor, Bob. I shall let him get ahead, and then catch him up. I shall go ahead myself, and let him catch me up. I shall race him neck and neck till the very end. Then, when his hair has turned white with the strain, and he’s lost a couple of stone in weight, and his eyes are starting out of his head, and he’s praying﻿—if he ever does pray﻿—to the Gods of Golf that he may be allowed to win, I shall go ahead and beat him by a hole. *I’ll* teach him, Robert. He shall taste of my despair, and learn by proof in some wild hour how much the wretched dare. And when it’s all over, and he’s torn all his hair out and smashed all his clubs, I shall go and commit suicide off the Cob. Because, you see, if I can’t marry Phyllis, I shan’t have any use for life.”

Bob wagged his tail cheerfully.

===Chinese===
来人果然恭候。他候了半个钟头，因为我发现没法打发他走，又不忍对一个上了年纪的人动粗——除非先灌他几杯酒。他把我们那桶日见其少的啤酒喝掉了不少，多到叫我们心疼，然后揣着一张便条跋涉而去。那便条用漂亮的第三人称写成：加尼特先生于连串敬语与谢忱之后，谨通知德里克教授，他将于所述时刻莅临会所。

「而且，」我补了一句——补给我自己，没写进便条——「我要把他打得一败涂地，叫他自己拿克里克铁杆敲破脑袋。」

因为我对他没有好感。一想到要从他手里夺走奖杯，我便意识到一阵恶意的欢喜。我知道他一心要赢今年的锦标赛。连着两年屈居第二，只会刺激人对第一名的渴望。他的劲敌上校缺席，又唤醒了他心中的希望，而败在一个新来者手里，于他是加倍苦涩。我也知道自己办得到。即便把坏运气算上——而我从来不算个很倒霉的球手——我几乎可以稳操胜券地碾碎这个人。

「我一定办到。」我对跑过来的博布说。我常拿博布当我倾吐心事的对象。他听得津津有味，从不打岔，也从不自己诉苦。世上若说有哪种人我最厌恶，就是那种在我要诉苦时偏要诉他的苦的人。

「博布，」我用手指捋着他的尾巴说，「听我说，我的老大学同窗，我想好了一个黑心计。别跑。你知道你其实不想去看那只鸡。听我说。今儿下午我若是状态好——我骨头缝里觉出必定好——我就要窝住教授。我要耍着他玩。你懂高尔夫比洞赛的原理吗，罗伯特？按洞计胜负，不按杆数。一共十八洞。好吧，我怎么*就*知道你不用我说就懂了？嗐，你既懂这么多，必能欣赏我的黑心计。我要戏耍教授，博布。我放他领先，再追上他。我自己领先，再放他追上。我跟他并辔争先，一直斗到最后。等他熬得头发白了、掉了两英石肉、眼珠子凸出眼眶、开始祷告——他若果真祷告的话——求高尔夫诸神许他获胜，我便一杆超出，赢他一洞。*我*要教训教训他，罗伯特。他要尝尝我绝望的滋味，并在某个狂乱的时辰以实证领教：不幸者敢企求多少。等一切收场，他拔光了头发、砸光了球杆，我就去科布防波堤投海自尽。因为你要明白，我若娶不成菲莉斯，这条命留着也就没用了。」

博布欢快地摇尾巴。

===Original===
“I mean it,” I said, rolling him on his back and punching him on the chest till his breathing became stertorous. “You don’t see the sense of it, I know. But then you’ve got none of the finer feelings. You’re a jolly good dog, Robert, but you’re a rank materialist. Bones and cheese and potatoes with gravy over them make you happy. You don’t know what it is to be in love. You’d better get right side up now, or you’ll have apoplexy.”

It has been my aim in the course of this narrative to extenuate nothing, nor set down aught in malice. Like the gentleman who played euchre with the Heathen Chinee, I state but facts. I do not, therefore, slur over my scheme for disturbing the professor’s peace of mind. I am not always good and noble. I am the hero of this story, but I have my off moments.

I felt ruthless towards the professor. I cannot plead ignorance of the golfer’s point of view as an excuse for my plottings. I knew that to one whose soul is in the game as the professor’s was, the agony of being just beaten in an important match exceeds in bitterness all other agonies. I knew that, if I scraped through by the smallest possible margin, his appetite would be destroyed, his sleep o’ nights broken. He would wake from fitful slumber moaning that if he had only used his iron instead of his mashie at the tenth, all would have been well; that, if he had putted more carefully on the seventh green, life would not be drear and blank; that a more judicious manipulation of his brassey throughout might have given him something to live for. All these things I knew.

And they did not touch me. I was adamant. The professor was waiting for me at the Club House, and greeted me with a cold and stately inclination of the head.

“Beautiful day for golf,” I observed in my gay, chatty manner. He bowed in silence.

“Very well,” I thought. “Wait. Just wait.”

===Chinese===
「我是认真的。」我说，把他翻了个四脚朝天，捶他的胸口，捶到他呼吸都打起呼噜。「我知道你看不出这里面的道理。可你本来也没有细腻的感情。你是条好狗，罗伯特，可你是个彻头彻尾的唯物主义者。骨头、奶酪、浇了肉汁的土豆就能叫你幸福。你不懂恋爱是什么滋味。趁早翻正了吧，不然你要中风。」

本篇叙事自始至终力求不增一词以饰非，不着一字以泄愤。正如那位同「异教徒中国佬」打尤克牌的君子，我陈述的只是事实。因此，我并不掩饰我扰乱教授心境的计谋。我并非永远高尚善良。我是本故事的主人公，但也有走火入魔的时刻。

我对教授毫不留情。我不能借口不懂球手的心理，来开脱我的阴谋。我知道，对一个像教授这样魂系于此道的人，一场重要比赛以毫厘之差落败，其苦涩超过其余一切痛苦。我知道，我若以最小之差险胜，他的胃口将败坏，夜里的睡眠将破碎。他会从断续的浅寐中醒来呻吟：第十洞要是用铁杆不用马希杆，一切都会好；第七洞果岭上要是推杆仔细些，人生就不至于这样灰暗空虚；全场要是把布拉西木杆调度得更审慎些，或许还能给他一点活下去的指望。这些我全知道。

而这些全打动不了我。我铁石心肠。教授在会所等我，以一个冷峻而庄严的颔首迎接我。

「打高尔夫的好天气。」我以轻快闲聊的口吻议论道。他默默鞠了一躬。

「很好，」我心想，「等着瞧。你就等着瞧吧。」

===Original===
“Miss Derrick is well, I hope?” I added, aloud.

That drew him. He started. His aspect became doubly forbidding.

“Miss Derrick is perfectly well, sir, I thank you.”

“And you? No bad effect, I hope, from your dip yesterday?”

“Mr. Garnet, I came here for golf, not conversation,” he said.

We made it so. I drove off from the first tee. It was a splendid drive. I should not say so if there were anyone else to say so for me. Modesty would forbid. But, as there is no one, I must repeat the statement. It was one of the best drives of my experience. The ball flashed through the air, took the bunker with a dozen feet to spare, and rolled on to the green. I had felt all along that I should be in form. Unless my opponent was equally above himself, he was a lost man. I could toy with him.

===Chinese===
「德里克小姐想必安好？」我又大声说。

这一句打中了他。他一震。面色加倍地拒人千里。

「德里克小姐十分康健，先生，谢谢您。」

「您呢？昨天那场落水，没留下什么不良影响吧？」

「加尼特先生，我来此地是打球的，不是聊天的。」他说。

我们便照此办理。我从第一洞发球台开球。一记漂亮的开球。若有别人代我夸口，我本不必自夸，谦虚也会不允许。可既然无人代劳，我只好重复这句话：那是我生平最佳的开球之一。球闪过天空，越过沙坑还富余十几英尺，滚上果岭。我早觉出今天必有状态。除非对手也超水平发挥，他已是死定之人。我可以随意耍弄他。

===Original===
The excellence of my drive had not been without its effect on the professor. I could see that he was not confident. He addressed his ball more strangely and at greater length than anyone I had ever seen. He waggled his club over it as if he were going to perform a conjuring trick. Then he struck, and topped it.

The ball rolled two yards.

He looked at it in silence. Then he looked at me﻿—also in silence.

I was gazing seawards.

When I looked round he was getting to work with a brassey.

This time he hit the bunker, and rolled back. He repeated this manoeuvre twice.

===Chinese===
我开球之精妙，并非没在教授身上起作用。看得出他没了自信。他瞄球瞄得又古怪又漫长，超过我平生所见的一切人。他在球上方摆弄球杆，仿佛要变戏法。然后一击——打了个滚地球。

球滚了两码。

他默默看着球。又默默看着我。

我正眺望大海。

我回头时，他已抡起布拉西木杆开工了。

这一回他打进了沙坑，球又滚回来。这套动作他重复了两遍。

===Original===
“Hard luck!” I murmured sympathetically on the third occasion, thereby going as near to being slain with a niblick as it has ever been my lot to go. Your true golfer is easily roused in times of misfortune; and there was a red gleam in the eye of the professor turned to me.

“I shall pick my ball up,” he growled.

We walked on in silence to the second tee. He did the second hole in four, which was good. I did it in three, which﻿—unfortunately for him﻿—was better.

I won the third hole.

I won the fourth hole.

I won the fifth hole.

===Chinese===
第三回上，我满怀同情地低语了一句「手气不佳！」——此语险些为我招来一记尼布利克杆，险到我这辈子没险过这个。真正的球手在倒霉时最易暴起；教授转向我的眼睛里，有一道红光。

「我捡球。」他从牙缝里说。

我们默默走到第二洞发球台。他第二洞四杆完成，不错。我三杆完成——于他不幸——更妙。

第三洞我胜。

第四洞我胜。

第五洞我胜。

===Original===
I glanced at my opponent out of the corner of my eyes. The man was suffering. Beads of perspiration stood out on his forehead.

His play had become wilder and wilder at each hole in arithmetical progression. If he had been a plough he could hardly have turned up more soil. The imagination recoiled from the thought of what he could be doing in another half-hour if he deteriorated at his present speed.

A feeling of calm and content stole over me. I was not sorry for him. All the viciousness of my nature was uppermost in me. Once, when he missed the ball clean at the fifth tee, his eye met mine, and we stood staring at each other for a full half-minute without moving. I believe, if I had smiled then, he would have attacked me without hesitation. There is a type of golfer who really almost ceases to be human under stress of the wild agony of a series of foozles.

The sixth hole involves the player in a somewhat tricky piece of cross-country work, owing to the fact that there is a nasty ditch to be negotiated some fifty yards from the green. It is a beast of a ditch, which, if you are out of luck, just catches your second shot. “All hope abandon ye who enter here” might be written on a notice board over it.

The professor entered there. The unhappy man sent his second, as nice and clean a brassey shot as he had made all day, into its very jaws. And then madness seized him. A merciful local rule, framed by kindly men who have been in that ditch themselves, enacts that in such a case the player may take his ball and throw it over his shoulder, losing a stroke. But once, so the legend runs, a scratch man who found himself trapped, scorning to avail himself of this rule at the expense of its accompanying penalty, wrought so shrewdly with his niblick that he not only got out but actually laid his ball dead: and now optimists sometimes imitate his gallantry, though no one yet has been able to imitate his success.

The professor decided to take a chance: and he failed miserably. As I was on the green with my third, and, unless I putted extremely poorly, was morally certain to be down in five, which is bogey for the hole, there was not much practical use in his continuing to struggle. But he did in a spirit of pure vindictiveness, as if he were trying to take it out of the ball. It was a grisly sight to see him, head and shoulders above the ditch, hewing at his obstinate colonel. It was a similar spectacle that once induced a lay spectator of a golf match to observe that he considered hockey a silly game.

===Chinese===
我用眼角余光瞟我的对手。这人在受罪。他额头上沁出一粒粒汗珠。

他的球技一洞比一洞狂野，呈等差级数上升。他若是张犁，翻起的土也未必比这更多。想到他若照眼下的速度继续退化，再过半个钟头能干出什么来，想象力都要却步。

一种平静与满足之感悄悄爬上我心头。我不怜惜他。我天性里一切的凶恶都浮上了面。有一回他在第五洞发球台上挥了个空，他的眼撞上我的眼，我们一动不动对视了足足半分钟。我相信，那时我若微笑，他会毫不犹豫地扑上来。有一类球手，在一连串臭球激起的狂乱剧痛之下，简直不复为人。

第六洞要球手干一段颇为刁钻的越野活，因为离果岭约五十码处有一条讨厌的水沟要过。那是条畜生般的沟，你若不走运，它就恰好接住你的第二杆。「入此沟者，一切希望俱弃」——大可写在沟上方立一块告示牌。

教授入沟了。这不幸的人送出他的第二杆——一记漂亮干净的布拉西木杆，他全天打得最妙的一记——恰恰送进沟口。随后疯狂攫住了他。当地有一条仁慈的规则，由亲身陷过那条沟的好心人制定：遇到这种情形，球手可捡起球，从肩上向后抛出，罚一杆。但据传说，从前有位零差点高手身陷其中，不屑援用这条规则而付出附带的罚杆，他以尼布利克杆运作得如此精妙，不但脱了困，竟还把球贴死在洞边：从此乐观者们时而仿效他的豪勇，只是至今无人能仿效他的成功。

教授决定搏一把：结果败得凄惨。我第三杆已上果岭，只要推杆不太糟糕，稳稳当当五杆进洞——正是该洞的标准分——他再挣扎实际已无用处。但他偏偏以一种纯粹的泄愤精神挣扎，仿佛要把账算在球身上。看他探在沟上，头和肩膀露在外面，劈砍着他那倔强的「上校」，是一幅惨烈的景象。当年正是类似的一幕，诱使一位观看高尔夫比赛的门外汉发表感想说：他认为曲棍球是项愚蠢的运动。

===Original===
“*Sixteen!*” said the professor between his teeth. Then he picked up his ball.

I won the seventh hole.

I won the eighth hole.

The ninth we halved, for in the black depths of my soul I had formed a plan of fiendish subtlety. I intended to allow him to win﻿—with extreme labour﻿—eight holes in succession.

Then, when hope was once more strong in him, I would win the last, and he would go mad.

I watched him carefully as we trudged on. Emotions chased one another across his face. When he won the tenth hole he merely refrained from oaths. When he won the eleventh a sort of sullen pleasure showed in his face. It was at the thirteenth that I detected the first dawning of hope. From then onward it grew.

===Chinese===
「*十六杆*！」教授从牙缝里挤出来。然后他捡起了球。

第七洞我胜。

第八洞我胜。

第九洞我们打平，因为我灵魂的黑渊里已酝酿出一个恶魔般精细的计划。我打算放他连赢八洞——赢得千辛万苦。

然后，等他心中的希望再度昂扬，我便赢下最后一洞，叫他就此疯掉。

我们跋涉前行，我细细观察他。各种情绪在他脸上你追我赶。赢第十洞时，他还只是忍住没骂。赢第十一洞时，脸上现出一种阴郁的快意。到第十三洞，我察出了希望的第一线微光。自那以后，它愈长愈大。

===Original===
When, with a sequence of shocking shots, he took the seventeenth hole in seven, he was in a parlous condition. His run of success had engendered within him a desire for conversation. He wanted, as it were, to flap his wings and crow. I could see Dignity wrestling with Talkativeness. I gave him the lead.

“You have got back your form now,” I said.

Talkativeness had it. Dignity retired hurt. Speech came from him in a rush. When he brought off an excellent drive from the eighteenth tee, he seemed to forget everything.

“Me dear boy﻿—” he began; and stopped abruptly in some confusion. Silence once more brooded over us as we played ourselves up the fairway and onto the green.

He was on the green in four. I reached it in three. His sixth stroke took him out.

I putted carefully to the very mouth of the hole.

===Chinese===
当他以一连串吓死人的杆数、七杆拿下第十七洞时，他已到了危殆的境地。这一串胜利在他心里养出了谈话的欲望。他简直想拍拍翅膀打鸣。我看得出「尊严」正在同「话痨」角力。我递了话头。

「您找回状态了。」我说。

话痨赢了。尊严负伤退场。话从他嘴里决堤而出。到第十八洞发球台他打出一记精彩的开球时，他似乎把一切都忘了。

「我亲爱的小伙子——」他开了腔；又猛地煞住，颇有些狼狈。当我们一路把球打上球道、送上果岭，沉默重新笼罩了我们。

他四杆上果岭。我三杆上。第六杆把他打出了局。

我小心地推杆，推到洞口。

===Original===
I walked up to my ball, and paused. I looked at the professor. He looked at me.

“Go on,” he said hoarsely.

Suddenly a wave of compassion flooded over me. What right had I to torture the man like this?

“Professor,” I said.

“Go on,” he repeated.

“That looks a simple shot,” I said, eyeing him steadily, “but I might easily miss it.”

===Chinese===
我走到球前，停了停。我看教授。教授看我。

「推吧。」他哑着嗓子说。

一阵恻隐之波突然漫过我全身。我有什么权利这样折磨一个人？

「教授。」我说。

「推吧。」他重复道。

「这一杆看着简单，」我目不转睛地望着他说，「我却很容易失手。」

===Original===
He started.

“And then you would win the Championship.”

He dabbed at his forehead with a wet ball of a handkerchief.

“It would be very pleasant for you after getting so near it the last two years.”

“Go on,” he said for the third time. But there was a note of hesitation in his voice.

“Sudden joy,” I said, “would almost certainly make me miss it.”

===Chinese===
他一震。

「那您就赢走冠军了。」

他用一团湿手帕抹了抹额头。

「前两年都功败垂成，今年一旦夺魁，您必然其乐无穷。」

「推吧。」他第三次说。但话音里带了一丝踌躇。

「突如其来的狂喜，」我说，「几乎必然会使我失手。」

===Original===
We looked at each other. He had the golf fever in his eyes.

“If,” I said slowly, lifting my putter, “you were to give your consent to my marriage with Phyllis﻿—”

He looked from me to the ball, from the ball to me, and back again to the ball. It was very, very near the hole.

“Why not?” I said.

He looked up, and burst into a roar of laughter.

“You young devil,” said he, smiting his thigh, “you young devil, you’ve beaten me.”

===Chinese===
我们对望着。他眼里燃着高尔夫的热病。

「倘若，」我缓缓地说，举起推杆，「您肯俯允我与菲莉斯的婚事——」

他的目光从我移到球上，从球移回我，再移回球。球离洞非常、非常近。

「有何不可呢？」我说。

他抬起头，爆出一阵大笑。

「你这个小魔鬼，」他拍着大腿说，「你这个小魔鬼，你把我打垮了。」

===Original===
I swung my putter, and the ball trickled past the hole.

“On the contrary,” I said, “you have beaten me.”

---

I left the professor at the Club House and raced back to the farm. I wanted to pour my joys into a sympathetic ear. Ukridge, I knew, would offer that same sympathetic ear. A good fellow, Ukridge. Always interested in what you had to tell him; never bored.

“Ukridge!” I shouted.

No answer.

===Chinese===
我挥动推杆，球擦洞而过。

「恰恰相反，」我说，「是您打垮了我。」

---

我把教授留在会所，一路奔回农场。我想把我的喜事倒进一只同情的耳朵。我知道，厄克里奇准会献上那只同情的耳朵。厄克里奇是个好汉子。你对他说什么都感兴趣；从不厌倦。

「厄克里奇！」我喊。

无人应答。

===Original===
I flung open the dining-room door. Nobody.

I went into the drawing-room. It was empty. I drew the garden, and his bedroom. He was not in either.

“He must have gone for a stroll,” I said.

I rang the bell.

The Hired Retainer appeared, calm and imperturbable as ever.

“Sir?”

===Chinese===
我推开饭厅的门。没有人。

我走进客厅。空的。我又搜了花园和他的卧室。都不见人影。

「他准是散步去了。」我说。

我摇铃。

那位雇仆出现了，一如既往地平静镇定。

「先生？」

===Original===
“Oh, where is Mr. Ukridge, Beale?”

“Mr. Ukridge, sir,” said the Hired Retainer nonchalantly, “has gone.”

“Gone!”

“Yes, sir. Mr. Ukridge and Mrs. Ukridge went away together by the three o’clock train.”

===Chinese===
「哦，比尔，厄克里奇先生在哪儿？」

「厄克里奇先生，老爷，」雇仆漫不经心地说，「走了。」

「走了！」

「是，老爷。厄克里奇先生和厄克里奇太太一起，搭三点钟的火车走了。」

## XII Some Emotions and Yellow Lupin / 十二 几番心事与黄花羽扇豆

===Original===
The fame which came to me through that gallant rescue was a little embarrassing. I was a marked man. Did I walk through the village, heads emerged from windows, and eyes followed me out of sight. Did I sit on the beach, groups formed behind me and watched in silent admiration. I was the man of the moment.

“If we’d wanted an advertisement for the farm,” said Ukridge on one of these occasions, “we couldn’t have had a better one than you, Garny, my boy. You have brought us three distinct orders for eggs during the last week. And I’ll tell you what it is, we need all the orders we can get that’ll bring us in ready money. The farm is in a critical condition. The coffers are low, deuced low. And I’ll tell you another thing. I’m getting precious tired of living on nothing but chicken and eggs. So’s Millie, though she doesn’t say so.”

“So am I,” I said, “and I don’t feel like imitating your wife’s proud reserve. I never want to see a chicken again. As for eggs, they are far too much with us.”

For the last week monotony had been the keynote of our commissariat. We had had cold chicken and eggs for breakfast, boiled chicken and eggs for lunch, and roast chicken and eggs for dinner. Meals became a nuisance, and Mrs. Beale complained bitterly that we did not give her a chance. She was a cook who would have graced an alderman’s house and served up noble dinners for gourmets, and here she was in this remote corner of the world ringing the changes on boiled chicken and roast chicken and boiled eggs and poached eggs. Mr. Whistler, set to paint signboards for public-houses, might have felt the same restless discontent. As for her husband, the Hired Retainer, he took life as tranquilly as ever, and seemed to regard the whole thing as the most exhilarating farce he had ever been in. I think he looked on Ukridge as an amiable lunatic, and was content to rough it a little in order to enjoy the privilege of observing his movements. He made no complaints of the food. When a man has supported life for a number of years on incessant Army beef, the monotony of daily chicken and eggs scarcely strikes him.

“The fact is,” said Ukridge, “these tradesmen round here seem to be a sordid, suspicious lot. They clamour for money.”

He mentioned a few examples. Vickers, the butcher, had been the first to strike, with the remark that he would like to see the colour of Mr. Ukridge’s money before supplying further joints. Dawlish, the grocer, had expressed almost exactly similar sentiments two days later; and the ranks of these passive resisters had been receiving fresh recruits ever since. To a man the tradesmen of Combe Regis seemed as deficient in Simple Faith as they were in Norman Blood.

===Chinese===
那场英勇救援给我带来的名声，有点叫人难为情。我成了众目所归的人物。我走过村子，一颗颗脑袋从窗户里探出来，一双双眼睛把我望到看不见。我往海滩上一坐，身后便聚起一堆人，默默景仰地看着。我是当红的人物。

「咱们若想给农场做个广告，」有一次这种场合下厄克里奇说，「再也找不到比你更好的了，加尼我的小子。过去一星期，你给我们招来了三笔实打实的蛋订单。我告诉你，能带来现钱的订单，来多少咱们要多少。农场正处危急关头。钱柜见底了，见底得厉害。再告诉你一件事：光吃鸡和蛋，我可是过够了。米莉也一样，只是不说。」

「我也是，」我说，「而且我不打算学你太太那份骄傲的缄默。我这辈子再也不想见鸡了。至于蛋，它们同我们实在是亲近得过了头。」

过去一星期，单调是我们伙食的主调。早饭是冷鸡和蛋，午饭是煮鸡和蛋，晚饭是烤鸡和蛋。吃饭成了苦差，比尔太太苦楚地抱怨咱们不给她机会。她是位本可以装点市政官宅邸、给美食家奉上华筵的厨子，如今却在这世界的一隅，翻来覆去煮鸡烤鸡、煮蛋卧蛋。让惠斯勒先生（Mr. Whistler）去画酒馆招牌，大约也会生出同样的、不得安宁的不满。至于她丈夫，那位雇仆，依旧把日子过得波澜不惊，似乎把整件事看作他生平看过的最提神的一出闹剧。我想他把厄克里奇看作一个和气的疯子，甘愿陪着吃点苦，以换得观察其一举一动的眼福。伙食方面他毫无怨言。一个连年靠军队牛肉支撑生命的人，天天鸡和蛋的单调根本算不得什么。

「实情是，」厄克里奇说，「这一带的买卖人似乎是帮又贪又疑的家伙。他们嚷着要钱。」

他举了几个例子。肉铺的维克斯（Vickers）第一个罢市，声称在继续供肉之前，想先见识见识厄克里奇先生钞票的颜色。杂货铺的道利什（Dawlish）两天后发表了几乎一模一样的感想；此后，这批消极抵抗者的队伍不断吸收新成员。可以说，库姆里吉斯的买卖人人同此心：他们之缺乏单纯的信念，一如他们之缺乏诺曼血统。

===Original===
“Can’t you pay some of them a little on account?” I suggested. “It would set them going again.”

“My dear old man,” said Ukridge impressively, “we need every penny of ready money we can raise for the farm. The place simply eats money. That infernal roop let us in for I don’t know what.”

That insidious epidemic had indeed proved costly. We had painted the throats of the chickens with the best turpentine﻿—at least Ukridge and Beale had﻿—but in spite of their efforts dozens had died, and we had been obliged to sink much more money than was pleasant in restocking the run. The battle which took place on the first day after the election of the new members was a sight to remember. The results of it were still noticeable in the depressed aspect of certain of the recently enrolled.

“No,” said Ukridge, summing up, “these men must wait. We can’t help their troubles. Why, good gracious, it isn’t as if they’d been waiting for the money long. We’ve not been down here much over a month. I never heard of such a scandalous thing. ’Pon my word, I’ve a good mind to go round, and have a straight talk with one or two of them. I come and settle down here, and stimulate trade, and give them large orders, and they worry me with bills when they know I’m up to my eyes in work, looking after the fowls. One can’t attend to everything. The business is just now at its most crucial point. It would be fatal to pay any attention to anything else with things as they are. These scoundrels will get paid all in good time.”

It is a peculiarity of situations of this kind that the ideas of debtor and creditor as to what constitutes a good time never coincide.

---

===Chinese===
「你不能先还他们一点账吗？」我提议。「好叫他们再动起来。」

「亲爱的老伙计，」厄克里奇郑重地说，「每一便士现钱，咱们都得搜刮来填农场。这地方简直是吃钱的。那场要命的鸡喉瘟，不知把咱们拖进了多深的坑。」

那场阴险的瘟疫确实代价高昂。我们给鸡喉咙涂了上好的松节油——至少厄克里奇和比尔涂了——但尽管他们努力，鸡还是成打地死，我们只好又往补笼的钱里，沉入了超出愉快程度的一大笔。新成员入伙后头一天那场大战，是值得铭记的景象。其后果，至今还在某些新近入伙者的委顿面容上依稀可见。

「不行，」厄克里奇总结道，「这些人只能等。他们的难处咱们爱莫能助。嗐，天哪，又不像他们已经等了很久。咱们下来统共才个把月。我没听说过这样骇人听闻的事。说实话，我真想挨家走一圈，同一两位正正经经谈谈。我来到此地定居，刺激了商业，给他们大笔订单，他们倒趁我埋头照管鸡群、忙得不可开交时，拿账单来烦我。人不能事事兼顾。生意眼下正处在最紧要的节骨眼上。照眼前这样，分心去管别的任何事都是致命的。这帮恶棍早晚分文不少地拿到钱。」

这类局面有个特点：债务人和债权人关于什么叫「早晚」，意见从来不曾一致。

---

===Original===
I am afraid that, despite the urgent need for strict attention to business, I was inclined to neglect my duties about this time. I had got into the habit of wandering off, either to the links, where I generally found the professor, sometimes Phyllis, or on long walks by myself. There was one particular walk along the cliffs, through some of the most beautiful scenery I have ever set eyes on, which more than any other suited my mood. I would work my way through the woods till I came to a small clearing on the very edge of the cliff. There I would sit and smoke by the hour. If ever I am stricken with smoker’s heart, or staggers, or tobacco amblyopia, or any other of the cheery things which doctors predict for the devotee of the weed, I shall feel that I sowed the seeds of it that summer in that little clearing overlooking the sea. A man in love needs much tobacco. A man thinking out a novel needs much tobacco. I was in the grip of both maladies. Somehow I found that my ideas flowed more readily in that spot than in any other.

I had not been inside the professor’s grounds since the occasion when I had gone in through the boxwood hedge. But on the afternoon following my financial conversation with Ukridge I made my way thither, after a toilet which, from its length, should have produced better results than it did. Not for four whole days had I caught so much as a glimpse of Phyllis. I had been to the links three times, and had met the professor twice, but on both occasions she had been absent. I had not had the courage to ask after her. I had an absurd idea that my voice or my manner would betray me in some way. I felt that I should have put the question with such an exaggerated show of indifference that all would have been discovered.

The professor was not at home. Nor was Mr. Chase. Nor was Miss Norah Derrick, the lady I had met on the beach with the professor. Miss Phyllis, said the maid, was in the garden.

I went into the garden. She was sitting under the cedar by the tennis-lawn, reading. She looked up as I approached.

I said it was a lovely afternoon. After which there was a lull in the conversation. I was filled with a horrid fear that I was boring her. I had probably arrived at the very moment when she was most interested in her book. She must, I thought, even now be regarding me as a nuisance, and was probably rehearsing bitter things to say to the maid for not having had the sense to explain that she was out.

“I﻿—er﻿—called in the hope of seeing Professor Derrick,” I said.

===Chinese===
恐怕尽管生意亟需专心，我在那阵子却怠慢了职守。我养成了溜号的癖好，不是去球场——在那儿常遇见教授，偶尔也有菲莉斯——就是独自做长途散步。有一条沿悬崖的散步道尤其合我心境，穿过我生平所见最美的几段风景。我总在树林里穿行，直到悬崖正边缘上的一片小空地。我便在那儿坐着，一抽烟就是几个钟头。日后我若害上烟客心脏病、眩晕症、烟草性弱视，或医生们为烟民预言的别的什么开心病，我必认定祸根是那年夏天、在那片俯瞰大海的小空地里种下的。恋爱中的人需要大量烟草。构思小说的人需要大量烟草。我两病兼患。也不知怎么，我发现在那地方，思路比在哪里都通畅。

自打从黄杨树篱钻进去那回以后，我再没进过教授家的园子。但与厄克里奇那番银钱谈话的次日午后，我梳洗了半天又往那儿去——那番梳洗，论工夫之长，本该产生比实际更好的效果。整整四天，我连菲莉斯的影子都没见到。球场我去了三回，教授遇见两回，两回她都不在场。我又没胆量问起她。我有个荒唐的念头，觉得我的嗓音或神色会以某种方式出卖我。我总觉得，我一发问，那份过分的漠然神气必定弄得欲盖弥彰。

教授不在家。蔡斯先生也不在。诺拉·德里克小姐——我在海滩上同教授一起遇见的那位女士——也不在。女仆说，菲莉斯小姐在园子里。

我走进园子。她正坐在网球场边的雪松下看书。我走近时，她抬起头来。

我说今儿下午真美。说完，谈话便陷入停顿。我心里充满一种可怕的疑惧，怕我烦着她了。我多半来得正是她书中情节最要紧的当口。我想，此刻她必把我看作一个讨嫌鬼，而且多半正在心里预演些刻毒话，回头好骂那个没脑子、不曾替她扯谎说「不在家」的女仆。

「我——呃——是抱着见德里克教授的指望来的。」我说。

===Original===
“You would find him on the links,” she replied. It seemed to me that she spoke wistfully.

“Oh, it﻿—it doesn’t matter,” I said. “It wasn’t anything important.”

This was true. If the professor had appeared then and there, I should have found it difficult to think of anything to say to him which would have accounted to any extent for my anxiety to see him.

“How are the chickens, Mr. Garnet?” said she.

The situation was saved. Conversationally, I am like a clockwork toy. I have to be set going. On the affairs of the farm I could speak fluently. I sketched for her the progress we had made since her visit. I was humorous concerning roop, epigrammatic on the subject of the Hired Retainer and Edwin.

“Then the cat did come down from the chimney?” said Phyllis.

===Chinese===
「您会在球场上找到他。」她答道。我觉得她说得怅然。

「哦，那——那不要紧，」我说。「没有什么要紧事。」

这是实话。教授当时若真当场出现，我也很难想出什么话对他说，能多少解释我那非见他不可的急切。

「鸡们都好吗，加尼特先生？」她说。

局面得救了。谈话方面，我像一只发条玩具，得有人上弦才动得起来。农场的事务我能谈得滔滔不绝。我给她描画了自她来访之后的进展。谈到鸡喉瘟我妙趣横生，论及那位雇仆和埃德温，我警句迭出。

「那么那只猫到底还是从烟囱里下来了？」菲莉斯说。

===Original===
We both laughed, and﻿—I can answer for myself﻿—felt the better for it.

“He came down next day,” I said, “and made an excellent lunch of one of our best fowls. He also killed another, and only just escaped death himself at the hands of Ukridge.”

“Mr. Ukridge doesn’t like him, does he?”

“If he does, he dissembles his love. Edwin is Mrs. Ukridge’s pet. He is the only subject on which they disagree. Edwin is certainly in the way on a chicken farm. He has got over his fear of Bob, and is now perfectly lawless. We have to keep a steady eye on him.”

“And have you had any success with the incubator? I love incubators. I have always wanted to have one of my own, but we have never kept fowls.”

“The incubator has not done all that it should have done,” I said. “Ukridge looks after it, and I fancy his methods are not the right methods. I don’t know if I have got the figures absolutely correct, but Ukridge reasons on these lines. He says you are supposed to keep the temperature up to a hundred and five degrees. I think he said a hundred and five. Then the eggs are supposed to hatch out in a week or so. He argues that you may just as well keep the temperature at seventy-two, and wait a fortnight for your chickens. I am certain there’s a fallacy in the system somewhere, because we never seem to get as far as the chickens. But Ukridge says his theory is mathematically sound, and he sticks to it.”

===Chinese===
我们都笑了，而且——我至少可以为自己担保——笑过之后轻快多了。

「第二天就下来了，」我说，「把咱们一只最好的鸡当了顿上好的午餐。他还咬死了另一只，自己险些死在厄克里奇手里。」

「厄克里奇先生不喜欢他，对吧？」

「就算喜欢，他也深藏不露。埃德温是厄克里奇太太的心肝。这是他们夫妇唯一意见相左的题目。埃德温在养鸡场上确实碍事。他已经不怕博布了，如今彻底无法无天。我们得时刻盯牢他。」

「那孵化器有成绩吗？我喜欢孵化器。我一直想自己有一台，可我们家从来不养鸡。」

「孵化器没干成它该干成的一切，」我说。「厄克里奇照管它，我看他那套办法不是正路。数字我未必记得分毫不差，但厄克里奇的推理是这么一路的。他说，人家规矩是把温度保持在华氏一百零五度。我记得他说的是一百零五。蛋呢，规矩是一礼拜上下孵出来。他的道理是：温度既不妨只保持在七十二度，等上两礼拜再要小鸡便是了。我敢肯定这套体系某处有个漏洞，因为我们好像从来走不到出小鸡那一步。可厄克里奇说他的理论数学上站得住，他咬定不放。」

===Original===
“Are you quite sure that the way you are doing it is the best way to manage a chicken farm?”

“I should very much doubt it. I am a child in these matters. I had only seen a chicken in its wild state once or twice before we came down here. I had never dreamed of being an active assistant on a real farm. The whole thing began like Mr. George Ade’s fable of the Author. An Author﻿—myself﻿—was sitting at his desk trying to turn out any old thing that could be converted into breakfast-food, when a friend came in, and sat down on the table, and told him to go right on and not mind him.”

“Did Mr. Ukridge do that?”

“Very nearly that. He called at my rooms one beautiful morning when I was feeling desperately tired of London and overworked and dying for a holiday, and suggested that I should come to Combe Regis with him and help him farm chickens. I have not regretted it.”

“It is a lovely place, isn’t it?”

“The loveliest I have ever seen. How charming your garden is.”

===Chinese===
「您敢肯定，你们眼下这套办养鸡场的路子，是最好的路子吗？」

「我很怀疑。这种事我是个娃娃。下来之前，我统共只在野生状态下见过一两回鸡。做梦也没想过有朝一日在一座真农场上当得力助手。整个事情的开头，活像乔治·艾德先生（Mr. George Ade）那则作家寓言。一位作家——区区在下——正坐在书桌前，想炮制随便什么能换成早饭吃食的玩意儿，这时一个朋友进来了，往桌上一坐，叫他只管忙他的，不必理会来客。」

「厄克里奇先生就是这么干的？」

「几乎分毫不差。一个明媚的早晨他到我寓所来，那时我正对伦敦厌倦透顶、操劳过度、巴不得放个假，他便提议我随他到库姆里吉斯来，帮他养鸡。我不曾后悔。」

「这地方真可爱，是不是？」

「我平生所见最可爱的。您的园子多美。」

===Original===
“Shall we go and look at it? You have not seen the whole of it.”

As she rose, I saw her book, which she had laid face downwards on the grass beside her. It was the same much-enduring copy of the *Manœuvres of Arthur*. I was thrilled. This patient perseverance must surely mean something. She saw me looking at it.

“Did you draw Pamela from anybody?” she asked suddenly.

I was glad now that I had not done so. The wretched Pamela, once my pride, was for some reason unpopular with the only critic about whose opinion I cared, and had fallen accordingly from her pedestal.

As we wandered down from the garden paths, she gave me her opinion of the book. In the main it was appreciative. I shall always associate the scent of yellow lupin with the higher criticism.

“Of course, I don’t know anything about writing books,” she said.

===Chinese===
「咱们去走走好吗？您还没看全呢。」

她起身时，我看见她那本书，方才面朝下扣在她身旁的草地上。正是那部久经磨难、屡读不倦的《阿瑟的谋略》。我一阵激动。这份坚韧的耐性，必定意味着什么。她看见我在看它。

「帕米拉是照着什么真人写的吗？」她突然问。

我这会儿庆幸没照真人写。那个可怜的帕米拉，从前是我的骄傲，如今不知怎么，在那位我唯一在乎其意见的批评家那里失了宠，也就相应地从宝座上跌了下来。

我们顺园中小径一路走下去，她把对这本书的意见说给我听。大体上是嘉许的。黄羽扇豆的香气，从此永远同高层文艺批评连在一起了。

「当然，写书的事我一窍不通。」她说。

===Original===
“Yes?” my tone implied, or I hope it did, that she was an expert on books, and that if she was not it didn’t matter.

“But I don’t think you do your heroines well. I have just got *The Outsider*﻿—” (My other novel. Bastable & Kirby, 6s. Satirical. All about Society﻿—of which I know less than I know about chicken-farming. Slated by *Times* and *Spectator*. Well received by *London Mail* and *Winning Post*.) “﻿—and,” continued Phyllis, “Lady Maud is exactly the same as Pamela in the *Manœuvres of Arthur*. I thought you must have drawn both characters from someone you knew.”

“No,” I said. “No. Purely imaginary.”

“I am so glad,” said Phyllis.

And then neither of us seemed to have anything to say. My knees began to tremble. I realised that the moment had arrived when my fate must be put to the touch; and I feared that the moment was premature. We cannot arrange these things to suit ourselves. I knew that the time was not yet ripe; but the magic scent of the yellow lupin was too much for me.

“Miss Derrick,” I said hoarsely.

===Chinese===
「是吗？」我的语气暗示——或者我希望它暗示——她是书籍的行家，即便不是也无关紧要。

「不过我觉得，您的女主人公写得不好。我刚拿到《局外人》——」（我的另一部小说。巴斯特布尔与柯比书店出版，六先令。讽刺性的。写的全是上流社会——这个领域，我所知比养鸡还少。《泰晤士报》和《旁观者》猛批。《伦敦邮报》和《赛马邮报》叫好。）「——而且，」菲莉斯接着说，「莫德小姐跟《阿瑟的谋略》里的帕米拉一模一样。我还以为，两个人物您都是照着熟人写的呢。」

「不，」我说，「不。纯属虚构。」

「我真高兴。」菲莉斯说。

接着，我们俩似乎都没了话。我的膝盖开始打颤。我意识到，摊牌的时候到了；可我又怕这时刻来得太早。这种事由不得我们自己安排。我知道火候未到；可黄羽扇豆那魔幻的香气，叫我再也把持不住。

「德里克小姐。」我嗓音发干地说。

===Original===
Phyllis was looking with more intentness than the attractions of the flower justified at a rose she held in her hand. The bee hummed in the lupin.

“Miss Derrick,” I said, and stopped again.

“I say, you people,” said a cheerful voice, “tea is ready. Hullo, Garnet, how are you? That medal arrived yet from the Humane Society?”

I spun round. Mr. Tom Chase was standing at the end of the path. The only word that could deal adequately with the situation slapped against my front teeth. I grinned a sickly grin.

“Well, Tom,” said Phyllis.

And there was, I thought, just the faintest tinkle of annoyance in her voice.

===Chinese===
菲莉斯正端详手里的一朵玫瑰，那份专注，远超这花的美色所值得的。蜜蜂在羽扇豆里嗡嗡作响。

「德里克小姐。」我又说，又止住了。

「喂，你们两位，」一个欢快的声音说，「茶备好了。哈罗，加尼特，你好吗？皇家救生会的那枚奖章到了没有？」

我霍地转身。汤姆·蔡斯先生站在小径尽头。唯一能恰当对付这局面的那个词，正拍打着我的门牙。我咧开嘴，赔出一个病弱的笑。

「嗳，汤姆。」菲莉斯说。

我心想，她的声音里恰有一丝恼意的轻响。

===Original===
---

“I’ve been bathing,” said Mr. Chase, *à propos des bottes*.

“Oh,” I replied. “And I wish,” I added, “that you’d drowned yourself.”

But I added it silently to myself.

===Chinese===
---

「我方才游泳来着。」蔡斯先生说，*顺口一提*。

「哦。」我应道。「而且我希望，」我补了一句，「你淹死了才好。」

不过这一句，我是默默补给自己听的。

## XIII Tea and Tennis / 十三 茶与网球

===Original===
“Met the professor’s late boatman on the Cob,” said Mr. Chase, dissecting a chocolate cake.

“Clumsy man,” said Phyllis, “I hope he was ashamed of himself. I shall never forgive him for trying to drown papa.”

My heart bled for Mr. Henry Hawk, that modern martyr.

“When I met him,” said Tom Chase, “he looked as if he had been trying to drown his sorrow as well.”

“I knew he drank,” said Phyllis severely, “the very first time I saw him.”

“You might have warned the professor,” murmured Mr. Chase.

===Chinese===
「在科布防波堤上遇见了教授从前的船夫，」蔡斯先生说，一面解剖一块巧克力蛋糕。

「笨手笨脚的家伙，」菲莉斯说，「我希望他自己害臊。他想淹死爸爸，我永远不原谅他。」

我为亨利·霍克先生——这位现代殉道者——一掬同情之泪。

「我遇见他的时候，」汤姆·蔡斯说，「他那模样，像是把愁闷也一并往肚子里灌了。」

「他酗酒，我头一眼看见他就知道了。」菲莉斯声色俱厉地说。

「你本可以预先提醒教授嘛。」蔡斯先生喃喃道。

===Original===
“He couldn’t have upset the boat if he had been sober.”

“You never know. He may have done it on purpose.”

“Tom, how absurd.”

“Rather rough on the man, aren’t you?” I said.

“Merely a suggestion,” continued Mr. Chase airily. “I’ve been reading sensational novels lately, and it seems to me that Mr. Hawk’s cut out to be a minion. Probably some secret foe of the professor’s bribed him.”

My heart stood still. Did he know, I wondered, and was this all a roundabout way of telling me that he knew?

===Chinese===
「他若清醒着，总不至于把船弄翻。」

「这可说不准。说不定他是故意的。」

「汤姆，多荒唐。」

「你们对这位未免太苛刻了吧？」我说。

「不过提个假设，」蔡斯先生轻飘飘地接着说，「我近来在读惊险小说，依我看，霍克先生天生是个爪牙的坯子。多半是教授的哪个秘密仇家买通了他。」

我的心停跳了。他是不是知道了，我暗想；这一番话，是不是他绕着弯子告诉我他知道？

===Original===
“The professor may be a member of an Anarchist League, or something, and this is his punishment for refusing to assassinate some sportsman.”

“Have another cup of tea, Tom, and stop talking nonsense.”

Mr. Chase handed in his cup.

“What gave me the idea that the upset was done on purpose was this. I saw the whole thing from the Ware Cliff. The spill looked to me just like dozens I had seen at Malta.”

“Why do they upset themselves on purpose at Malta particularly?” inquired Phyllis.

“Listen carefully, my dear, and you’ll know more about the ways of the Navy that guards your coasts than you did before. When men are allowed on shore at Malta, the owner has a fancy to see them snugly on board again at a certain reasonable hour. After that hour any Maltese policeman who brings them aboard gets one sovereign, cash. But he has to do all the bringing part of it on his own. Consequence is, you see boats rowing out to the ship, carrying men who have overstayed their leave; and when they get near enough, the able-bodied gentleman in custody jumps to his feet, upsets the boat, and swims for the gangway. The policemen, if they aren’t drowned﻿—they sometimes are﻿—race him, and whichever gets there first wins. If it’s the policeman, he gets his sovereign. If it’s the sailor, he is considered to have arrived not in a state of custody and gets off easier. What a judicious remark that was of the governor of North Carolina to the governor of South Carolina, respecting the length of time between drinks. Just one more cup, please, Phyllis.”

===Chinese===
「教授说不定是什么无政府主义者同盟的会员，这是他拒不受命刺杀某位运动员遭的报应。」

「再喝一杯茶吧，汤姆，别胡说八道了。」

蔡斯先生把杯子递了上去。

「我之所以想到这次翻船是故意的，缘由在此。全过程我在韦尔崖上看得一清二楚。那副落水相，在我看来，同我在马耳他见过的几十起一模一样。」

「为什么偏偏在马耳他，他们就特别爱故意翻船呢？」菲莉斯问。

「仔细听着，亲爱的，听完你对守卫你等海岸的海军的了解就胜过从前。在马耳他，水兵获准上岸后，舰长有个雅兴：要他们在某个合情合理的钟点安安稳稳回船。过了那个钟点，凡能把他们弄回船上的马耳他警察，即得现洋一镑。可弄人上船这一路的力气，全得他自己出。于是你就看见一条条小艇划向军舰，载着逾期不归的水兵；等靠得够近了，被押的那位壮士一跃而起，掀翻小艇，直奔舷梯游去。警察们——若没淹死的话，淹死也是时有的事——同他赛跑，谁先到谁赢。警察先到，他挣他的一镑。水兵先到，便算他不是在被押解状态中到船的，处分从轻。北卡罗来纳总督对南卡罗来纳总督就两杯酒之间隔多久所发的那句高论，说得多么有见识。麻烦再来一杯，菲莉丝。」

===Original===
“But how does all that apply?” I asked, dry-mouthed.

“Mr. Hawk upset the professor just as those Maltese were upset. There’s a patent way of doing it. Furthermore, by judicious questioning, I found that Hawk was once in the Navy, and stationed at Malta. *Now*, who’s going to drag in Sherlock Holmes?”

“You don’t really think﻿—?” I said, feeling like a criminal in the dock when the case is going against him.

“I think friend Hawk has been reenacting the joys of his vanished youth, so to speak.”

“He ought to be prosecuted,” said Phyllis, blazing with indignation.

Alas, poor Hawk!

===Chinese===
「可这些同咱们的事有什么相干？」我问，口干舌燥。

「霍克先生掀教授，正跟那些马耳他人被掀是同一个掀法。这有一套专利手法。而且，经我一连串审慎的盘问，我查出霍克从前在海军当差，驻的正是马耳他。*如今*，谁还用得着请夏洛克·福尔摩斯出马？」

「你该不是真觉得——？」我说，自觉像个官司眼看要输的被告。

「我看那位鹰兄，可以这么说吧，是在重温他一去不返的青春乐事。」

「该把他法办。」菲莉斯说，怒火中烧。

呜呼，可怜的霍克！

===Original===
“Nobody’s safe with a man of that sort, hiring out a boat.” Oh, miserable Hawk!

“But why on earth should he play a trick like that on Professor Derrick, Chase?”

“Pure animal spirits, probably. Or he may, as I say, be a minion.”

I was hot all over.

“I shall tell father that,” said Phyllis in her most decided voice, “and see what he says. I don’t wonder at the man taking to drink after doing such a thing.”

“I﻿—I think you’re making a mistake,” I said.

===Chinese===
「这种出租船的人，谁沾上谁倒霉。」啊，悲惨的霍克！

「可他到底为什么对德里克教授来这么一手呢，蔡斯？」

「多半是纯粹的血气之勇。再不然，就如我所说，是个爪牙。」

我浑身发热。

「这话我要告诉爸爸，」菲莉斯用她最斩钉截铁的嗓音说，「看他怎么说。这人干出这种事之后借酒浇愁，我毫不奇怪。」

「我——我想你们是弄错了。」我说。

===Original===
“I never make mistakes,” Mr. Chase replied. “I am called Archibald the All-Right, for I am infallible. I propose to keep a reflective eye upon the jovial Hawk.”

He helped himself to another section of the chocolate cake.

“Haven’t you finished *yet*, Tom?” inquired Phyllis. “I’m sure Mr. Garnet’s getting tired of sitting talking here,” she said.

I shot out a polite negative. Mr. Chase explained with his mouth full that he had by no means finished. Chocolate cake, it appeared, was the dream of his life. When at sea he was accustomed to lie awake o’ nights thinking of it.

“You don’t seem to realise,” he said, “that I have just come from a cruise on a torpedo-boat. There was such a sea on as a rule that cooking operations were entirely suspended, and we lived on ham and sardines﻿—without bread.”

“How horrible!”

===Chinese===
「我从不弄错，」蔡斯先生答道。「人家叫我『一贯正确的阿奇博尔德』（Archibald the All-Right），因为我从不出错。我打算对那位乐呵呵的霍克，保持一只思索的眼睛。」

他又给自己切了一角巧克力蛋糕。

「你*还*没吃完吗，汤姆？」菲莉斯问。「我敢说，加尼特先生坐在这儿陪聊，早就坐腻了。」她说。

我慌忙发出一声彬彬有礼的否认。蔡斯先生满嘴蛋糕，解释说他远没有吃完。巧克力蛋糕，看样子，是他毕生的梦想。在海上时，他惯于夜里躺着不睡，专想它。

「你们似乎不明白，」他说，「我刚从鱼雷艇巡航回来。多半日子浪大得厨房全面停工，我们全靠火腿和沙丁鱼过活——没有面包。」

「多可怕！」

===Original===
“On the other hand,” added Mr. Chase philosophically, “it didn’t matter much, because we were all ill most of the time.”

“Don’t be nasty, Tom.”

“I was merely defending myself. I hope Mr. Hawk will be able to do as well when his turn comes. My aim, my dear Phyllis, is to show you in a series of impressionist pictures the sort of thing I have to go through when I’m not here. Then perhaps you won’t rend me so savagely over a matter of five minutes’ lateness for breakfast.”

“Five minutes! It was three-quarters of an hour, and everything was simply frozen.”

“Quite right too in weather like this. You’re a slave to convention, Phyllis. You think breakfast ought to be hot, so you always have it hot. On occasion I prefer mine cold. Mine is the truer wisdom. You can give the cook my compliments, Phyllis, and tell her﻿—gently, for I don’t wish the glad news to overwhelm her﻿—that I enjoyed that cake. Say that I shall be glad to hear from her again. Care for a game of tennis, Garnet?”

“What a pity Norah isn’t here,” said Phyllis. “We could have had a four.”

===Chinese===
「话说回来，」蔡斯先生豁达地补充，「这也无妨大局，因为反正我们大部分时间都在晕船。」

「别讨人嫌，汤姆。」

「我不过替自己辩护。但愿轮到霍克的时候，他也能有这般表现。我亲爱的菲莉斯，我的目的，是用一组印象派的画图，让你看看我不在这儿时过的什么日子。这样，为着早饭迟到五分钟这类事，你也许就不至于把我撕得那么狠了。」

「五分钟！是整整三刻钟，什么都冻透了。」

「这种天气，冻透了才对呢。你是个习俗的奴隶，菲莉丝。你认为早饭该是热的，于是永远吃热的。我呢，偶尔偏爱冷的。我的才是更高的智慧。你可以替我向厨子致意，菲莉丝，告诉她——婉转着点儿，我不想叫这喜讯冲昏她——说那块蛋糕我吃得满意。就说，我乐于再听到她的消息。来一局网球吗，加尼特？」

「可惜诺拉不在，」菲莉斯说。「不然可以打双打。」

===Original===
“But she is at present wasting her sweetness on the desert air of Yeovil. You had better sit down and watch us, Phyllis. Tennis in this sort of weather is no job for the delicately-nurtured feminine. I will explain the finer points of my play as we go on. Look out particularly for the Tilden Backhanded Slosh. A winner every time.”

We proceeded to the tennis court. I played with the sun in my eyes. I might, if I chose, emphasise that fact, and attribute my subsequent rout to it, adding, by way of solidifying the excuse, that I was playing in a strange court with a borrowed racquet, and that my mind was preoccupied﻿—firstly, with *l’affaire* Hawk, secondly, and chiefly, with the gloomy thought that Phyllis and my opponent seemed to be on friendly terms with each other. Their manner at tea had been almost that of an engaged couple. There was a thorough understanding between them. I will not, however, take refuge behind excuses. I admit, without qualifying the statement, that Mr. Chase was too good for me. I had always been under the impression that lieutenants in the Royal Navy were not brilliant at tennis. I had met them at various houses, but they had never shone conspicuously. They had played an earnest, unobtrusive game, and generally seemed glad when it was over. Mr. Chase was not of this sort. His service was bottled lightning. His returns behaved like jumping crackers. He won the first game in precisely six strokes. He served. Only once did I take the service with the full face of the racquet, and then I seemed to be stopping a bullet. I returned it into the net. The last of the series struck the wooden edge of my racquet, and soared over the back net into the shrubbery, after the manner of a snick to long slip off a fast bowler.

“Game,” said Mr. Chase, “we’ll look for that afterwards.”

I felt a worm and no man. Phyllis, I thought, would probably judge my entire character from this exhibition. A man, she would reflect, who could be so feeble and miserable a failure at tennis, could not be good for much in any department of life. She would compare me instinctively with my opponent, and contrast his dash and brilliance with my own inefficiency. Somehow the massacre was beginning to have a bad effect on my character. All my self-respect was ebbing. A little more of this, and I should become crushed﻿—a mere human jelly. It was my turn to serve. Service is my strong point at tennis. I am inaccurate, but vigorous, and occasionally send in a quite unplayable shot. One or two of these, even at the expense of a fault or so, and I might be permitted to retain at least a portion of my self-respect.

I opened with a couple of faults. The sight of Phyllis, sitting calm and cool in her chair under the cedar, unnerved me. I served another fault. And yet another.

“Here, I say, Garnet,” observed Mr. Chase plaintively, “do put me out of this hideous suspense. I’m becoming a mere bundle of quivering ganglions.”

===Chinese===
「可她这会儿，正在约维尔（Yeovil）的荒漠空气里糟蹋她的甜美呢。你最好坐下来观战，菲莉斯。这种天气打网球，不是娇生惯养的女性干的活。边打我边给你讲解我球艺的精微之处。特别留意『蒂尔登式反手猛抽』。回回得分。」

我们走向网球场。我顶着日光打球。我原可以，若我愿意，大书特书这一节，把我日后的溃败全归罪于它，还可以添上几条以坐实这托词：球场陌生，球拍是借的，心又不在焉——第一在*霍克案*，第二，也是最主要的，在想那桩阴郁的心事：菲莉斯与我这位对手，彼此似乎交情匪浅。方才喝茶时，他们那模样几乎像一对订了婚的。两人之间有一种充分的谅解。然而我不打算躲进托词背后。我毫无保留地承认：蔡斯先生比我高明得多。我一向以为皇家海军的上尉们不擅网球。我在各家宅邸都遇见过他们，从未见谁出过风头。他们打的是一种兢兢业业、不求闻达的球，而且一般等球打完就露出如释重负的神情。蔡斯先生不是这路人。他的发球是装了瓶的闪电。他的回球像跳跳爆竹。头一局他不多不少六拍便拿下。他发球。我只有一回用拍面正正接住他的发球，那一接，我觉得自己像在挡子弹。我把它回了网里。那一串的最后一球撞上我球拍的木边，蹿过后网进了灌木丛，活似快投手的一记擦边斜飞。

「这一局，」蔡斯先生说，「回头咱们再找它算账。」

我自觉是条虫，算不得人。菲莉斯，我想，多半要从这场表演给我的一生下总评了。她会盘算：一个人打网球尚且这样软弱、这样可怜地一败涂地，在人生的任何部门恐怕都成不了什么气候。她会不由自主地拿我同对手比较，把他的锐气与光辉对照我的不顶事。不知怎么，这场屠杀开始对我的品性起了坏影响。我的自尊正节节流走。再来一会儿，我就要被碾碎了——一摊人形的冻子。轮到我发球。发球是我网球的强项。我发得不准，但有力，偶尔还能送出一记没法接的球。来上那么一两个——哪怕贴上一两次失误——我或许还能保住至少一部分自尊。

我开头连发两个失误。一望见菲莉斯安安静静、凉凉爽爽地坐在雪松下的椅子里，我就慌了神。又发一误。再一误。

「喂，我说加尼特，」蔡斯先生哀声说，「快把我从这可怕的悬念里解救出来吧。我快成一堆哆嗦的神经节了。」

===Original===
I loathe facetiousness in moments of stress.

I frowned austerely, made no reply, and served another fault, my fifth.

Matters had reached a crisis. Even if I had to lob it underhand, I must send the ball over the net with the next stroke.

I restrained myself this time, eschewing the careless vigour which had marked my previous efforts. The ball flew in a slow semicircle, and pitched inside the correct court. At least, I told myself, I had not served a fault.

What happened then I cannot exactly say. I saw my opponent spring forward like a panther and whirl his racquet. The next moment the back net was shaking violently, and the ball was rolling swiftly along the ground on a return journey to the other court.

“Love-forty,” said Mr. Chase. “Phyllis!”

===Chinese===
我最恨人家在紧急关头耍贫嘴。

我冷峻地皱起眉，不作声，又发一误，第五个。

事态已到存亡关头。哪怕下一拍得用下手抛球，我也必须把球送过网去。

这一回我克制住了，弃绝了先前那种不管不顾的蛮力。球划出一道缓慢的弧线，落在界内。至少，我对自己说，没发失误。

随后的事我说不真切。只见我的对手黑豹般一扑，球拍一抡。下一刻后网剧震，球正贴着地皮，疾速滚回那边的球场。

「零比四十。」蔡斯先生说。「菲莉斯！」

===Original===
“Yes?”

“That was the Tilden Slosh.”

“I thought it must be,” said Phyllis.

In the third game I managed to score fifteen. By the merest chance I returned one of his red-hot serves, and﻿—probably through surprise﻿—he failed to send it back again.

In the fourth and fifth games I omitted to score. Phyllis had left the cedar now, and was picking flowers from the beds behind the court.

We began the sixth game. And now for some reason I played really well. I struck a little vein of brilliance. I was serving, and this time a proportion of my serves went over the net instead of trying to get through. The score went from fifteen all to forty-fifteen. Hope began to surge through my veins. If I could keep this up, I might win yet.

===Chinese===
「嗯？」

「这就是蒂尔登式猛抽。」

「我料着必是它。」菲莉斯说。

第三局我好歹得了十五分。纯属偶然，我接回了他一个滚烫的发球，而——大概是吃了一惊——他没能再打回来。

第四局和第五局，我忘了得分这回事。菲莉斯已离开雪松，在球场后的花坛里采花。

第六局开打。也不知为什么，这时我打得当真好起来了。我摸着了一小条才气矿脉。我发着球，这一回，我的发球有相当一部分越过了网，而不是想从网里钻过去。比分从十五平涨到四十比十五。希望开始在我血管里涌动。这势头若保持得住，说不定还能赢。

===Original===
The Tilden Slosh diminished my lead by fifteen. The Johnson Slam brought the score to Deuce. Then I got in a really fine serve, which beat him. ’Vantage In. Another Slosh. Deuce. Another Slam. ’Vantage out. It was an awesome moment. There is a tide in the affairs of men, which, taken by the flood﻿—. I served. Fault. I served again﻿—a beauty. He returned it like a flash into the corner of the court. With a supreme effort I got to it. We rallied. I was playing like a professor. Then whizz﻿—!

The Slosh had beaten me on the post.

“Game *and*﻿—” said Mr. Chase, tossing his racquet into the air and catching it by the handle. “Good game that last one.”

I turned to see what Phyllis thought of it.

At the eleventh hour I had shown her of what stuff I was made.

She had disappeared.

===Chinese===
蒂尔登式猛抽把我的领先砍掉十五。约翰逊式扣杀把比分拉成平分。跟着我一记真正漂亮的发球，赢了他。我占先。又一记猛抽。平分。又一记扣杀。他占先。这是令人肃然的时刻。人事自有潮汐，趁涨潮引领，便得富贵——。我发球。失误。再发——一记妙球。他闪电般把球回到场角。我拼尽全力够着了。我们连续对抽。我打得像个教授。随即嗖的一声——！

那记猛抽在终点柱上把我击败了。

「这一局*兼*——」蔡斯先生说，把球拍抛上天，又抓住拍柄。「最后这局打得好。」

我转身去看菲莉斯作何感想。

在最后关头，我总算让她见识了我的料子。

她已经不见了。

===Original===
“Looking for Miss Derrick?” said Chase, jumping the net, and joining me in my court, “she’s gone into the house.”

“When did she go?”

“At the end of the fifth game,” said Chase.

“Gone to dress for dinner, I suppose,” he continued. “It must be getting late. I think I ought to be going, too, if you don’t mind. The professor gets a little restive if I keep him waiting for his daily bread. Great Scott, that watch can’t be right! What do you make it? Yes, so do I. I really think I must run. You won’t mind. Good night, then. See you tomorrow, I hope.”

I walked slowly out across the fields. That same star, in which I had confided on a former occasion, was at its post. It looked placid and cheerful. *It* never got beaten by six games to love under the very eyes of a lady-star. *It* was never cut out ignominiously by infernally capable lieutenants in His Majesty’s Navy. No wonder it was cheerful.

===Chinese===
「找德里克小姐吗？」蔡斯说着跳过球网，到我这半场来，「她进屋去了。」

「她什么时候走的？」

「第五局完的时候。」蔡斯说。

「多半回屋换晚饭装束去了，」他接着说，「想必不早了。我想我也该走了，你不介意吧。教授要是等他的日粮等久了，可要有点儿躁了。老天，这表不可能对吧！你看几点了？是，我也这么看。我真得跑了。你别见怪。那么，晚安。希望明天见。」

我缓缓地穿过田野往回走。那颗星——上回我曾在它跟前掏过心——仍在岗位上。它显得安详而愉快。*它*从没在哪位女士星的眼皮底下，被人以六比零横扫。*它*也从没被陛下海军里精明强干得可恶的上尉们窝囊地挤出局。难怪它愉快。

## XV The Arrival of Nemesis / 十五 复仇女神驾到

===Original===

Some people do not believe in presentiments. They attribute that curious feeling that something unpleasant is going to happen to such mundane causes as liver, or a chill, or the weather. For my own part, I think there is more in the matter than the casual observer might imagine.

I awoke three days after my meeting with the professor at the clubhouse, filled with a dull foreboding. Somehow I seemed to know that that day was going to turn out badly for me.
